### [1] [A Promising Ohmic Contacts Approach for High-Al AlxGa1-xN (x>0.6) Channel HEMTs with AlN/GaN Digital Alloy Channel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24221)
*Tariq Jamil,Abdullah Al Mamun Mazumder,S M Tazbiul Hasan,Mafruda Rahman,Muhammad Ali,Ankit Malik,Kamal Hussain,Chandan Joishi,Mansura Sadek,James G. Fiorenza,Grigory Simin,Asif Khan*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a novel ohmic contact formation scheme for high-Al-content AlGaN channel HEMTs, using low-temperature PMOCVD n++-GaN regrown contacts and AlGaN digital alloy channel layers, achieving record-low contact resistance of ~6.5 Ω-mm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the challenge of forming low-resistance ohmic contacts to extreme bandgap (x>0.6) AlGaN channel HEMTs with undoped barrier layers, which is critical for high-power and high-frequency electronics.

Method: Utilizing low-temperature PMOCVD for n++-GaN regrown contacts to achieve higher doping efficiency and lower sheet resistivity, combined with an AlGaN digital alloy (SPSL of AlN/GaN) channel layer that eliminates hetero-barriers with the regrown GaN contacts.

Result: PMOCVD doping yields 3-5x lower sheet resistivity than conventional doped n++-GaN. Regrown ohmic contacts achieve record-low Rc ~6.5 Ω-mm on Al0.62Ga0.38N DA channel HEMTs with AlN barrier.

Conclusion: The proposed LT-PMOCVD regrown n++-GaN contact scheme combined with AlGaN DA channels enables record-low ohmic contact resistance for high-Al-content AlGaN HEMTs, advancing extreme bandgap semiconductor device technology.

Abstract: In this paper we report a novel ohmic contact formation scheme for Extreme Bandgap (EBG) AlxGa1-xN (x>0.6) channel HEMTs with undoped barrier layers. Our approach consists of using a new low temperature (LT) pulsed metal-organic chemical vapor deposition (PMOCVD) doping scheme for the n++-GaN regrown contacts and an AlxGa1-xN digital alloy (DA) channel layer comprising short period super lattices (SPSL) of AlN and GaN. Pulsed growth and doping yield a sheet resistivity which is a factor of 3-5 lower than that of conventional doped n++-GaN layers grown under identical conditions. Moreover, the regrown n++-GaN layer has no hetero-barrier with the GaN layers of the AlxGa1-xN DA channel. These innovations led to MOCVD regrown linear ohmic contacts and a record-low contact resistance Rc ~6.5 Ω-mm to the Al0.62Ga0.38N DA channel layer of a HEMT with AlN barrier layer.

</details>


### [2] [P-dit Probabilistic Ising Machine for Solving the Quadratic Assignment Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24408)
*Christian Duffee,Chadbourne M. Burling-Smith,Jordan Athas,Andrea Grimaldi,Giovanni Finocchio,Ermin Wei,Pedram Khalili Amiri*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: This paper presents a probabilistic Ising machine (PIM) using generalized multi-state probabilistic variables (p-dits) to solve the quadratic assignment problem (QAP). The PIM finds the best-known solution on 95% of QAP Library instances (vs. 36% for Gurobi), achieves 2-3 orders of magnitude speedup on the largest problem, and outperforms state-of-the-art QAP solvers on both CPU and GPU implementations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial optimization problems like the quadratic assignment problem (QAP) involve complex interactions that make finding optimal solutions difficult, yet they describe many real-world scenarios such as supply chain logistics. Existing solvers struggle to find high-quality solutions efficiently.

Method: A probabilistic Ising machine (PIM) is implemented using probabilistic d-dimensional variables (p-dits), which are generalized multi-state, multi-dimensional extensions of probabilistic bits (p-bits). Each p-dit corresponds to a location and stochastically oscillates between facility assignments based on interactions with other p-dits. The approach is also parallelized via GPU implementations.

Result: With identical runtime and CPU, the PIM finds the best-known solution on 95% of QAP Library instances versus 36% for Gurobi. On the largest problem in the library, a 2-3 order-of-magnitude reduction in time-to-solution is achieved. Both CPU and GPU implementations of the PIM consistently outperform state-of-the-art QAP solvers.

Conclusion: The proposed probabilistic Ising machine with p-dit variables offers a highly effective and efficient approach for solving QAP instances, significantly outperforming conventional solvers and specialized state-of-the-art algorithms, with additional gains from parallel GPU implementation.

Abstract: Combinatorial optimization problems represent a wide range of real-world scenarios where complicated interactions make it difficult to find the best solution. One example is the quadratic assignment problem (QAP), which involves determining the optimal placement of facilities at set locations which minimizes the products of material flow and facility distance. This representation is descriptive of many real-world scenarios, including the aggregate transportation costs of a supply chain. In this work, a probabilistic Ising machine (PIM) approach is implemented using probabilistic d-dimensional variables (p-dits), which are generalized, multi-state and multi-dimensional extensions to probabilistic bits (p-bits). Each p-dit corresponds to a location and stochastically oscillates between facility assignments based on the influence of the other p-dits. We show that with the same runtime and CPU, the PIM finds the best-known solution on 95% of considered instances from the QAP Library dataset, compared to just 36% for the standard Gurobi solver. For the unique largest problem in the library, a 2 to 3 order-of-magnitude decrease is observed in the time needed to reach specific solution qualities. We also show parallelization of our PIM through GPU implementations. A comparison to state-of-the-art QAP solver algorithms shows that they are consistently outperformed by both CPU and GPU implementations of the p-dit Ising machine.

</details>


### [3] [Universal square-root-log scaling for slender-body end effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24653)
*Gunnar Peng,Ory Schnitzer*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: Charge on slender conducting cylinders becomes uniformly distributed at large aspect ratios, with end enhancements scaling as the square-root of the logarithm of the aspect ratio, validated by simulations and applicable to diffusion, plasmonics, and Stokes flow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the slow approach to uniform charge distribution on slender conductors and quantify the persistent nonuniformity near the ends, characterized by a specific scaling law.

Method: Local resummation of a perturbation-series solution to the integral equation of slender-body theory, validated by boundary-element simulations for cylinders with flat and hemispherical caps.

Result: Charge density and field magnitude near the ends are enhanced by a factor scaling as √(log(aspect ratio)). This scaling applies broadly to truncated slender bodies and other physical setups.

Conclusion: A universal scaling law governs end effects in slender conducting bodies, with demonstrated applicability to diffusion, plasmonics, and Stokes flow.

Abstract: The charge on a conducting cylinder becomes uniformly distributed at large aspect ratios, albeit very slowly and with nonuniformity persisting near the ends. We show that the charge density and field magnitude are enhanced near the ends by a factor scaling as the square-root of the logarithm of the aspect ratio. This scaling is obtained by locally resumming a perturbation-series solution to the integral equation of slender-body theory. The same scaling applies to a broad class of "truncated" slender bodies -- including cylinders and shapes tapered near the ends on the cross-sectional scale -- as well as other physical setups. We validate this scaling through boundary-element simulations for cylinders with flat and hemispherical caps, and demonstrate its applicability to diffusion, plasmonics and Stokes flow.

</details>

### [4] [AB2X4 spinel structures: similarity and difference between the centrosymmetric, Fd-3m, and non-centrosymmetric, F4132, space groups](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24198)
*Alla Arakcheeva,Arnaud Magrez,Gervais Chapuis*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Spinel compounds are often incorrectly assigned to centrosymmetric space groups when they may actually be non-centrosymmetric. This work shows that centrosymmetric Fd-3m and non-centrosymmetric F4132 space groups are indistinguishable by X-ray diffraction without atomic displacements, requiring anharmonic modeling to differentiate them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the discrepancy between the physical properties of some spinel compounds (which suggest non-centrosymmetry) and their traditional structural assignment to the centrosymmetric space group Fd-3m.

Method: The authors demonstrate that the centrosymmetric Fd-3m and non-centrosymmetric F4132 space groups are equivalent for structure determination and refinement based on X-ray diffraction data, and that distinguishing them requires an anharmonic model of atomic displacements.

Result: Loss of inversion symmetry in spinels can occur without atomic displacements because Fd-3m and F4132 are indistinguishable by standard X-ray diffraction; only anharmonic modeling can differentiate them when consistent with experiment.

Conclusion: Certain misconceptions about structural symmetry and physical properties of spinel-type compounds can be clarified by recognizing that centrosymmetric and non-centrosymmetric space groups may be equivalent under X-ray diffraction, necessitating anharmonic models for proper assignment.

Abstract: Many compounds belonging to the spinel AB2X4 structure play an important role due to their wide range of practical applications. Most of them are traditionally assigned to the centrosymmetric space group Fd-3m. However, the physical properties of some spinels are incompatible with centrosymmetry. This discrepancy is often accounted for by reducing the symmetry to the non-centrosymmetric space group F-43m, allowing thus small atomic displacements from their original position in Fd-3m. In this work, we demonstrate that the loss of the inversion symmetry can occur without any atomic displacements, since the centrosymmetric Fd-3m and non-centrosymmetric F4132 space groups are equivalent for structure determination and refinement based on X-ray diffraction data. If consistent with experiment, only the use of an anharmonic model of atomic displacements can distinguish these space groups. This study aims to clarify certain misconceptions regarding the structural symmetry and physical properties of spinel type compounds.

</details>


### [5] [ChatMOSP: A Chemistry-Grounded Mobile Agent for Working-State Catalyst Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24265)
*Sanyang Ye,Rui Qi,Beien Zhu,Yi Gao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: ChatMOSP is a chemistry-grounded mobile scientific agent that translates natural-language catalytic requests into validated multiscale simulations of nanoparticle restructuring, demonstrated on Pd and Pt CO oxidation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Converting experimentally specified reaction environments into physically meaningful morphology-performance simulations is difficult because it requires specialized knowledge in computational catalysis to translate conditions into model-specific parameters.

Method: ChatMOSP maps catalyst identity, temperature, pressure, gas composition, and target observables onto multiscale structure reconstruction and kMC tasks, retrieves database parameters or constructs missing inputs via an online literature-retrieval workflow, and executes validated MOSP workflows using the Multi-scale Operando Simulation Package.

Result: Verified on Pd CO oxidation: simulations capture temperature-induced faceted-to-rounded morphology transitions observed by in-situ TEM, using either built-in databases or web-retrieved literature data. Demonstrated end-to-end mobile simulation of a pressure-coverage-morphology-activity feedback cycle for Pt CO oxidation, interpreting oscillatory CO conversion.

Conclusion: ChatMOSP establishes a physically constrained mobile agent for accessible and interpretable catalyst working-state simulations.

Abstract: Catalytic nanoparticles restructure dynamically under reaction conditions, so their working morphology and activity are governed by temperature, pressure, and gas composition. However, converting experimentally specified environments into physically meaningful morphology-performance simulations remains difficult because the translation of reaction conditions into model-specific energetic, kinetic, and execution parameters requires the specialized knowledge in computational catalysis. Here we report ChatMOSP, a chemistry-grounded mobile scientific agent that translates natural-language and voice-expressed catalytic requests into parameter-validated simulations using the Multi-scale Operando Simulation Package. ChatMOSP maps catalyst identity, temperature, pressure, gas composition, and target observables onto multiscale structure reconstruction and kinetic Monte Carlo tasks, retrieves database parameters or constructs missing inputs from an online literature-retrieval workflow, and executes validated MOSP workflows. Using CO oxidation on Pd nanoparticles as an example, we verify the ChatMOSP simulations capture the temperature-induced transition from faceted to rounded morphologies observed by in-situ TEM experiments either by built-in database or from web-retrieved literature information when the parameters are absent. Moreover, we demonstrate the capability of ChatMOSP to perform end-to-end study at mobile devices to simulate a pressure-coverage-morphology-activity feedback cycle for Pt CO oxidation to interpret the oscillatory CO conversion. These results establish ChatMOSP as a physically constrained mobile agent for accessible and interpretable catalyst working-state simulations.

</details>


### [6] [The structure of a melt: The case of liquid bismuth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24234)
*Flor B. Quiroga,Isaías Rodríguez,David Hinojosa,Alexander Valladares,Renela M. Valladares,Ariel A. Valladares*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: MD simulations on 216-atom bismuth supercells from 300K to 573K (liquid state) were performed. PDFs revealed peaks at 3.25Å, 6.55Å, and a shoulder at 4.6Å linked to crystalline neighbor peaks and diagonal distances in deformed squares. PADs showed prominent angles at 53°/85° (MS) and 58°/90° (RMC), indicating deformed triangles and squares in liquid bismuth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To perform a detailed structural characterization of liquid bismuth at 573 K using MD simulations combined with RMC modeling, focusing on the atomic-scale geometric motifs (polyhedral arrangements) that emerge beyond simple pair correlations.

Method: MD simulations on 216-atom bismuth supercells using Materials Studio, heating from 300K to 573K over 100 steps, then maintaining at 573K for 500 steps. PDFs and PADs were computed from the last 1, 10, 25, and 100 MD steps. RMC modeling was applied to the averaged last-100-step structure (four independent runs with different initial random velocities) to generate atomic configurations. PDFs, PADs, and J(r) were calculated and analyzed.

Result: PDFs show two main peaks at 3.25Å and 6.55Å, plus a shoulder at 4.6Å. The shoulder is attributed to third/fourth neighbor peaks in the crystalline Wyckoff structure and diagonal distances in deformed squares. MS-based PADs show peaks at 53° and 85°; RMC-based PADs show peaks at 58° and 90°, indicating deformed triangles and squares. Higher-order polyhedral geometries are less abundant.

Conclusion: Liquid bismuth at 573K exhibits a local structure dominated by deformed triangular and square motifs, with the PDF shoulder at 4.6Å serving as a key signature of the persistence of crystalline-like correlations (third/fourth neighbors) and square diagonal distances in the liquid state.

Abstract: Molecular Dynamics (MD) is performed on supercells of 216 atoms of bismuth, going from 300 K to 573 K in 100 steps and maintaining it in the liquid state, at 573 K, during 500 steps using the Materials Studio (MS) suite of codes. The Pair Distribution Functions (PDFs) and the Plane Angle Distributions (PADs) of the last 1, 10, 25 and 100 steps of the MD have been obtained. Averaging the last 100 steps, as representative of the liquid, Reverse Monte Carlo (RMC) was applied to obtain 4 atomic structures, one for each set of initial random velocities. Then, a detailed structural study of liquid bismuth at 573 K was undertaken; PDFs and PADs are calculated and reported. Two noticeable peaks appear for the PDFs, at 3.25 and 6.55 Å, along with a pseudo peak (shoulder) at 4.6 Å. This shoulder (after the first peak) of the PDFs is found to be related to the third and fourth neighbor peaks of the crystalline Wyckoff structure and also to the diagonal distances in deformed squares in the liquid structure. For completeness J(r)s are also reported. Two prominent peaks in the MS PADs are observed: 53° and 85°; and two for the RMC PADs: 58° and 90°, suggesting the existence of deformed triangles and squares. Less abundant are higher-order geometrical structures.

</details>


### [7] [Ultrafast Magneto-optical Fingerprints of Altermagnetism in MnTe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24320)
*Xu Yang,Xingkai Cheng,Zhuo Deng,Yu-Han Gao,Qing-Lin Yang,Zheng Chang,Peng-Tao Yang,Hong-Mei Feng,Xiang-Qun Zhang,Wei He,Junwei Liu,Zhao-Hua Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reports the coexistence of magneto-optical Kerr and Voigt effects in the altermagnet MnTe using time-resolved pump-probe measurements, directly revealing the dual-space nature of altermagnets—spin-split bands in momentum space (Kerr effect from Berry curvature) and compensated magnetization in real space (Voigt effect from Néel order).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets possess a unique dual-space nature (spin-split bands like ferromagnets in momentum space but compensated magnetization like antiferromagnets in real space), yet a robust experimental signature to directly reveal this duality is critically needed.

Method: Time-resolved pump-probe magneto-optical measurements combined with magnetization measurements and first-principles calculations on the altermagnet MnTe.

Result: Coexistence of pronounced Kerr and Voigt effects in MnTe; Kerr effect originates from intrinsic Berry curvature of altermagnetism distribution in momentum space, while Voigt effect arises from anisotropic permittivity due to in-plane Néel order in real space; transient Kerr signal shows faster relaxation dynamics than transient Voigt signal.

Conclusion: Transient magneto-optical responses serve as distinctive fingerprints of altermagnetism, positioning altermagnets as promising platforms for ultrafast spin optoelectronics.

Abstract: Recently identified altermagnets exhibit a distinctive dual-space nature: they possess spin-split electronic bands akin to ferromagnets in momentum space while maintaining the fully compensated magnetization of antiferromagnets in real space. This inherent duality, originating from the same crystal symmetry, gives rise to various intriguing physical phenomena unique to altermagnets. Consequently, a robust and efficient experimental signature capable of revealing this dual character is critically needed. The magneto-optical Kerr and Voigt effects, given their high sensitivity to ferromagnetism and antiferromagnetism, respectively, are ideally suited to probe this duality. Here, using time-resolved pump-probe magneto-optical measurements, we report the coexistence of pronounced Kerr and Voigt effects in the altermagnet MnTe. Combining the magnetization measurement and first-principles calculations, we demonstrate that the Kerr effect originates from the intrinsic Berry curvature of altermagnetism distribution in momentum space, while the Voigt effect arises from an anisotropic permittivity induced by the in-plane Néel order in real space, directly revealing the dual-space nature of altermagnets. Furthermore, the transient Kerr signal exhibits faster relaxation dynamics than the transient Voigt signal, underscoring their distinct origins in Berry curvature and Néel order, respectively. These findings establish transient magneto-optical responses as distinctive fingerprints of altermagnetism and position altermagnets as promising platforms for manipulating magneto-optical phenomena in ultrafast spin optoelectronics.

</details>


### [8] [Systematic comparison of approximations and functionals in first-principle calculations of aluminum-based III-V ferroelectric nitrides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24334)
*Alejandro Mercado Tejerina,Peng Chen,Keisuke Yazawa,Andriy Zakutayev,Laurent Bellaiche,Charles Paillard*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study revisits first-principles predictions of Al₁₋ₓScₓN and Al₁₋ₓBₓN alloys, showing that exchange-correlation functionals (PBE, PBESol, SCAN, SCAN+rVV10) and disorder modeling (VCA vs. SQS) significantly affect predicted phase stability, and concluding that SQS+SCAN offers the most consistent theoretical framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically assess the role of chemical disorder and exchange-correlation approximations in predicting structural, ferroelectric, and electronic properties of Al-based III-V nitride alloys (Al₁₋ₓScₓN and Al₁₋ₓBₓN), given that prior predictions show large quantitative and qualitative discrepancies.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) within a unified 48-atom supercell framework, comparing virtual crystal approximation (VCA) vs. special quasirandom structures (SQS), and PBE, PBESol, SCAN, and SCAN+rVV10 functionals.

Result: PBESol (popular functional) strongly underestimates the ferroelectric wurtzite stability domain in Al₁₋ₓScₓN compared to SQS PBE or SCAN. Al₁₋ₓBₓN shows faster wurtzite destabilization with bond breaking, enhanced polarization, and transitions to zincblende and a threefold coordinated hexagonal layer phase. A 5-fold coordinated hexagonal phase identified as a low-energy metastable state in Al₁₋ₓScₓN.

Conclusion: Local disorder modeling and exchange-correlation functional choice critically affect predictions; SQS combined with SCAN is the most consistent theoretical framework for nitride-based ferroelectrics.

Abstract: We revisit first-principles predictions of structural, ferroelectric, and electronic properties in aluminum-based III-V nitride alloys, focusing on Al1-xScxN and Al1-xBxN. Using density functional theory within a unified 48-atom supercell framework, we systematically assess the role of chemical disorder and exchange-correlation approximations by comparing the virtual crystal approximation (VCA) and special quasirandom structures (SQS), as well as PBE, PBESol, SCAN, and SCAN+rVV10 functionals. We demonstrate that, even amongst the similar PBE and PBESol functionals, big quantitative and qualitative differences emerge. In particular, the VCA or SQS PBESol (a popular functional) strongly underestimate the stability domain of the ferroelectric wurtzite phase in Al1-xScxN compared to SQS PBE or SQS SCAN. We demonstrate that the 5-fold coordinated hexagonal phase predicted in 2002 by Farrer and Bellaiche [Phys. Rev. B 66, 201203] is a low-energy metastable state between the four-fold coordinated ferroelectric wurtzite phase and the six-fold coordinated rocksalt phase near the transition point upon increasing the Sc content. In contrast, Al1-xBxN shows a much faster destabilization of the wurtzite ferroelectric phase, with bond breaking which strongly distorts the wurtzite structure (with enhanced polarization) and eventually favor a zincblende phase and a threefold coordinated hexagonal layer phase. Our analysis highlights the critical importance of both local disorder and exchange-correlation treatment in predicting the functional properties of III-V nitride ferroelectrics. Overall, SQS combined with SCAN provides the most consistent theoretical framework for understanding and optimizing emerging nitride-based ferroelectric materials.

</details>


### [9] [Dilute Magnetism and Edge-State Engineering in Monolayer SnO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24376)
*Yuya Fukuta,Souren Adhikary,Kazuhito Tsukagoshi,Katsunori Wakabayashi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: DFT study shows TM-doped SnO monolayer exhibits localized magnetic moments from impurity d-orbitals, and nanoribbon edges (especially oxygen-rich chiral edges) are semiconducting while Sn-terminated edges host metallic 1D channels, enabling spintronic and nanoelectronic applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how transition-metal doping and edge engineering can tailor the electronic and magnetic properties of SnO monolayer for spintronic and nanoelectronic applications.

Method: DFT calculations on large supercell TM-doped (Mn, Fe, Co, W) SnO monolayer, plus nanoribbon geometry analysis of edge states and thermodynamic stability.

Result: All dopants induce localized magnetic moments from d-orbitals; Co-doped SnO shows nearly dispersionless bands near Fermi energy. Chiral nanoribbons with oxygen-rich edges are thermodynamically stable and semiconducting, while Sn-terminated edges host metallic 1D channels.

Conclusion: TM doping and edge engineering are effective routes to tailor SnO monolayer properties, promising for spintronics and nanoelectronics.

Abstract: Tin monoxide (SnO) is a p-type oxide semiconductor whose electronic properties can be widely modified via atomic-scale engineering. Using density functional theory, we investigate the electronic and magnetic properties of transition-metal (TM = Mn, Fe, Co and W) doped SnO monolayer within a large supercell. We find that all dopants induce finite localized magnetic moments, primarily originating from $d$-orbitals of the impurity atoms. We show that these localized magnetic states give rise to nearly
  dispersionless bands in the vicinity of the Fermi energy (taking Co doped SnO as an example). In addition, we investigate dimensional effects by constructing nanoribbon geometries of SnO monolayer. The ribbons exhibit intrinsic edge-localized states that are largely independent of ribbon width. For chiral nanoribbons oriented along a low-symmetry direction of the square lattice, we find that oxygen-rich edges are thermodynamically most stable and remain semiconducting, whereas Sn-terminated edges host metallic one-dimensional conduction channels. Our results demonstrate that transition-metal doping and edge engineering provide effective routes to tailor the electronic properties of SnO monolayer, making it a promising candidate for future spintronic and nanoelectronic applications.

</details>


### [10] [Light-Driven Ferroic Switching Enables Reversible Control of Hydrogen Adsorption Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24378)
*Xueqing Wan,Zhenlong Zhang,Charles Paillard,Jian Zhou,Jinyang Ni,Chuanlu Yang,Zhijun Jiang,Laurent Bellaiche*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that photoinduced ferroic-order switching in 2D ionic ferroelectric monolayers (TiGeSe₃) enables ultrafast, reversible control of hydrogen binding energy, with adsorption free energy tunable from 0.33 to 1.11 eV, validated across multiple material systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reversible ultrafast switching of surface thermodynamics is highly desirable for hydrogen storage and catalysis yet remains elusive at the nanoscale.

Method: Photoinduced ferroic-order switching in 2D ionic ferroelectric monolayers (TiGeSe₃) via carrier-density-driven redistribution of transition-metal 3d orbital occupations, enabling sequential ferroelectric-to-paraelectric phase transitions with staggered or Zig-Zag antiferromagnetic order.

Result: Continuous tuning of hydrogen adsorption free energy from 0.33 to 1.11 eV, shifting the interface from near-thermoneutrality to spontaneous desorption; nonadiabatic dynamics show electron-phonon coupling promotes nonthermal H release, with picosecond carrier recombination restoring initial ferroic order.

Conclusion: Ferroic order serves as an optically addressable knob for dynamic thermodynamic reconfiguration beyond static design, validated in AgBiP₂Se₆ and CuInP₂S₆.

Abstract: Reversible ultrafast switching of surface thermodynamics is highly desirable for hydrogen storage and catalysis yet remains elusive at the nanoscale. Here we demonstrate that photoinduced ferroic-order switching in two-dimensional ionic ferroelectric monolayers enables rapid, reversible control of hydrogen binding. In TiGeSe$_3$, carrier-density-driven redistribution of transition-metal 3\textit{d} orbital occupations triggers a sequential evolution from the ferroelectric ground state to paraelectric phases with staggered or Zig-Zag antiferromagnetic order. This switch continuously tunes the hydrogen adsorption free energy from 0.33 to 1.11 eV, shifting the interface from near-thermoneutrality to spontaneous desorption. Nonadiabatic dynamics indicate that electron-phonon coupling promotes nonthermal H release, while picosecond carrier recombination rapidly restores the initial ferroic order, closing an ultrafast reversible cycle. Generality is further validated in AgBiP$_2$Se$_6$ and CuInP$_2$S$_6$, establishing ferroic order as an optically addressable knob for dynamic thermodynamic reconfiguration beyond static design.

</details>


### [11] [Alignment-free ultra-broadband parametric frequency conversion in lead-halide perovskites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25718)
*Abhishek Shiva Kumar,Dusan Lorenc,Ayan A. Zhumekenov,Osman M. Bakr,Zhanybek Alpichshev*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Lead-halide perovskites (LHPs) exhibit ultra-broadband four-wave mixing of near- and mid-infrared femtosecond pulses, generating bright, coherent, collimated emission across a wide continuous tuning range without phase-matching engineering, enabled by large intrinsic χ(3) response near crystal surfaces, positioning LHPs as a platform for compact nonlinear photonics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lead-halide perovskites exhibit some of the largest known optical nonlinearities, yet their potential for frequency conversion remains largely unexploited.

Method: Ultra-broadband four-wave mixing of near- and mid-infrared femtosecond pulses in thick single-crystal LHPs, without phase-matching engineering, angular alignment, or dispersion optimization.

Result: Bright, coherent, highly collimated emission across an exceptionally wide continuous tuning range. Emission originates near crystal surfaces where phase-matching constraints are relaxed, enabled by large intrinsic χ(3) response.

Conclusion: LHPs are a powerful bulk platform for ultra-broadband nonlinear photonics, enabling compact, alignment-free architectures for ultrafast frequency conversion.

Abstract: Lead-halide perovskites were demonstrated to exhibit some of the largest known optical nonlinearities, yet their potential for frequency conversion remains largely untapped. Here we demonstrate ultra-broadband four-wave mixing of near- and mid-infrared femtosecond pulses in thick single-crystal LHPs, generating bright, coherent, and highly collimated emission across an exceptionally wide continuous tuning range without phase-matching engineering, angular alignment, or dispersion optimization. Time resolved measurements reveal that the emission originates near the crystal surfaces, where phase-matching constraints are relaxed, while the unusually large intrinsic $χ^{(3)}$ response preserves efficient and directional frequency conversion despite the strongly localized interaction volume. These results position LHPs as a powerful bulk platform for ultra-broadband nonlinear photonics, opening a pathway toward compact, alignment-free architectures for ultrafast frequency conversion.

</details>


### [12] [Colossal Type-II Multiferroic Polarization Driven by Collinear Spin Orders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24382)
*Chengxi Huang,Xinhai Tu,Jintao Jiang,Xiangang Wan,Erjun Kan*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper proposes a spin-orbit-coupling (SOC) and spin-lattice-coupling (SLC) independent route to achieve strong magnetoelectric coupling (MEC) and large ferroelectric polarization in type-II multiferroics using collinear 3d magnetic systems. Using spin-group symmetry and first-principles calculations on monolayer 2H-VS2, they demonstrate that collinear stripy antiferromagnetic order induces an in-plane ferroelectric polarization up to 25.00 μC/cm², about two orders of magnitude larger than typical type-II multiferroics, with the mechanism attributed to SOC-independent p-d hybridization via electronic hopping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional type-II multiferroics suffer from weak SOC in 3d transition-metal ions and limited SLC-induced lattice distortions, resulting in small polarizations and low magnetic transition temperatures. The central challenge is achieving strong MEC together with large ferroelectric polarization.

Method: The authors use spin-group symmetry analysis to propose an SOC- and SLC-independent route to MEC in collinear 3d magnetic systems, and perform first-principles calculations (excluding SOC) on monolayer 2H-VS2 to validate their predictions. A microscopic model based on p-d hybridization governed by electronic hopping is also developed.

Result: Collinear stripy antiferromagnetic order in monolayer 2H-VS2 induces an in-plane ferroelectric polarization up to 25.00 μC/cm², which is about two orders of magnitude larger than that of typical type-II multiferroics. The mechanism is independent of SOC and relies on p-d hybridization via electronic hopping.

Conclusion: The work reveals a possible route toward type-II multiferroics that combine strong MEC with large electronic polarization in collinear 3d magnetic systems, independent of SOC and SLC mechanisms.

Abstract: Achieving strong magnetoelectric coupling (MEC) together with large ferroelectric polarization remains a central challenge in type-II multiferroics. In conventional spin-driven multiferroics, the induced polarization is usually mediated by spin-orbit coupling (SOC) or spin-lattice coupling (SLC). Since many representative systems are based on 3d transition-metal ions, where SOC is relatively weak and SLC-induced lattice distortions are often limited, their polarizations are typically much smaller than those of proper ferroelectrics. Moreover, electric polarizations in type-II multiferroics are generally induced by spiral spin orders stabilized by competing magnetic interactions, which often leads to relatively low magnetic transition temperatures. In this Letter, using spin-group symmetry, we propose an SOC- and SLC-independent route to MEC in collinear 3d magnetic systems. We show that, even for a noncentrosymmetric lattice structure, different collinear magnetic configurations can either forbid or allow electric polarization, indicating direct magnetic control of polarization and hence strong MEC. The first-principles calculations excluding SOC on monolayer 2H-VS2 support this picture: a collinear stripy antiferromagnetic order induces an in-plane ferroelectric polarization up to 25.00 μC/cm2, about two orders of magnitude larger than that of typical type-II multiferroics. Furthermore, our microscopic model suggests that the induced polarization originates from SOC-independent p-d hybridization governed by electronic hopping. Our results suggest a possible route toward type-II multiferroics combining strong MEC with large electronic polarization in collinear 3d magnetic systems.

</details>


### [13] [Target-Distribution-Guided Cross-Functional Fine-Tuning of Machine-Learning Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25032)
*Yuki Nagai,Bo Thomsen,Motoyuki Shiga*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper addresses the distribution mismatch problem in cross-functional fine-tuning of MLIPs by using a self-learning hybrid Monte Carlo (SLHMC) workflow that ensures target-distribution coverage. Applied to rutile TiO₂ with MACE-MP-0 fine-tuned toward PBE, r²SCAN, and HSE06, the method outperforms standard relabeling approaches, particularly for expensive hybrid functionals like HSE06.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-functional fine-tuning of MLIPs is often treated as a relabeling problem, but the resulting training data may be drawn from the wrong equilibrium distribution because statistical weights of configurations change across exchange-correlation functionals. This distribution mismatch can degrade model accuracy even when target-level labels are used.

Method: The authors employ a target-distribution-guided workflow based on self-learning hybrid Monte Carlo (SLHMC), where trial configurations are proposed by a machine-learning potential and accepted/rejected using target-functional DFT energies. They fine-tune the MACE-MP-0 foundation potential toward PBE, r²SCAN, and HSE06 functionals for rutile TiO₂.

Result: The adapted potentials reproduce target-anchored nearest-neighbor Ti–O distributions, radial distribution functions, and NPT cell metrics more accurately than foundation-model and off-target relabeling controls. HSE06-guided fine-tuning improves structural and thermodynamic properties that are computationally prohibitive with direct hybrid-functional molecular dynamics.

Conclusion: Target-distribution coverage is an essential component of cross-functional MLIP transfer; accurate target-level labels alone are insufficient when the configurational distribution is mismatched.

Abstract: Cross-functional fine-tuning of machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) is often treated as a relabeling problem, where configurations generated at one density-functional level are relabeled using a higher-fidelity target functional. However, the resulting training data may be drawn from the wrong equilibrium distribution, because the statistical weights of configurations change across exchange--correlation functionals. Here we address this distribution mismatch using a target-distribution-guided workflow based on self-learning hybrid Monte Carlo (SLHMC), in which trial configurations are proposed by a machine-learning potential and accepted or rejected using target-functional density-functional-theory energies. Using rutile TiO$_2$ as a test system, we fine-tune the MACE-MP-0 foundation potential toward PBE, r$^2$SCAN, and HSE06 target functionals. The resulting adapted potentials reproduce target-anchored nearest-neighbor Ti--O distributions, radial distribution functions, and the NPT cell metrics examined here more accurately than the foundation-model and off-target relabeling controls considered in this work. In particular, HSE06-guided fine-tuning improves structural and thermodynamic properties that are difficult to access with direct hybrid-functional molecular dynamics because of the computational cost of exact exchange. These results indicate that target-distribution coverage is an essential component of cross-functional MLIP transfer, and that accurate target-level labels alone may be insufficient when the configurational distribution is mismatched.

</details>


### [14] [Termination-Dependent Surface States and Magnetic Fingerprints of Chiral Helimagnet Cr1/3TaS2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24415)
*Bo Liang,Xue Li,Congcong Le,Zirui Wu,Wenpei Zhu,Neng Cai,Yong-Chang Lau,Xianxin Wu,Jiayu Liu,Zhanfeng Liu,Hongen Zhu,Tongrui Li,Zhicheng Jiang,Yu Huang,Wenchuan Jing,Xun Ma,Qi Jiang,Hang Li,Zhihao Cai,Xuezhi Chen,Gexing Qu,Yiwei Cheng,Bing-Jie Chen,Zhengtai Liu,Dawei Shen,Mao Ye,Shengtao Cui,Zhe Sun,Koji Miyamoto,Taichi Okuda,Kenya Shimada,Yaobo Huang,Zhenhua Chen,Lin Zhao,Baojie Feng,Xinguo Ren,Wenhong Wang,Xingjiang Zhou,Guodong Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using micro-focused ARPES and first-principles calculations, the study reveals termination-dependent surface electronic states in Cr1/3TaS2 chiral helimagnets, showing distinct temperature evolution across the magnetic transition (TC~142K), including magnetic band splitting that vanishes above TC and serves as a spectroscopic fingerprint of chiral helimagnetic order.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct experimental studies of termination-dependent surface electronic structures and their temperature-driven magnetic evolution in chiral helimagnets remain largely unexplored, hindering microscopic understanding of electronic states crucial for low-dimensional spintronic devices.

Method: Combined high-resolution ARPES with a micro-focused beam and surface-state-resolved first-principles calculations on Cr1/3TaS2.

Result: TaS2-terminated surface shows folded monolayer-like bands with a triangular electron pocket at superlattice K̄ point; Cr-terminated surface exhibits reconstructed hole pockets with magnetic band splitting that follows the chiral helimagnetic order parameter and disappears above TC; multiple ultranarrow Cr-d-derived surface flat bands are observed.

Conclusion: Cr1/3TaS2 is established as a model system where surface electronic states are strongly coupled to chiral magnetism, opening opportunities for chiral spintronic and valleytronic micro/nanodevices.

Abstract: Chiral helimagnets based on intercalated transition-metal dichalcogenides, characterized by nano-scale spin ordering, provide a powerful route to engineer chiral spin textures (e.g. the topologically protected magnetic solitons) and emergent electronic functionality at reduced dimensions, where surface and interface states often dominate device operation. However, despite growing interest, direct experimental studies of termination-dependent surface electronic structures and their temperature-driven magnetic evolution remain largely unexplored, hindering a microscopic understanding of the electronic states that is crucial for the development of low-dimensional spintronic devices. Here, for the first time, taking Cr1/3TaS2 as a representative example, we systematically investigate the termination-dependent surface electronic states of the chiral helimagnets and uncover their distinct temperature evolution across the magnetic transition (TC~142K) by combining high-resolution ARPES with a micro-focused beam and surface-state-resolved first-principles calculations. The TaS2-terminated surface hosts folded monolayer-like TaS2 bands under the $\sqrt3\times\sqrt3$ superlattice potential and a shallow triangular electron pocket at the superlattice $\bar K$ point arising from Cr-Ta orbital hybridization. In contrast, the Cr-terminated surface exhibits reconstructed hole pockets with pronounced magnetic band splitting. This splitting disappears above TC and closely follows the chiral helimagnetic order parameter, providing a direct spectroscopic fingerprint of chiral helimagnetic order. In addition, multiple ultranarrow Cr-d-derived surface flat bands are resolved. These findings establish Cr1/3TaS2 as a model system in which surface electronic states are strongly coupled to chiral magnetism, opening new opportunities for chiral spintronic and valleytronic micro/nanodevices.

</details>


### [15] [Multi-Source Domain Transfer Learning for Accurate Property Prediction in Two-Dimensional Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24455)
*Huiyang Zhang,Xinyu Chen,Qionghua Zhou,Jinlan Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A multi-source domain transfer learning framework predicts 2D carrier mobilities from crystal structures alone (R² > 0.90) and screens 55 novel high-mobility 2D semiconductors validated by first-principles calculations, overcoming data scarcity and distribution fragmentation in 2D materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data scarcity and fragmented distributions hinder machine learning for 2D materials discovery, as evaluating functional properties requires time-consuming simulations and known structural prototypes limit unified high-throughput screening.

Method: A multi-source domain transfer learning framework combining (1) a shared feature extractor with adversarial transfer learning and maximum mean discrepancy to create domain-invariant latent spaces, and (2) a sample-adaptive weighted ensemble strategy for dynamic aggregation of predictions from multiple source domains.

Result: Achieved R² > 0.90 for 2D carrier mobility prediction using only crystal structures; screened 55 novel high-mobility 2D semiconductors validated via first-principles electron-phonon coupling analysis confirming exceptional transport and stability.

Conclusion: This framework can accelerate machine learning-assisted materials design and discovery with reduced data restrictions, particularly for data-scarce material systems.

Abstract: Machine learning has revolutionized materials discovery, but data scarcity remains a critical bottleneck for complex functional properties. As emerging systems, two-dimensional (2D) materials possess limited overall data volumes. Evaluating their diverse functional properties requires time-consuming simulations, hindering unified high-throughput screening. Furthermore, restrictions in known structural prototypes lead to highly fragmented data distributions. To address these challenges, we propose a multi-source domain transfer learning framework to extract generalizable and complementary knowledge from diverse crystalline systems. To mitigate data scarcity, the framework employs a shared feature extractor that integrates adversarial transfer learning with maximum mean discrepancy, mapping crystal structures into a domain-invariant latent space while preserving underlying physical correlations. To resolve distribution fragmentation, a sample-adaptive weighted ensemble strategy is subsequently utilized to dynamically aggregate predictions from multiple source domains. Relying solely on crystal structures, the framework predicts 2D carrier mobilities with an R2 score exceeding 0.90. The framework successfully screened 55 novel high-mobility 2D semiconductors, which were validated via first-principles electron-phonon coupling analysis, confirming their exceptional transport properties and stability. This work can potentially accelerate machine learning-assisted materials design and discovery with less data restriction.

</details>


### [16] [Techno-economic Analysis of Light Isotope-enriched Elements for Lightweighting Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24474)
*Wenbo Bao,Zhihao Yang,Taeyoung Wang,Joseph F. Wild,Yuan Yang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents a techno-economic analysis of using light isotope-enriched elements as a lightweighting strategy for aerospace applications. By enriching the lightest stable isotopes in elements, atomic mass can be reduced without altering structural/chemical properties. Among 12 aerospace-relevant elements analyzed, 9 (especially Li, B, Zn, Ni, Mo, Sn) show attractive economic benefits. An Airbus A380 could save ~$700K over 30 years, a Falcon 9 could save ~$516K, and a Starship could save ~$2.37M over its lifetime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mass reduction is critical for aerospace applications (aircraft, spacecraft). Current lightweighting strategies rely on novel materials/structures design. This work explores an alternative: enriching the lightest stable isotopes in elements to lower atomic mass while preserving material properties, but the economic feasibility of this approach was previously unclear.

Method: The authors conducted a techno-economic analysis estimating both isotope enrichment costs (scaled from established large-scale enrichment processes) and economic gains from mass reduction. They analyzed 12 aerospace-relevant elements: Li, B, C, Mg, Cl, Ti, Ni, Fe, Cu, Zn, Mo, and Sn.

Result: Nine elements show potentially attractive economic benefits at moderate enrichment levels: Li, B, Zn, Ni, Mo, and Sn are especially promising. C, Mg, and Fe provide little or no benefit. Case studies: Airbus A380 could save ~USD 700K over 30 years; SpaceX Falcon 9 could save ~USD 516K; SpaceX Starship could save ~USD 2.37M over its lifetime.

Conclusion: Isotopic mass reduction is a potential drop-in lightweighting strategy. The results provide an initial screening of promising candidate elements. However, exact enrichment costs need further investigation.

Abstract: Lightweighting is critical to mass-sensitive applications such as aircraft and space transportation. Conventional lightweight strategies often rely on new designs of materials and structures. An alternative approach is to enrich the lightest stable isotopes in an element to reduce the elements atomic mass while having little effect on structural and chemical properties. However, the economic feasibility of this concept remains unclear. Here we present a techno-economic analysis of light isotope-enriched elements for lightweighting applications by estimating isotope enrichment cost and the economic gain from mass reduction. The enrichment cost is scaled from established large-scale processes. Twelve common aerospace-relevant elements are considered, including Li, B, C, Mg, Cl, Ti, Ni, Fe, Cu, Zn, Mo, and Sn. We find that nine elements, especially Li, B, Zn, Ni, Mo, and Sn, show potentially attractive economic benefit at moderate enrichment levels, whereas C, Mg, and Fe provide little or no benefit. With the optimized enrichment levels, an Airbus A380 is expected to save approximately USD 700 K over a 30-year operational lifetime, a SpaceX Falcon 9 could save USD 516 K, and a SpaceX Starship is expected to save USD 2.37 million over its whole lifetime. While the exact enrichment cost needs to be further investigated, these results provide an initial screening of promising candidate elements and highlight isotopic mass reduction as a potential drop-in lightweighting strategy.

</details>


### [17] [Application and Performance Assessment of Annealing Methods for Electrostatic-Energy-Based Configuration Search in Mixed Crystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24557)
*Tack Saquai,Kenta Hongo,Ryo Maezono,Tom Ichibha*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a framework using simulated annealing (SA) and quantum annealing (QA) to pre-screen mixed-crystal configurations by mapping Ewald electrostatic energy onto an Ising-type Hamiltonian. SA achieved robust speed-ups of 30-300x across all system scales, while QA was effective only for small systems due to chain-break issues. SA is recommended as the most practical general-purpose approach for accelerating candidate-structure generation before first-principles calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exhaustive evaluation of all possible substitutional configurations in solid solutions and disordered materials is impractical due to combinatorial explosion (exponential growth in site occupations). A pre-screening method to rapidly identify low-energy configurations is needed before expensive first-principles calculations.

Method: Substitutional occupations are represented as binary variables, mapping the Ewald electrostatic energy onto an Ising-type Hamiltonian. The search for low-energy configurations is formulated as a combinatorial optimization problem, solved using both simulated annealing (SA) and quantum annealing (QA). Benchmarked against exhaustive search on small-scale (CaYAlO$_4$), medium-scale ($\beta$-KSbF$_4$), and large-scale (Ba-doped SiAlON) systems.

Result: For CaYAlO$_4$, SA achieved ~30x speed-up and QA >100x speed-up, both identifying all lowest-energy configurations. For $\beta$-KSbF$_4$ and Ba-doped SiAlON, SA achieved ~200-300x speed-ups with robust identification of lowest-energy structures. QA was effective for small-scale but showed limited speed-up for medium-scale and missed low-energy configurations due to chain breaks.

Conclusion: SA is currently the most robust and general-purpose approach for rapid electrostatic-energy-based pre-screening of mixed-crystal configurations. The formulation can be implemented automatically using publicly available libraries, offering a practical route to accelerate candidate-structure generation before first-principles calculations.

Abstract: In first-principles design of solid solutions and disordered materials, exhaustive evaluation of all possible substitutional configurations is often impractical because the number of site occupations increases exponentially. Here, we develop a framework for pre-screening mixed-crystal configurations using annealing methods, where the Ewald electrostatic energy is used as the objective function. Substitutional occupations are represented by binary variables, allowing the Ewald energy to be mapped onto an Ising-type Hamiltonian and the search for low-energy configurations to be formulated as a combinatorial optimization problem.
  We implement this formulation using simulated annealing (SA) and quantum annealing (QA), and benchmark their performance against exhaustive search. For the small-scale system CaYAlO$_4$, SA achieved a speed-up of about 30 times, while QA achieved a speed-up of more than 100 times; both methods identified all lowest-energy configurations. For the medium-scale system $β$-KSbF$_4$ and the large-scale Ba-doped SiAlON system, SA achieved speed-ups of about 200-300 times while robustly identifying the lowest-energy structures. In contrast, QA was effective for the small-scale case but showed limited speed-up for medium-scale problems and missed some low-energy configurations due to chain breaks.
  These results indicate that SA is currently the most robust and general-purpose approach for rapid pre-screening of mixed-crystal configurations based on electrostatic energy. The proposed formulation can be implemented automatically using publicly available libraries and provides a practical route for accelerating candidate-structure generation before first-principles calculations.

</details>


### [18] [Ab-initio Crystal Structure Determination from Powder X-Ray Diffraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24594)
*Kaixiang Su,Osman Goni Ridwan,Hongfei Xue,Qiang Zhu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A hybrid ab-initio approach for crystal structure determination from powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) that decomposes the problem into two-stage optimization: discrete selection of space group/unit cell/Wyckoff sites, followed by continuous coordinate optimization. Integrates AI techniques with physics-informed constraints to overcome limitations of purely data-driven methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Determining crystal structures from PXRD data remains challenging, especially with noisy experimental data or high structural complexity. Existing AI generative models learn direct pattern-to-structure mappings but often fail on complex or out-of-distribution cases.

Method: A two-stage hierarchical optimization: (1) discrete selection of space group symmetry, unit cell parameters, and Wyckoff site combinations; (2) continuous optimization of atomic coordinates within selected Wyckoff positions. Integrates AI-based peak profile analysis, density estimation, and energy minimization with physics-informed constraints.

Result: The hierarchical optimization framework enables robust structure determination even for challenging cases with high structural complexity or limited experimental data quality, overcoming limitations of purely data-driven PXRD solvers.

Conclusion: Provides a principled pathway for incorporating crystallographic knowledge into AI models, leading to more reliable and generalizable crystal structure determination from PXRD data.

Abstract: Determining crystal structures from powder X-ray diffraction (PXRD) has been a significant challenge in materials science, particularly when experimental data contain noise or the target structure has a high complexity. While recent AI generative models show promise for rapid structure generation, they predominantly employ data-driven approaches to learn direct mappings between PXRD patterns and crystal structures, often failing on complex or out-of-distribution cases. In this work, we present a hybrid ab-initio approach that decomposes structure determination into a two-stage optimization problem: (1) discrete selection of space group symmetry, unit cell parameters, and Wyckoff site combinations; and (2) continuous optimization of atomic coordinates within the selected Wyckoff positions. By integrating AI-based techniques for peak profile analysis, density estimation and energy minimization with physics-informed constraints, our method systematically overcomes limitations of purely data-driven PXRD solvers. We demonstrate that this hierarchical optimization framework enables robust structure determination even for challenging cases with high structural complexity or limited experimental data quality. Our approach provides a principled pathway for incorporating crystallographic knowledge into AI models for more reliable and generalizable crystal structure determination.

</details>


### [19] [Redox behaviour of Fe impurities in BaTiO$_3$ based on many-body calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24747)
*Zhiyuan Li,Hamza Zerdoumi,Hao Wang,Ruiwen Xie,Hongbin Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using DFT-based many-body techniques, the study investigates Fe impurities in BaTiO₃, revealing mixed valence states (Fe²⁺/Fe³⁺) tunable by oxygen vacancies, with the redox behavior driven by charge transfer via d₃z²₋r² orbital shifts, and successfully reproduces experimental XPS spectra via crystal field multiplet calculations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the redox behavior and mixed valence nature of Fe impurities in BaTiO₃ and how they can be tuned by oxygen vacancies, with implications for ferroelectric and multiferroic applications.

Method: DFT-based many-body techniques for electronic structure and spectroscopy; Wannier function-derived local atomic Hamiltonian solved with the crystal field multiplet approach for XPS evaluation.

Result: Fe impurities exhibit mixed valence (Fe²⁺ and Fe³⁺ configurations), tunable via oxygen vacancies that favor Fe²⁺; the redox behavior originates from charge transfer associated with shifting of d₃z²₋r² orbitals; simulated XPS spectra agree well with experimental measurements.

Conclusion: The redox mechanism of Fe in BaTiO₃ is governed by orbital-specific charge transfer mediated by oxygen vacancies, and the crystal field multiplet approach reliably reproduces experimental spectroscopic features.

Abstract: Based on detailed electronic structure and spectroscopy obtained using DFT-based many-body techniques, the redox behavior of Fe impurities in BaTiO$_3$ is investigated. It is observed that Fe impurities exhibit a mixed valence nature, comprising mostly Fe$^{2+}$ ($3d^6$) and Fe$^{3+}$ ($3d^5$) configurations, and such configurations can be tuned via oxygen vacancies which favor Fe$^{2+}$. The origin of such a redox behavior can be attributed to the charge transfer caused by shifting of the $d_{3z^2-r^2}$ orbitals. Furthermore, x-ray photoemission spectroscopy is evaluated by solving the Wannier function-derived local atomic Hamiltonian using the crystal field multiplet approach, with good agreements with recent experimental measurements.

</details>


### [20] [Proton-electron coupled catalyst for ionomer-free electrochemical energy conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24757)
*Ao Zhang,Ran Wang,Mohammed O. Bazaid,Shiyi Wang,Ting-Jung Hsiao,Yibo Wang,Antonio Sorrentino,Yang Liu,Yu-Han Joseph Tsai,Boxuan Zhou,Bosi Peng,Zeyan Liu,Xiangfeng Duan,Adam Z. Weber,William A. Goddard,Seung Soon Jang,Yu Huang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces a one-dimensional proton-electron coupled catalyst (PECC) that integrates electronic and protonic transport into the catalyst itself, enabling ionomer-free cathode catalyst layers in PEMFCs. This design reduces oxygen transport resistance by 95%, boosts power density by up to 85%, improves durability, and enhances proton conductivity by 249% over Nafion, leading to ~20% higher fuel efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional PEMFC catalyst layers rely on separate percolating carbon networks (electron transport) and ionomer binders (proton transport), creating fundamental trade-offs between reactant accessibility, ionic conductivity, and catalyst activity. This passive catalyst design limits overall electrochemical device performance.

Method: A one-dimensional proton-electron coupled catalyst (PECC) architecture is designed where the catalyst material itself simultaneously conducts both electrons and protons to active sites, eliminating the need for separate ionomer binders in the cathode catalyst layer. Performance is evaluated in PEMFCs and electrochemical hydrogen pumps, with molecular dynamics simulations quantifying proton conductivity.

Result: PECC enables ionomer-free CCLs, achieving 95% reduction in non-Fickian oxygen transport, 34% and 85% higher power density at ~0.090 mg/cm² and ~0.037 mg/cm² Pt loadings respectively, 65% mass activity retention with 32% higher power density after 30k accelerated stress tests, and 249% higher proton conductivity than Nafion. ~20% fuel efficiency improvement is demonstrated.

Conclusion: The PECC architecture fundamentally addresses core mass and charge transport limitations in PEMFCs by unifying proton and electron transport pathways within the catalyst itself. This approach opens new design paradigms for high-performance, cost-effective electrochemical energy devices.

Abstract: Efficient electrochemical energy devices are vital to renewable energy technology, yet coordinating the effective flow of electrons, ions, and chemical species continues to be a major challenge. In conventional proton-exchange membrane fuel cell (PEMFC) catalyst layers, proton and electron transport are supplied separately through percolating carbon networks and ionomer binders, rendering the catalyst largely passive and imposing fundamental trade-offs between reactant accessibility, ionic conductivity, and catalyst activity. Here, we introduce a one-dimensional proton-electron coupled catalyst (PECC) design, a transport-integrated electrocatalyst architecture in which the catalyst itself simultaneously supplies electronic and protonic transport to catalyst active sites. Using this PECC, PEMFCs can have an ionomer-free cathode catalyst layer (CCL), resulting in a dramatic 95% reduction in non-Fickian oxygen transport and boosting power density by 34% and 85% compared to traditional CCLs, with cathode Pt loadings of approximately 0.090 mg/cm^2 and 0.037 mg/cm^2, respectively. Meanwhile, PECC retains 65% of its mass activity and exhibits 32% higher power density than its ionomer-based CCL counterpart after 30k accelerated stressed test. Similar mass transport improvements have been observed in the electrochemical hydrogen pump (EHP) using PECC in the catalyst layers. Molecular dynamics simulations show the PECC's proton conductivity is 249% higher than Nafion. This PECC catalyst structure addresses core transport problems in PEMFCs, leading to almost 20% improvement in fuel efficiency and opens up new possibilities for designing high-performance, cost-effective electrochemical devices.

</details>


### [21] [Charge dynamics at nitrogen impurities and nitrogen-vacancy centers in diamond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24768)
*Chandan Kumar Vishwakarma,J. K. Nangoi,Mark E. Turiansky,Chris G. Van de Walle*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses first-principles DFT to quantitatively investigate carrier capture processes at NV centers and substitutional nitrogen impurities in diamond, finding that fast nonradiative capture occurs via excited states for NV centers and providing key capture coefficients for charge-state dynamics modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Charge-state instability and spectral diffusion induced by substitutional nitrogen impurities (N_C) remain key challenges for NV-center-based quantum technologies, and quantitative understanding of carrier capture processes is lacking.

Method: First-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations of nonradiative carrier capture mediated by multiphonon emission, along with radiative and thermal emission rates.

Result: Electron capture at N_C^0→N_C^-: coefficient 2.2×10⁻⁸ cm³s⁻¹ at 300K, matching experiment. N_C^+ capture even faster (1.0×10⁻⁴ cm³s⁻¹). NV ground-state capture negligibly slow; excited-state capture dominates: NV^-→NV^{0*} hole capture coefficient 1.8×10⁻⁷ cm³s⁻¹, NV^0→NV^{-*} electron capture coefficient 2.1×10⁻⁹ cm³s⁻¹ at 300K.

Conclusion: NV center charge-state dynamics proceeds via nonradiative capture into excited states followed by fast radiative decay, rather than direct ground-state capture; the calculated coefficients provide essential inputs for modeling charge-state dynamics in diamond quantum devices.

Abstract: The nitrogen-vacancy (NV) center in diamond is the prototype quantum defect that enables a variety of diamond-based quantum technologies. However, charge-state instability and spectral diffusion, often induced by substitutional nitrogen impurities (N$_{\rm C}$), remain key challenges for device performance. Here, we employ first-principles density functional theory calculations to quantitatively investigate nonradiative carrier capture processes mediated by multiphonon emission at both the NV center and the N$_{\rm C}$ impurity. For relevant cases, we also compute the rates of radiative and thermal emission processes. For N$_{\rm C}^0$ $\to$ N$_{\rm C}^-$, we obtain an electron capture coefficient of $2.2 \times 10^{-8}$ cm$^3$s$^{-1}$ at 300 K. Both the magnitude and temperature dependence are in excellent agreement with experimentally measured capture cross sections. Electron capture at N$_{\rm C}^+$ is even faster, with a capture coefficient of $1.0 \times 10^{-4}$ cm$^3$s$^{-1}$ at 300 K. For the NV center, we find that carrier capture rates involving only the ground states of NV$^0$ and NV$^-$ are negligibly slow. However, capture into the excited states (NV$^{0*}$ and NV$^{-*}$) is significantly faster. In particular, the capture coefficient for the hole capture process NV$^-$ $\to$ NV$^{0*}$ is as large as $1.8 \times 10^{-7}$ cm$^3$s$^{-1}$ and largely temperature-independent. Hole capture at NV$^-$ will thus occur via nonradiative capture into an excited state of NV$^{0}$ followed by fast radiative decay to the NV$^0$ ground state. Similarly, electron capture at NV$^0$ will occur via the NV$^0$ $\to$ NV$^{-*}$ $\to$ NV$^-$ pathway, but with a lower nonradiative capture coefficient ($2.1 \times 10^{-9}$ cm$^3$s$^{-1}$ at 300 K). Our calculated capture coefficients and rates provide essential information for analyzing charge-state dynamics.

</details>


### [22] [Photoluminescence Identification of Multiple Local Eu3+ Environments in BaTiO3 Ceramics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24800)
*Yutong Cai,Duanting Yan,Hancheng Zhu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Eu3+ photoluminescence reveals two distinct local environments in BaTiO3 ceramics, with their relative populations varying systematically with sintering temperature, even when bulk diffraction shows no structural change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional diffraction techniques predominantly probe the average lattice structure and can miss subtle changes in local coordination environments, defect chemistry, and dopant distribution in ferroelectric perovskites like BaTiO3.

Method: Eu3+ photoluminescence was used as a local optical probe in BaTiO3 ceramics sintered at 1250, 1300, and 1350 °C, complemented by XRD, Raman spectroscopy, electron microscopy, and elemental mapping.

Result: The 1250 °C sample showed the highest emission intensity; sintering temperature altered the charge-transfer band vs. 4f-4f transition ratios. The 5D0→7F0 emission split into two components (~579.5 nm and ~582.2 nm) with temperature-dependent relative weights, and double-exponential decay at 612 nm confirmed multiple Eu-related local environments.

Conclusion: Eu3+ luminescence is a sensitive method for detecting and tracking local structural heterogeneity in BaTiO3 ceramics that is invisible to conventional diffraction techniques.

Abstract: BaTiO3 is a model ferroelectric perovskite whose properties are highly sensitive to local structure, defect chemistry, and dopant distribution. However, conventional diffraction mainly probes the average lattice and can miss subtle changes in the local coordination environment. Here we use Eu3+ photoluminescence as a local optical probe for BaTiO3 ceramics prepared at 1250, 1300, and 1350 °C. X-ray diffraction and Raman spectra show that all samples retain the tetragonal BaTiO3 phase within the detection limits of these techniques. Electron microscopy reveals a porous ceramic microstructure with temperature-dependent grain growth, and elemental mapping confirms a spatially distributed Eu signal. The Eu3+ excitation and emission spectra show strong sensitivity to the processing temperature. The sample sintered at 1250 °C gives the highest emission intensity, while higher sintering temperatures change the relative intensity of the charge-transfer band and the 4f-4f transitions. Most importantly, the 5D0 to 7F0 emission contains two reproducible components near 579.5 nm and 582.2 nm. Their relative weights vary with sintering temperature, and double-exponential decay at 612 nm further supports the presence of multiple Eu-related local environments. These results show that Eu3+ luminescence provides a sensitive route to track local structural heterogeneity in BaTiO3 ceramics.

</details>


### [23] [Orbital-Engineered Altermagnetism in Two-Dimensional Square Lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25128)
*Yixuan Che,Peibo Xu,Haifeng Lv,Xiaojun Wu,Jinlong Yang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper establishes a microscopic framework linking altermagnetic splitting in 2D square lattices to orbital character, showing that dual-orbital configurations enable d-wave and g-wave altermagnetism, and identifies M-TCNX monolayers as candidate g-wave altermagnets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the microscopic origin of altermagnetic splitting in 2D square lattices and link it to orbital character, providing a design framework beyond phenomenological descriptions.

Method: Tight-binding modeling, symmetry analysis, and orbital-resolved hopping analysis on 2D square lattices, validated against candidate metal-organic framework monolayers.

Result: Single-orbital antiferromagnetic square lattices remain spin-degenerate; dual-orbital interwoven configurations lift Kramers degeneracy and produce d-wave or g-wave altermagnetic states. M-TCNX monolayers with mcm topology are identified as g-wave altermagnets.

Conclusion: Orbital anisotropy in same-spin hopping channels is the key mechanism; the work provides a symmetry-explicit, wavefunction-level framework for designing orbital-controlled altermagnetism in 2D square lattices.

Abstract: Altermagnetism is characterized by even-parity spin-momentum locking in spin-split bands despite zero net magnetization and negligible spin-orbit coupling. Here, we formulate a microscopic framework that links altermagnetic splitting in two-dimensional (2D) square lattices to orbital character. Using tight-binding models and symmetry analysis, we show that, within the minimal antiferromagnetic square-lattice model, single-orbital lattices remain spin-degenerate, whereas interwoven dual-orbital configurations lift Kramers degeneracy and generate d-wave or g-wave altermagnetic states. The spin-splitting originates from orbital anisotropy in the same-spin hopping channels. Guided by this framework, we identify M-TCNX (M = Cr, Mn, Fe; TCNX = TCNE, TCNQ) metal-organic framework monolayers with mcm topology as candidate g-wave altermagnets. Our work provides a symmetry-explicit wavefunction-level design framework for orbital-controlled altermagnetism in 2D square lattices.

</details>


### [24] [Native defects and erbium impurities in CaWO4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24809)
*Minseok Choi,Mark E. Turiansky,BaiQing Zhao,Jeff D. Thompson,Chris G. Van de Walle*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Hybrid DFT calculations on CaWO₄ reveal that oxygen and calcium vacancies are the most stable native defects, with oxygen-related defects responsible for key optical transitions. Ca interstitials, O vacancies, and O interstitials are highly mobile even below room temperature. Er dopants preferentially occupy Ca sites but can be deactivated by complexes with V_Ca and O_i; annealing mobilizes Er interstitials to substitutional sites for stable emission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the energetics, electronic properties, optical transitions, and migration barriers of native defects in CaWO₄, and to examine the behavior of Er dopants for applications requiring stable optical emission.

Method: Hybrid density functional theory (DFT) calculations were performed to study native defect energetics, electronic structure, optical transition levels, and migration barriers. Er doping was also investigated computationally.

Result: O and Ca vacancies are the most likely native defects; W-related defects are unlikely. V_O⁺ and V_Ca⁻ form complexes. Optical absorption/emission peaks are mainly from oxygen-related defects. Ca_i²⁺, V_O²⁺, and O_i²⁻ are highly mobile below room temperature. Er substitutes on Ca site in positive charge state but can be deactivated by V_Ca and O_i complexes. Er interstitials cause spectral diffusion/blinking; annealing moves them to substitutional sites for stable emission.

Conclusion: Native defect chemistry in CaWO₄ is dominated by oxygen and calcium vacancies. Oxygen-related defects govern the optical properties. High mobility of certain defects explains low-temperature migration. Er doping requires careful management of defect complexes and annealing to achieve stable emission.

Abstract: We perform hybrid density functional calculation to study the energetics, electronic properties, optical transitions, and migration barriers of native defects in CaWO$_4$. Oxygen and calcium vacancies are most likely to form in the absence of doping, but interstitials could also incorporate. Tungsten-related defects are unlikely to be present. The positively charged $V_{\rm O}$ and the negatively charged $V_{\rm Ca}$ are likely to form complexes. Calculated optical transition levels indicate that experimentally observed absorption and emission peaks can be attributed mainly to oxygen-related defects. Calculations of migration barriers allow us to conclude that Ca$_i^{2+}$, $V_{\rm O}^{2+}$ and O$_i^{2-}$ are highly mobile, even below room temperature. We have also examined Er dopants, finding that erbium easily substitutes on the Ca site in a positive charge state. Erbium can form complexes with $V_{\rm Ca}$ and O$_i$, which would deactivate the Er. If Er is introduced by implantation, Er interstitials are likely present, which will produce emission that is prone to spectral diffusion and blinking. Our calculated properties of Er$_i$ explain why annealing at modest temperatures allows the interstitials to move into substitutional sites and point defects to move away, resulting in stable emission.

</details>


### [25] [Thermal PBE in warm dense matter: Does it matter and is it accurate?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25270)
*Kushal Ramakrishna,Mani Lokamani,Zhandos A. Moldabekov,Tobias Dornheim,Kieron Burke,Attila Cangi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Thermal PBE, derived from conditional probability density functional theory, is implemented and benchmarked for warm dense matter. It significantly improves predictions of energies, forces, pressures, and charge densities over LDA and PBE, closely matching PIMC reference data at negligible extra cost, demonstrating its practical utility as an accurate semilocal functional for warm dense regime simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the PBE GGA functional to finite temperatures using conditional probability density functional theory and systematically benchmark its accuracy for warm dense matter simulations, addressing the need for improved semilocal exchange-correlation free energy functionals beyond thermal LDA.

Method: The authors implement thermal PBE within Kohn-Sham DFT and benchmark it against thermal LDA, standard PBE, and LDA functionals, comparing calculated energies, forces, pressures, and electronic charge densities with path integral Monte Carlo reference data for warm dense matter.

Result: Thermal PBE significantly improves the description of warm dense matter properties compared to LDA, PBE, and thermal LDA, achieving close agreement with PIMC reference data at negligible additional computational cost.

Conclusion: Thermal PBE is an accurate and practical semilocal functional for warm dense matter simulations, offering a substantial improvement over existing functionals without significant computational overhead.

Abstract: Conditional probability density functional theory has recently been used to derive the temperature dependence of the Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) generalized gradient approximation (GGA) for the exchange-correlation (XC) free energy. We implement and systematically benchmark thermal PBE within Kohn-Sham density functional theory calculations of warm dense matter. Comparisons with the local density approximation (LDA) and PBE functionals, as well as thermal LDA, show that thermal PBE significantly improves the description of warm dense matter properties, including energies, forces, pressures, and electronic charge densities. In particular, thermal PBE exhibits close agreement with path integral Monte Carlo (PIMC) reference data at negligible additional computational cost. This work demonstrates the practical utility of thermal PBE as an accurate semilocal functional for simulations in the warm dense regime.

</details>


### [26] [Edge Dislocation Mediated Anomalous Charge Transfer in Face Centered Cubic High Entropy Alloys](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24887)
*Gautam Anand,Swarnava Ghosh,Suman Chabri,Markus Eisenbach*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses large-scale ab initio calculations to reveal anomalous charge redistribution near edge dislocation cores in CoNi, CoCrNi, and CoCrFeMnNi high-entropy alloys, driven by collective electronegativity equalization rather than pairwise interactions, and links this to asymmetric volume response via magneto-volume fluctuations, establishing a direct coupling between dislocation-mediated charge transfer and local volumetric effects in chemically complex alloys.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While dislocation-volume misfit interactions are central to solid-solution strengthening models in high-entropy alloys, the explicit influence of dislocations on local charge transfer has not been investigated, leaving a gap in understanding the electronic origins of strengthening mechanisms.
