Method: Large-scale ab initio (first-principles) calculations were performed to examine dislocation-mediated charge transfer in CoNi, CoCrNi, and CoCrFeMnNi alloys, focusing on electronic redistribution near edge dislocation cores.

Result: An anomalous charge redistribution near edge dislocation cores was observed, deviating from conventional electronegativity trends. This behavior arises from collective electronegativity equalization effects. Asymmetric atomic volume response in compressive/tensile regions is rationalized by anomalous magneto-volume fluctuations.

Conclusion: A direct coupling exists between dislocation-induced electronic redistribution and local volumetric response in chemically complex alloys. This provides a pathway toward electronically informed solid-solution strengthening models and defect-aware alloy design strategies, with local electronic redistribution near dislocation cores playing a critical role in governing deformation behavior and defect energetics.

Abstract: Charge transfer in concentrated alloys governs their structural stability and functional response, and can be strongly perturbed by lattice defects. In high-entropy alloys, the interaction between edge dislocations and volume misfit plays a central role in solid-solution strengthening models; however, the influence of dislocations on the local charge transfer has not been explicitly investigated. In this work, large-scale ab initio calculations are employed to examine the dislocation-mediated charge transfer in CoNi, CoCrNi and CoCrFeMnNi alloys. The calculations reveal an anomalous charge redistribution near edge dislocation cores, including deviation from the conventional electronegativity trend. The observed behavior is shown to originate from collective electronegativity equalisation effects rather than simple pairwise atomic interactions. Furthermore, the asymmetric atomic volume response within the compressive and tensile regions of the dislocation field is rationalised in terms of anomalous magneto-volume fluctuations. These results establish a direct coupling between dislocation-induced electronic redistribution and local volumetric response in chemically complex alloys. The demonstrated coupling between dislocation-mediated charge transfer and atomic volume fluctuations provides a pathway toward electronically informed solid-solutions strengthening models and defect-aware alloy design strategy for chemically complex alloys. These findings further suggest that local electronic redistribution near dislocation-cores can play a critical role in governing the deformation behavior and defect-enbergetics in high-entropy alloys.

</details>


### [27] [Efficient cooling by ferroelectric or ferromagnetic hysteresis loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24947)
*M. Apostol*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper proposes an efficient cooling effect achieved by applying external electric or magnetic fields along hysteresis loops, using a simplified energy-expansion model and comparing with the electrocaloric effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To propose and investigate an efficient cooling mechanism that utilizes hysteresis loop cycling under external electric or magnetic fields.

Method: A simplified hysteresis model based on a second-order expansion of energy in powers of polarization and external field is used for numerical illustration.

Result: The cooling effect along hysteresis loops is demonstrated numerically, and compared with the electrocaloric effect.

Conclusion: The proposed hysteresis-loop-driven cooling provides an efficient alternative to conventional electrocaloric cooling.

Abstract: An efficient cooling effect is put forward, by means of external electric or magnetic fields along hysteresis loops. A simplified model of hysteresis is used for numerical illustration. The model is based upon a second-order expansion of the energy in powers of polarization and external field. The electrocaloric effect along hysteresis loops is discussed for comparison.

</details>


### [28] [Imaging Surface Magnetization in Altermagnetic MnTe Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25241)
*Ling-Jie Zhou,Senlei Li,Zi-Jie Yan,Yufei Zhao,Hongtao Rong,Zelong Xiong,Yiran Zhao,Pu Xiao,Lok Kan Lai,Hyeonhu Bae,Haoyu Liu,Chao-Xing Liu,Binghai Yan,Cui-Zu Chang,Hailong Wang,Chunhui Rita Du*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reports nanoscale quantum sensing of the altermagnet α-MnTe using scanning-probe quantum microscopy, visualizing magnetic domains and weak magnetization at interfaces, showing thickness-dependent control of altermagnetic order via external magnetic fields, and linking interfacial magnetization to the anomalous Hall effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets exhibit exotic spin-splitting, unconventional symmetries, and magneto-transport properties, but microscopic imaging of their spontaneous magnetic domains and phases remains underexplored, which is crucial for understanding material properties and spin behaviors.

Method: The authors employed scanning-probe quantum microscopy for nanoscale quantum sensing of epitaxial MnTe films, varying film thicknesses down to the atomic scale to probe magnetization, magnetic domains, and their response to external magnetic fields.

Result: Visualized evanescent magnetization and associated magnetic domains in MnTe films; demonstrated external magnetic field control of altermagnetic order; provided evidence for interfacial origin of weak magnetization; showed correlation between interfacial magnetization and anomalous Hall effect.

Conclusion: The findings advance understanding of emergent altermagnetism and provide insights for designing altermagnet-integrated spintronic devices.

Abstract: Altermagnets with pronounced spin-splitting band structure, unconventional magnetic and crystal symmetries, and exotic magneto-transport properties have received immense interest in cutting-edge spintronics, materials science, and condensed matter physics research. Microscopic imaging of spontaneous magnetic domains and phases in altermagnets constitutes an important step for investigating their underlying material properties, mechanisms, and spin behaviors. Taking advantage of scanning-probe quantum microscopy, here we report nanoscale quantum sensing of a prototypical altermagnet candidate $α$-MnTe. We visualize evanescent magnetization and the associated magnetic domains in epitaxial MnTe films, which allows external magnetic fields to control the intrinsic altermagnetic order and configurations. By evaluating a series of MnTe films with different thicknesses down to the atomic scale, we further present evidence for the interfacial origin of the observed weak magnetization and show its correlation with the anomalous Hall effect in MnTe film. Our results advance the current understanding of emergent altermagnetism, providing insights into future material design of altermagnet-integrated spintronic devices.

</details>


### [29] [Assessment of a GW-BSE approximation scheme on an asymmetric two-dimensional interacting electron system in a perpendicular magnetic field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25053)
*Xiaoguang Wu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper tests a GW-BSE approximation on an asymmetric 2D electron system in a perpendicular magnetic field. The self-consistent Hartree-Fock Green's function and ladder BSE correlation function fail to satisfy Kohn's theorem when effective masses differ, predicting a cyclotron resonance shift that contradicts the theorem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether the GW-BSE approximation scheme respects Kohn's theorem, which demands that cyclotron resonance frequency remains unchanged by electron-electron interactions in a 2D electron system under a perpendicular magnetic field.

Method: The Green's function is computed via self-consistent Hartree-Fock approximation, and the electron density correlation function is obtained by solving the Bethe-Salpeter equation in the ladder diagram approximation for an asymmetric 2D system with two independent effective masses.

Result: When the two effective masses differ, the excitation frequency near cyclotron resonance is lower than the cyclotron resonance frequency at small wave vectors. When masses are equal, the frequency correctly approaches the cyclotron resonance frequency.

Conclusion: The GW-BSE approximation scheme fails to satisfy Kohn's theorem in the asymmetric mass case, indicating that more advanced approximations beyond this scheme are required.

Abstract: A GW-BSE approximation scheme is assessed by applying it to a model of asymmetric two-dimensional (2D) interacting electron system. The model is assumed to have a parabolic band characterized by two independent effective mass parameters. A perpendicular magnetic field is applied to the asymmetric 2D electron system, and the well-known Kohn's theorem is still valid, i.e., the cyclotron resonance is not affected by the electron-electron interaction. This theorem imposes a constraint on the approximation scheme employed in the treatment of electron-electron interaction. In the present study, the Green's function is calculated in the self-consistent Hartree-Fock approximation. The electron density correlation function is calculated by solving a Bethe-Salpeter equation (BSE) in the ladder diagram approximation. It is found that, the excitation frequency near the cyclotron resonance frequency approaches a value that is lower than the cyclotron resonance frequency at small wave vectors, when two effective masses are different. When two effective masses are the same, the excitation frequency approaches the cyclotron resonance frequency at small wave vectors as required. Our findings suggest that the approximation scheme used in this theoretical investigation fails to satisfy the requirement due to the Kohn's theorem, and one should go beyond this approximation scheme.

</details>


### [30] [Theory of Electrically Detected Magnetic Resonance of Silicon Vacancy-Related Spin Pairs in Silicon Carbide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26071)
*David A. Fehr,Corey J. Cochrane,Stephen R. McMillan,Nicholas J. Harmon,Patrick M. Lenahan,Michael E. Flatté*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper presents a quantitative theory using Lindblad master equations to simulate EDMR of silicon vacancy-related spin pairs in SiC, explains recent room-temperature measurements, and predicts new low-temperature and double-quantum sensing capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a quantitative theory for EDMR of silicon vacancy-related spin pairs in SiC that can describe existing measurements and predict new phenomena for quantum sensing.

Method: Steady-state Lindblad master equations are used to model EDMR of V1a and V2a silicon vacancies near the (0/-) charge state transition, paired with an incomplete K-center nitrogen-related complex, incorporating hyperfine, spin structure, and Landé g factor.

Result: The theory reproduces room-temperature EDMR measurements attributed to V1a vacancies with reasonable coherence and transport parameters. At lower temperatures, the shallow level hyperfine structure becomes spectrally resolvable. The EDMR spectrum for V2a spin pairs is also predicted, and two-photon double quantum transitions of the negatively charged vacancy are predicted to enable electrically-read magnetic field sensing.

Conclusion: The quantitative EDMR theory successfully explains existing V1a measurements and predicts new capabilities: low-temperature hyperfine resolution and electrically-detected double quantum transitions for enhanced magnetic field sensing using V2a vacancies.

Abstract: We present a quantitative theory for simulating the electrically detected magnetic resonance (EDMR) of silicon vacancy-related spin pairs in silicon carbide using steady-state Lindblad master equations. In our theory, we consider V1a and V2a deep level silicon vacancies near the (0/-) charge state transition level in proximity to a previously identified nitrogen-related complex, the incomplete K-center, due to the hyperfine, spin structure, and Landé g factor of the shallow state. Our theory describes recent room temperature measurements attributed to V1a silicon vacancies, with reasonable extracted parameters for defect spin coherence times and electrical transport rates. At lower temperatures we predict that the shallow level hyperfine structure may be spectrally resolvable. Finally, we predict the EDMR spectrum of V2a silicon vacancy-related spin pairs and predict that two-photon, double quantum transitions of the silicon vacancy's negative charge state can be electrically read-out for enhanced magnetic field sensing.

</details>


### [31] [First-principles finite-size correction schemes for point defects of Cu$_3$N](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25153)
*Abdul M. Reyes,Sebastian E. Reyes-Lillo,Eduardo Menéndez-Proupin*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study combines PBE+U structural relaxations with HSE hybrid-functional calculations to determine defect formation energies in Cu₃N, assesses and extends finite-size correction schemes (Makov-Payne and Lany-Zunger) using supercells of 256–2048 atoms, and demonstrates that charged localized vacancies follow Makov-Payne scaling while interstitial defects with shallow states require a hydrogenic impurity model, confirming the intrinsic p-type character of Cu₃N.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point defects critically determine semiconductor properties (e.g., conductivity, photoluminescence). Accurate first-principles supercell simulations require reliable finite-size corrections, which are currently inadequate for diverse defect types in Cu₃N.

Method: Combined PBE+U structural relaxations with HSE hybrid-functional calculations. Finite-size trends quantified via Γ-point calculations in supercells of 256, 864, and 2048 atoms. Extended Makov-Payne and Lany-Zunger correction schemes with additional 1/Lⁿ terms, core-level potential alignment, and defect-specific scaling models.

Result: Charged vacancies with strongly localized states follow Makov-Payne-type scaling (1/L + 1/L³); interstitial defects with shallow/weakly localized states are better captured by a hydrogenic impurity model accounting for defect-band dispersion; neutral or weakly localized defects require higher-order polynomial fits in 1/L³ and 1/L⁴. Cu₃N confirmed as intrinsic p-type.

Conclusion: No single finite-size correction is transferable across all defect types; defect-specific correction models are necessary for accurate hybrid-functional energetics of Cu₃N.

Abstract: Point defects play a key role in determining semiconductor properties, such as electrical conductivity and photoluminescence, and often enable functional behavior. Accurate first-principles supercell simulations of point defects require reliable finite-size corrections. In this study, we combine PBE+U structural relaxations with HSE hybrid-functional calculations to determine defect formation energies and thermodynamic transition levels of Cu$_3$N. Finite-size trends are quantified using $Γ$-point calculations in supercells containing 256, 864, and 2048 atoms. We assess and extend the Makov-Payne and Lany-Zunger correction schemes by introducing additional $1/L^n$ terms, together with core-level potential alignment and defect-specific scaling models. Using Cu$_3$N as a case study, we show that charged vacancies with strongly localized defect states are accurately described by the Makov-Payne-type scaling ($1/L + 1/L^{3}$), whereas interstitial defects with shallow or weakly localized electronic character are better captured by a hydrogenic impurity model that accounts for defect-band dispersion. Residual trends for neutral or weakly localized defects are described by higher-order polynomial fits in $1/L^{3}$ and $1/L^{4}$. Hybrid-functional energetics corrected using PBE+U-based finite-size trends confirm the intrinsic $p$-type character of Cu$_3$N under the conditions considered and demonstrate that no single finite-size correction can be transferred across all defect types.

</details>


### [32] [Exploring Multi-Transition-Metal NASICON Frameworks as High-Performance Cathodes for Sodium-Ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25219)
*Santosh Behara,Achinthya Krishna Bheemaguli,Gopalakrishnan Sai Gautam*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using DFT calculations, the authors systematically investigate nine NASICON-type phosphate cathodes (NaₓTM₂(PO₄)₃, TM = Mn, Cr, Fe) for sodium-ion batteries, examining unary, binary, and ternary compositions. They find that multi-TM systems exhibit complex phase behavior, Fe elevates voltage (~4.0 V), mixed-TM frameworks lower Na⁺ migration barriers to 0.3–0.4 eV, and identify NaₓMnFe₀.₅Cr₀.₅(PO₄)₃ as the most promising ternary composition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To identify sustainable, high-performance NASICON cathodes using earth-abundant transition metals and systematically understand how multiple TMs influence the properties of NASICON cathodes for sodium-ion batteries.

Method: Density functional theory (DFT) calculations were employed to investigate nine NASICON compositions with Mn, Cr, and/or Fe, spanning unary, binary, and ternary combinations, analyzing phase behavior, intercalation voltages, electronic structure, and Na⁺ mobility.

Result: Unary systems show well-defined stabilization at intermediate Na contents; binary/ternary systems exhibit complex phase behavior with thermodynamic minima shifting from x=3 to 2. Fe⁴⁺/Fe³⁺ redox elevates average voltages (~4.0 V); Mn and Cr provide intermediate-to-low voltage redox. Mixed-TM frameworks achieve low Na⁺ migration barriers (0.3–0.4 eV). NaₓMnFe₀.₅Cr₀.₅(PO₄)₃ is identified as a promising ternary composition.

Conclusion: The study provides fundamental insights into the interplay between compositional complexity, thermodynamic stability, electronic structure, and ionic transport in NASICONs, offering actionable design principles for utilizing multi-TM NASICONs as high-performance SIB cathodes.

Abstract: The search for sustainable, high-performance cathodes has driven a growing interest in sodium superionic conductor (NASICON)-type phosphates for sodium-ion batteries (SIBs). To identify promising NASICONs containing earth-abundant transition metals (TMs) and to systematically examine the role of multiple TMs in influencing the various properties of NASICON cathodes, we employ density functional theory calculations to investigate nine NASICON compositions containing Mn, Cr, and/or Fe, and spanning unary, binary, and ternary combinations. Our calculations reveal that unary systems, in terms of their Na intercalation phase behavior, exhibit well-defined stabilization at intermediate Na contents ($x$ in Na$_x$TM$_2$(PO$_4$)$_3$), while binary and ternary systems display more complex phase behavior, with some systems showing a shift of thermodynamic minima from $x$ = 3 to 2. Intercalation voltages highlight the dominant role of Fe$^{4+}$/Fe$^{3+}$ redox activity in elevating average voltages ($\sim$4.0 V), while Mn and Cr introduce intermediate-to-low voltage redox activity. Electronic structure data demonstrate non-systematic changes in the band gap, especially in systems containing multiple TMs. Na$^+$ mobility results identify mixed-TM frameworks as favorable, achieving Na$^+$ migration barriers in the 0.3-0.4 eV range. Importantly, we identify Na$_x$MnFe$_{0.5}$Cr$_{0.5}$(PO$_4$)$_3$ to be a promising ternary composition for subsequent experimental validation, offering an optimal intersection of phase stability, voltages, thermodynamic (meta)stability, and Na$^+$ migration barriers. Together, our study provides fundamental insights into the interplay between compositional complexity, thermodynamic stability, electronic structure, and ionic transport in NASICONs, and offers actionable design principles for utilising multi-TM NASICONs as high performance SIB cathodes.

</details>


### [33] [Composition-Driven High-Entropy Alloys with Enhanced Magnetocaloric Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25236)
*Nishant Tiwari,Juan Rafael Gomez Quispe,Noorbasha Bhavani Sai,Saikat Talapatra,Pedro Alves Da Silva Autreto,Varun Chaudhary,Chandra Sekhar Tiwary*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper combines experiments and first-principles spin modeling to engineer magnetocaloric properties in earth-abundant Fe-Ni-Co-Cr-Cu high entropy alloys, demonstrating tunable Curie temperatures and magnetocaloric response by modifying Cu content, with experimental validation of entropy change and cooling power.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop earth-abundant, tunable magnetocaloric materials for solid-state refrigeration by engineering high entropy alloys with controlled operating temperatures through compositional modifications.

Method: Combined experimental synthesis (equiatomic and non-equiatomic Fe-Ni-Co-Cr-Cu HEAs), density functional theory (DFT) simulations to analyze electronic structure, classical Heisenberg model with exchange couplings derived from DFT, and atomistic Monte Carlo simulations to predict Curie temperatures.

Result: Equiatomic HEA has TC=110 K; non-equiatomic Fe34Ni17.7Co24.8Cr15.2Cu8.3 has TC=420 K. Under 1.6 T, entropy change = 1.24 J/kgK, RCP = 92 J/kg. DFT shows Cu reduction enhances spin-polarized 3d states near Fermi level, strengthening ferromagnetic exchange. Increasing Cu monotonically dilutes magnetic sublattice and lowers TC.

Conclusion: Cu content provides a quantitative design guideline for tuning magnetocaloric operating temperatures in transition metal HEAs, enabling tailored solid-state cooling applications.

Abstract: High entropy alloys (HEAs) are promising magnetocaloric materials with tunable operating temperature conditions using compositional modifications. Here, we combine experiments and first principles based spin modelling to engineer magnetocaloric response in single-phase cubic HEAs consisting of earth-abundant elements such as Fe, Ni, Co, Cr, and Cu. An equiatomic (Fe20Ni20Co20Cr20Cu20) and a Fe and Co rich non equiatomic (Fe34Ni17.7Co24.8Cr15.2Cu8.3) show a continuous ferromagnetic to paramagnetic transition with Curie temperature (TC)=110 K and TC=420 K for non equiatomic. Under the 1.6 Tesla magnetic field, the investigated alloy shows the entropy change =1.24 J per kgK with relative cooling power (RCP) =92 J per kg due to a broader effective cooling span. Density functional theory simulations reveal that reducing Cu enhances the spin-polarized Fe, Co, or Ni, 3d weight near fermi level, consistent with stronger ferromagnetic exchange. Exchange couplings mapped onto a classical Heisenberg model and solved by atomistic Monte Carlo to theoretically predict TC of both the investigated alloys. A controlled theoretical Cu sweep in equimolar further confirms that increasing Cu monotonically dilutes the magnetic sublattice and lowers TC, providing a quantitative design guideline to tune magnetocaloric operating temperatures in transition metal HEAs.

</details>


### [34] [Anomalous Subsurface Vacancy Stabilization Dictated by Geometry-Electronic Decoupling on Metal Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25351)
*Yiming Tan,Pai Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper discovers an anomalous thermodynamic inversion on close-packed metal surfaces (Ir, Pt, Au, Be, Zn, Cd) where subsurface vacancies are more stable than surface ones, driven by a "geometry-electronic decoupling" mechanism in FCC metals, enabling self-healing on Pt(111) and explaining the critical defect threshold for Au(100) reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge conventional coordination-dependent broken-bond models by identifying and explaining the anomalous thermodynamic inversion where subsurface vacancies are more stable than surface ones on close-packed metal surfaces, and to understand its implications for surface integrity and catalytic behavior.

Method: High-throughput DFT calculations combined with machine learning force fields to study vacancy formation energetics on close-packed FCC (Ir, Pt, Au) and HCP (Be, Zn, Cd) metal surfaces.

Result: Subsurface vacancies are intrinsically more stable than surface ones on these close-packed surfaces. The physical origin is decoupled between FCC and HCP systems. For FCC metals, pronounced surface relaxation and real-space electronic localization induce directional covalent-like intralayer bonding ("geometry-electronic decoupling"), enabling a dynamic self-healing mechanism on Pt(111) and explaining the ~8% defect threshold for lifting Au(100) surface reconstruction.

Conclusion: This work challenges classical scalar defect models and provides a new paradigm for engineering catalyst surface integrity by leveraging unconventional thermodynamic vacancy hierarchies.

Abstract: Vacancy formation energetics fundamentally govern the structural integrity and catalytic behavior of metal surfaces. Contrary to conventional coordination-dependent broken-bond models, we identify an anomalous thermodynamic inversion on close-packed surfaces across Ir, Pt, Au with face-centered cubic (FCC) lattice and Be, Zn, Cd with hexagonal close-packed (HCP) lattice, where subsurface vacancies are intrinsically more stable than surface ones. Using high-throughput DFT calculations and machine learning force fields, we demonstrate that the physical origins of this anomaly are fundamentally decoupled between the two crystal systems. For the FCC trio, pronounced surface relaxation and a profound real-space electronic localization induce directional, covalent-like intralayer bonding, materializing a "geometry-electronic decoupling" mechanism. Crucially, this unconventional thermodynamic hierarchy enables a dynamic "self-healing" mechanism on Pt(111) that preserves an intact topmost layer and prevents catalytic degradation during hydrogen evolution and oxygen reduction reactions. It also successfully decoding the critical defect threshold (~8%) for lifting the Au(100) surface reconstruction. Our work challenges classical scalar defect models and provides a fresh paradigm for engineering catalyst surface integrity.

</details>


### [35] [Effects of Band Symmetry on Spin-Dependent Transport in Noncollinear Antiferromagnetic Tunnel Junctions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25369)
*Mohamed Elekhtiar,Ding-Fu Shao,Evgeny Y. Tsymbal*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using first-principles calculations, the authors show that band symmetry, not just spin polarization, critically controls tunneling magnetoresistance in Mn3NiN/LaAlO3/Mn3NiN antiferromagnetic tunnel junctions, achieving TMR >2000%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To elucidate the role of band symmetry in governing spin-dependent transport in antiferromagnetic tunnel junctions (AFMTJs), which are promising for ultrafast and field-robust spintronic devices.

Method: First-principles density-functional theory combined with quantum-transport calculations on Mn3NiN/LaAlO3/Mn3NiN (001) junctions based on the noncollinear Γ₄₉ antiferromagnetic phase of Mn₃NiN.

Result: Orbital-symmetry selection rules suppress interband transmission in the parallel configuration, while the antiparallel configuration enables symmetry-compatible interband tunneling along diagonal directions of the 2D Brillouin zone, enhancing antiparallel conductance and reducing TMR relative to spin-polarization-only predictions. TMR still exceeds 2000%.

Conclusion: Band-symmetry filtering is an essential ingredient of spin-dependent tunneling in AFMTJs, controlling the attainable magnitude of TMR.

Abstract: Antiferromagnetic tunnel junctions (AFMTJs) can exhibit large tunneling magnetoresistance (TMR), making them promising candidates for ultrafast and field-robust spintronic devices. Here, we elucidate the role of band symmetry in governing spin-dependent transport in AFMTJs. Using first-principles density-functional theory combined with quantum-transport calculations, we investigate Mn3NiN/LaAlO3/Mn3NiN (001) junctions based on the noncollinear $Γ_{4g}$ antiferromagnetic phase of Mn3NiN. Although Mn3NiN exhibits a large momentum-dependent spin polarization due to broken $PT$ symmetry, we show that the tunneling conductance is critically controlled by band symmetry of the electrode Bloch states and their symmetry-selective coupling to evanescent states in the LaAlO3 barrier. Orbital-symmetry selection rules suppress interband transmission in the parallel configuration, whereas the antiparallel configuration enables symmetry-compatible interband tunneling along the diagonal directions of the two-dimensional Brillouin zone. These additional transmission channels enhance the antiparallel conductance and reduce the TMR relative to predictions based solely on spin polarization. Nevertheless, the TMR remains exceptionally large, exceeding 2000%, while band symmetry controls the attainable magnitude of TMR in AFMTJs. Our results establish band-symmetry filtering as an essential ingredient of spin-dependent tunneling in AFMTJs.

</details>


### [36] [Three-Dimensional Atomic-Scale Structural Transformation in a SrTiO3 Grain Boundary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25410)
*Xiaoyue Gao,Jiake Wei,Bo Han,Junpin Luo,Ruilin Mao,Xiaowen Zhang,Xiaomei Li,Ryo Ishikawa,Bin Feng,Naoya Shibata,Yuichi Ikuhara,Peng Gao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Using multislice electron ptychography, the 3D atomic structure of a Σ13(510)/[001] tilt GB in SrTiO₃ is resolved, revealing a hidden transition from symmetric (STR1) to asymmetric (STR2) configurations with distinct chemical and vacancy distributions, mediated by atomic shuffling and collective shear, establishing a direct link between 3D atomic structure, local chemistry, and lattice order parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grain boundaries in complex oxides critically govern functional properties, yet their 3D atomic structures remain poorly understood due to projection limitations of conventional (S)TEM.

Method: Multislice electron ptychography was used to resolve the 3D atomic structure of a Σ13(510)/[001] tilt GB in SrTiO₃, enabling simultaneous visualization of both cation and oxygen columns with depth resolution.

Result: Depth-resolved reconstruction uncovered a transition from symmetric (STR1) to asymmetric (STR2) GB configurations hidden in conventional imaging; these configurations exhibit distinct local chemical and vacancy distributions, and the transformation involves local atomic shuffling at the GB core and collective shear displacement in adjoining grains mediated by step and dislocation character.

Conclusion: The study establishes a direct link among 3D atomic structure, local chemical composition, and lattice order parameters at GBs, highlighting the critical importance of depth-resolved characterization for understanding and engineering GB-mediated functionalities in complex oxides.

Abstract: Grain boundaries (GBs) in complex oxides play critical roles in governing their functional properties, which are intrinsically linked to their three-dimensional (3D) atomic configurations and local chemical environments that can deviate markedly from those of the bulk. However, the 3D atomic structures of GBs remain poorly understood due to the projection limitations of conventional (S)TEM. Here, using multislice electron ptychography, we resolve the 3D atomic structure of a Σ13(510)/[001] tilt GB in SrTiO3 with simultaneous visualization of both cation and oxygen columns. Depth-resolved reconstruction reveals pronounced structural inhomogeneity along the GB, uncovering a transition from the canonical symmetric configuration (STR1) to an asymmetric configuration (STR2) that is hidden in conventional projection imaging. Quantitative analysis of atomic-column intensities demonstrates that these two GB configurations possess distinct local chemical and vacancy distributions. By further mapping the atomic displacement fields, we reveal that the transformation between STR1 and STR2 proceeds via local atomic shuffling at the GB core and collective shear displacement in the adjoining grains, mediated by the step and dislocation character of the junction, respectively. Moreover, analysis of oxygen octahedral rotations reveals a strong dependence on the local atomic structure with pronounced asymmetry around the STR2 region. These findings establish a direct link among the 3D atomic structure, local chemical composition, and lattice order parameters at the GB, underscoring the critical importance of depth-resolved characterization in understanding and engineering GB-mediated functionalities in complex oxides.

</details>


### [37] [Experimental and computational diffusion analysis in Ni-X binary and Ni-Al-X (X = Cr, Mo, Ta, W, Re) ternary systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25425)
*Ankur Srivastava,Suman Sadhu,Satyam Kumar,Ujjval Bansal,Raju Ravi,Saswata Bhattacharyya,Gopalakrishnan Sai Gautam,Aloke Paul*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper presents a comprehensive diffusion analysis of binary Ni-X and ternary Ni-Al-X (X = Cr, Mo, Ta, W, Re) systems, using first-principles calculations, experimental activation energies, and a PINN optimization method to extract composition-dependent diffusion coefficients, highlighting the critical role of cross-diffusion effects and constraint-enhanced numerical methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand diffusion behavior in Ni-Al-X systems crucial for microstructural evolution and phase stability in Ni-Al-based superalloys, particularly focusing on how Al presence alters X diffusion coefficients and how diffusional interactions differ across alloy systems.

Method: Combines first-principles calculations with experimentally derived activation energies for binary systems; uses intersecting diffusion profiles and a single-profile method (for Ni-Al-Re) to estimate ternary diffusion coefficients; applies a PINN optimization method with experimental equality constraints to extract composition-dependent diffusion coefficients across the full composition range.

Result: Main interdiffusion coefficients of X in ternary systems are comparable to binary counterparts with similar activation energies; cross-diffusion coefficients significantly influence fluxes, enhancing or reducing diffusion lengths based on relative diffusion directions; PINN optimization requires experimentally estimated diffusion coefficients as equality constraints for reliability.

Conclusion: Establishes a robust framework for diffusion studies in Ni-Al-X systems, emphasizing the necessity of accounting for cross-diffusion effects and using constraint-enhanced numerical methods like PINN for accurate composition-dependent diffusion coefficient extraction.

Abstract: An extensive diffusion analysis is presented for binary Ni-X and ternary Ni-Al-X (X = Cr, Mo, Ta, W, Re) systems, which play a crucial role in microstructural evolution and phase stability in Ni-Al-based superalloys. Specifically, we highlight changes in the diffusion coefficients of X in the presence of Al and compare diffusional interactions across systems considered. First-principles calculations, combined with activation energies derived from temperature-dependent experiments, reveal consistent trends in Ni-X systems, with variations in activation energies largely attributed to differences in migration energies. In ternary systems, diffusion coefficients estimated from intersecting diffusion profiles show that the main interdiffusion coefficient of X is comparable to its binary counterpart, with similar activation energies. However, cross-diffusion coefficients are shown to significantly influence fluxes, either enhancing or reducing diffusion lengths depending on the relative directions of diffusing elements. For Ni-Al-Re, a single-profile method is employed to overcome uncertainties in estimating composition gradients at the near-end-member intersecting composition. The diffusion coefficients obtained correlate well with the nature of diffusion paths when represented on Gibbs triangles. To extend these findings, a physics-informed neural network (PINN) optimization method is applied to extract composition-dependent diffusion coefficients across the full composition range. The analysis demonstrates the necessity of incorporating experimentally estimated diffusion coefficients as equality constraints, without which optimization reliability is compromised. Overall, the results establish a robust framework for diffusion studies in Ni-Al-X systems, highlighting the critical role of cross-diffusion effects and constraint-enhanced numerical methods.

</details>


### [38] [Disentangling the contributions of individual cations to magnetic order in a spinel high entropy oxide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25432)
*Mario Ulises González-Rivas,Chun-Fu Chang,Martin Bluschke,Jessica Freese,Peter Bencok,Ronny Sutarto,Teak D. Boyko,Robert J. Green,George A. Sawatzky,Liu Hao Tjeng,Alannah M. Hallas*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses element-specific XMCD to reveal how different cations in ferrimagnetic spinel high-entropy oxides contribute to magnetic ordering, showing that magnetic moment growth rates depend on 3d crystal field filling and exchange pathways, with non-magnetic substitution reducing frustration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how individual chemical constituents in high-entropy oxides (HEOs) contribute to the emergence of long-range magnetic order despite extreme chemical disorder, as this knowledge is essential for tailoring HEO magnetism.

Method: Element-specific magnetometry via X-ray magnetic circular dichroism (XMCD) on two ferrimagnetic spinel-structured HEOs: (Cr,Mn,Fe,Co,Ni)₃O₄ and (Cr,Mn,Fe,Co,Ni)₂.₄Ga₀.₆O₄.

Result: Magnetic transition occurs simultaneously for all species, but magnetic moment growth rates vary strongly by cation. Tetrahedral Fe³⁺ and octahedral Ni²⁺ harden rapidly via dominant A-B sublattice exchange, while octahedral Fe³⁺ and Cr³⁺ exhibit sluggish transitions due to frustration. Non-magnetic Ga substitution suppresses these differences by relieving frustration.

Conclusion: Tailoring magnetism in HEO spinels requires detailed understanding of site selectivities and exchange pathways; the varying 3d crystal field level fillings of magnetic cations determine their participation in different exchange pathways.

Abstract: High entropy oxides (HEOs) can possess long-range ordered magnetic states despite their extreme chemical disorder. Very little is known about how the different chemical constituents in HEOs contribute to the emergence of these magnetic states. In this work, we leverage element-specific magnetometry attained via x-ray magnetic circular dichroism (XMCD) to understand how magnetic order is driven in two ferrimagnetic spinel-structured HEOs with compositions (Cr,Mn,Fe,Co,Ni)$_3$O$_4$ and (Cr,Mn,Fe,Co,Ni)$_{2.4}$Ga$_{0.6}$O$_4$. We find that while the magnetic transition is simultaneous for all chemical species, the rate at which their magnetic moments grow is strongly cation dependent. This behavior is explained by the varying $\textit{3d}$ crystal field level fillings of the magnetic cations, which in turn determine their ability to participate in the different magnetic exchange pathways available in the spinel structure. Dominant $A$-$B$ sublattice exchange enables some species to harden rapidly ($\textit{e.g.}$ tetrahedral Fe$^{3+}$ and octahedral Ni$^{2+}$) while others exhibit a sluggish transition due to frustration from competing interactions ($\textit{e.g.}$ octahedral Fe$^{3+}$ and Cr$^{3+}$). Non-magnetic substitution suppresses these differences, introducing broken magnetic linkages that relieve frustration. Tailoring the magnetism of HEO spinels therefore requires detailed knowledge of both their site selectivities and their exchange pathways.

</details>


### [39] [Local Structural Signatures of Shear Bands in Metallic Glasses via Electron Nanodiffraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25433)
*Huyen T. Pham,Daniel East,Chunguang Tang,Matteo Baggioli,Alessio Zaccone,Timothy C. Petersen,Amelia C. Y. Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses cryogenic ion polishing and electron nano-diffraction to preserve and probe the intrinsic atomic structure of shear bands in metallic glasses, revealing nanoscale stripe-like regions of localized strain and reduced centrosymmetry that provide direct evidence of coordinated nanoscale structural transformations underlying plastic deformation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structural changes in deformed glasses are subtle and difficult to quantify with conventional imaging/diffraction, and TEM sample preparation via energetic ions introduces artefacts that are hard to detect in disordered materials. The authors aim to overcome these challenges to directly observe the intrinsic deformation microstructure.

Method: Inverted cross-sectional TEM lamellae of shear bands (formed during bending) were prepared using cryogenic ion polishing to minimize artefacts. Local nanoscale structure was probed using sensitive new parameters derived from electron nano-diffraction, mapping local centrosymmetry and strain.

Result: Nanoscale, stripe-like regions oriented at 45° to the applied strain were found beneath surface shear steps. These regions exhibit a high density of local atomic structures transformed to configurations with reduced centrosymmetry and increased magnitudes of shear and normal strain.

Conclusion: Plastic deformation in metallic glasses arises from coordinated nanoscale structural transformations, providing direct experimental insight into a long-standing problem in the field.

Abstract: Structural changes in a glass due to deformation are subtle and difficult to quantify using conventional imaging and diffraction techniques. Additionally, transmission electron microscopy (TEM) sample preparation using energetic ions often causes structural modifications that are challenging to detect in disordered materials. By preparing inverted cross-sectional transmission electron microscopy lamellae of shear bands formed during bending, and employing cryogenic ion polishing to minimize preparation artefacts, we preserve the intrinsic atomic structure. Using sensitive, new parameters derived from electron nano-diffraction, we directly probe the local nano-scale structure in the plastic zone beneath surface shear steps in metallic glasses. Mapping of local centrosymmetry and strain reveals nanoscale, stripe-like regions oriented at 45 degree to the applied strain where strain has localized. These regions exhibit a high density of local atomic structures that have transformed to configurations with reduced centrosymmetry and increased magnitudes of shear and normal strain. Our results demonstrate that plastic deformation in metallic glasses arises from coordinated nanoscale structural transformations, providing direct experimental insight into a long-standing problem.

</details>


### [40] [Spin layer groups and their corepresentations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25484)
*Zeying Zhang,Gui-Bin Liu,Mu Tian,Run-Wu Zhang,Zhi-Ming Yu,Yugui Yao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper systematically classifies inequivalent spin layer groups and derives their irreducible corepresentations, establishing a foundational framework for studying symmetry-protected properties and novel quantum states in low-dimensional magnetic materials.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct application of 3D symmetry groups to 2D systems is often inadequate due to anisotropic axes and dimensional reduction, necessitating a dedicated classification for spin layer groups.

Method: Systematic classification of inequivalent spin layer groups and analytical derivation of their irreducible corepresentations.

Result: Complete classification of inequivalent spin layer groups and their corresponding irreducible corepresentations.

Conclusion: The classification provides a foundational framework for investigating symmetry-protected properties and novel quantum states in low-dimensional magnetic materials.

Abstract: Spin layer groups are the crystallographic symmetry groups with a periodic plane, and their symmetry operations are inherited from three-dimensional (3D) spin space groups. However, the direct application of 3D symmetry groups to two-dimensional systems is often inadequate due to anisotropic axes and dimensional reduction. In this work, we systematically classify inequivalent spin layer groups and analytically derive their irreducible corepresentations. This classification establishes a foundational framework for investigating symmetry-protected properties and novel quantum states in low-dimensional magnetic materials.

</details>


### [41] [Transition from Homogeneous to Domain-Wall-Mediated Polarization Switching in BaTiO3: A Machine-Learning Molecular Dynamics Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25485)
*Po-Yen Chen,Teruyasu Mizoguchi*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This study uses machine-learning molecular dynamics to show that polarization switching in BaTiO3 transitions from homogeneous to domain-wall-mediated as supercell size increases, with a >50% coercive field increase, driven by size-dependent polarization fluctuations quantified via Shannon entropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The conditions determining which polarization switching pathway (homogeneous vs. domain-wall-mediated) is realized in ferroelectric BaTiO3 remain poorly understood.

Method: Machine-learning potential-based molecular dynamics with the MACEField model, systematically varying supercell size and analyzing Shannon entropy of polarization fluctuations.

Result: A clear transition from homogeneous to domain-wall-mediated switching with increasing supercell size, accompanied by >50% coercive field increase; switching pathway also depends on supercell geometry and relative orientation of applied stress and electric field.

Conclusion: Homogeneous and domain-wall-mediated switching are distinct physical regimes in BaTiO3; atomistic simulations must account for system size to correctly capture the operative switching mechanism.

Abstract: Polarization switching in ferroelectric BaTiO3 can proceed through fundamentally different mechanisms - yet the conditions that determine which pathway is realized remain poorly understood. Using machine-learning potential-based molecular dynamics with the MACEField model, we systematically vary supercell size to reveal a clear transition from homogeneous polarization switching to domain-wall-mediated switching, accompanied by a coercive field increase of over 50%. Shannon entropy analysis demonstrates that this transition is driven by size-dependent polarization fluctuations that promote 180 degree domain-wall nucleation - establishing a direct, quantitative link between local configurational disorder and macroscopic switching behavior. Furthermore, the switching pathway and hysteresis response are shown to depend critically on supercell geometry and the relative orientation of applied stress and electric field. These findings reveal that homogeneous and domain-wall-mediated switching are distinct physical regimes in BaTiO3, and that atomistic simulations must account for system size to correctly capture the operative switching mechanism.

</details>
