*Håkon W. Ånes,Phillip Crout,Lars Andreas Lervik,Ole Natlandsmyr,Tina Bergh,Jarle Hjelen,Antonius T. J. van Helvoort,Knut Marthinsen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: kikuchipy is an open-source Python toolbox for analyzing electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD) patterns, supporting Hough and dictionary indexing, orientation/projection center refinement, validation, and visualization, demonstrated on three metallurgical applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a flexible, open-source, Python-based toolbox for EBSD pattern analysis that improves indexing workflows by leveraging the Python ecosystem, enables validation independent of indexing, and is freely available for the electron microscopy community.

Method: The software implements Hough indexing and dictionary indexing, orientation and projection center refinement, supports all major vendor file formats, uses Python scientific libraries for flexible workflow iteration, and validates results via indexing-independent maps and visual comparison of experimental vs. simulated patterns.

Result: Demonstrated on three examples: orientation relationship analysis in super duplex stainless steel, phase differentiation of Al and Si in cast modified Al-Si alloy, and phase differentiation of Al6Mn vs. alpha-AlMnSi particles in an Al-Mn alloy.

Conclusion: kikuchipy serves as an open community resource for EBSD analysis, enabling flexible, reproducible workflows and allowing integration into other software.

Abstract: We present kikuchipy, an open-source toolbox for analysis of electron backscatter diffraction patterns, written in Python. The software is capable of both Hough and dictionary indexing and orientation and/or projection center refinement of patterns stored in file formats from all major vendors. Indexing results can be validated using maps independent of indexing and by visually comparing experimental and simulated patterns. By leveraging scientific packages in the Python ecosystem, emphasis is put on making the indexing workflow flexible and improve results through fast iteration. The software's capabilities are demonstrated on three application examples: analysis of orientation relationships in a super duplex stainless steel, phase differentiation of aluminium and silicon in a cast modified Al-Si alloy, and phase differentiation of particles in an Al-Mn alloy as Al6Mn or alpha-AlMnSi. The diffraction patterns and analysis workflows are made publicly available. kikuchipy was created and is developed as a resource for the electron microscopy community, allowing anyone to improve the software or include it into their own analysis workflows or softwares.

</details>


### [43] [Dealloying by peritectic melting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25834)
*Mingwang Zhong,Alain Karma*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Phase-field simulations reveal that bicontinuous structures in peritectic melting of Ti-Ag arise from a 3D morphological instability of liquid film migration, leading to branched seaweed/dendritic structures whose side branches coalesce into handles, creating high-genus topology. Sharp-interface theory predicts constant solidification-front velocity and initial ligament width, with subsequent t^{1/3} coarsening matching experimental observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To elucidate the unclear mechanism by which bicontinuous structures form during peritectic melting of Ti-Ag, which has been observed experimentally but not explained theoretically.

Method: Phase-field simulations of peritectic melting in Ti-Ag, combined with sharp-interface theory to predict solidification dynamics.

Result: Bicontinuous structures result from a 3D morphological instability of liquid film migration, where Ti-rich solid grows through Ag-rich liquid film forming branched seaweed/dendritic structures; side branches coalesce into handles, generating high-genus topology. Solidification-front velocity and initial ligament width are time-constant, unlike liquid metal dealloying, with t^{1/3} coarsening matching experimental final ligament width.

Conclusion: The mechanism of bicontinuous structure formation in peritectic melting is identified as a 3D morphological instability of liquid film migration, producing high-genus topology via branch coalescence, with dynamics distinct from liquid metal dealloying.

Abstract: Peritectic melting of Ti--Ag has been shown experimentally to form bicontinuous structures, but the mechanism remains unclear. Here we use phase-field simulations to show that these structures arise from a morphological instability of liquid film migration in three dimensions: a Ti-rich solid growing through an Ag-rich liquid film develops a branched seaweed or dendritic structure whose side branches coalesce to form handles, generating a high-genus bicontinuous topology. A sharp-interface theory predicts a solidification-front velocity and an initial ligament width that are constant in time, in contrast to liquid metal dealloying; subsequent $t^{1/3}$ coarsening reproduces the experimentally observed final ligament width.

</details>


### [44] [Benchmarking Transparent Conductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25904)
*Amit Cohen,Lior Kornblum*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper introduces an application-rooted benchmarking framework for transparent conducting oxides (TCOs) that evaluates materials at fixed, device-relevant sheet resistance ($R_S$), producing a metric ($T_{app}(R_S)$) that quantifies achievable optical transparency at required $R_S$, thereby bridging the gap between materials design and device integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing figures of merit for TCOs are disconnected from the actual electrical requirements of real devices, leading to material design guided by conditions that can be impractical for applications.

Method: A benchmarking framework is introduced where transparent conductors are evaluated at fixed, application-relevant sheet resistance ($R_S$), producing a new metric $T_{app}(R_S)$ that asks: What optical transparency can be obtained at the sheet resistance required by a given application?

Result: The framework provides a directly interpretable measure of performance, enabling benchmarking of materials in terms of absolute transparency gains at a specified $R_S$. Applied to conventional and emerging TCOs, it defines the sheet-resistance landscape relevant to each application and maps how different materials perform.

Conclusion: This approach establishes a general strategy for evaluating materials under fixed operational constraints, bridging the gap between materials design and device integration, and provides an application-rooted guide to TCO material development and selection.

Abstract: Transparent conducting oxides (TCOs) are central to optoelectronic technologies, yet their design is often guided by popular figures of merit that are disconnected from the electrical requirement of actual devices. As a result, widely used metrics guide material design under conditions that can be impractical for devices. Here, we introduce a benchmarking framework to guide TCO development, in which transparent conductors are evaluated at fixed, application-relevant sheet resistance $(R_S)$. The resulting metric, $T_{app}(R_S)$, anchors comparison to device requirements, asking instead: What optical transparency can be obtained at the sheet resistance required by a given application? This approach provides a directly interpretable measure of performance, enabling materials to be benchmarked in terms of absolute transparency gains at a specified $R_S$. Applied to representative conventional and emerging TCOs, the framework defines the sheet-resistance landscape relevant to each application and maps how different materials perform within it. In doing so, it provides an application-rooted guide to material development and selection. More broadly, this approach establishes a general strategy for evaluating materials under fixed operational constraints, bridging the gap between materials design and device integration.

</details>


### [45] [Anomalous Hall Effect in Silicon-Compatible Altermagnetic alpha-MnTe Thin Films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25953)
*Rajib Sarkar,Subhransu Kumar Negi,Arindom Das,Arijit Mandal,Pankaj Bhardwaj,Sohini Guin,Aryaman Das,Naresh Shyaga,Laxmipriya Nanda,B. R. K. Nanda,Dhavala Suri*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper reports the direct epitaxial growth of alpha-MnTe thin films on Si(111) substrates and demonstrates a robust anomalous Hall effect in this silicon-compatible altermagnetic system, establishing a pathway for integrating Berry-phase-driven spintronic functionalities with mainstream semiconductor technology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To integrate spin-dependent functionality with mainstream silicon-based semiconductor technology by finding materials compatible with silicon platforms, overcoming the incompatibility of most candidate spintronic materials with Si-based platforms.

Method: Direct epitaxial integration of alpha-MnTe thin films on Si(111) via molecular beam epitaxy (MBE), combined with high-resolution structural and spectroscopic characterization, magnetotransport measurements, and first-principles calculations to analyze the anomalous Hall effect and Berry curvature.

Result: Phase-pure, epitaxial growth of hexagonal NiAs-type symmetry MnTe on Si(111); pronounced hysteretic anomalous Hall effect despite zero net magnetization; correlated hysteresis in transverse and longitudinal channels with systematic temperature evolution; uncompensated Berry curvature from spin-split band structure consistent with altermagnetic symmetry.

Conclusion: MnTe/Si(111) serves as a silicon-compatible altermagnetic platform, enabling the embedding of Berry-phase-driven functionalities into scalable semiconductor device architectures for modern spintronics.

Abstract: Integrating spin-dependent functionality with mainstream semiconductor technology is a central goal of modern spintronics, yet most candidate materials remain incompatible with silicon-based platforms. Here, we report the direct epitaxial integration of alpha-MnTe thin films on Si(111) via molecular beam epitaxy and demonstrate a robust anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in this silicon-compatible altermagnetic system. Despite the absence of net magnetization, the films exhibit a pronounced hysteretic Hall response, providing clear evidence of finite Berry curvature generated by symmetry breaking in the thin-film geometry. High resolution structural and spectroscopic characterization confirms phase-pure, epitaxial growth with hexagonal NiAs-type symmetry, while magnetotransport measurements reveal correlated hysteresis in both transverse and longitudinal channels with systematic temperature evolution. First-principles calculations reveal substantial uncompensated Berry curvature arising from the spin-split band structure consistent with altermagnetic symmetry and the origin of the observed Hall response. These results establish MnTe/Si(111) as a silicon-compatible altermagnetic platform and chart a concrete pathway for embedding Berry-phase-driven functionalities into scalable semiconductor device architectures.

</details>


### [46] [Light-Induced Transient Polarization Reversal in Rhombohedrally Stacked Bilayer Transition-Metal Dichalcogenides via an Electronic Mechanism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25982)
*Xiangzhou Zhu,Stefano Mocatti,Matteo Calandra*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates ultrafast (200 fs) electronic reversal of out-of-plane polarization in rhombohedral TMD bilayers via photoexcitation, without interlayer sliding, enabling sub-ps optical memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing light-induced sliding ferroelectricity in 2D materials requires tens of ps (shear modes) and high fluences that risk sample damage. The goal is to find a faster, gentler mechanism for polarization switching.

Method: Constrained density functional theory (cDFT) and many-body real-time simulations are used to study photoexcited rhombohedrally stacked transition-metal dichalcogenide (TMD) bilayers.

Result: Total out-of-plane polarization reverses sign within 200 fs at moderate fluences (~50× faster than shear-mode period), driven by localized dipole rearrangement around tungsten sites, without interlayer sliding.

Conclusion: A novel general mechanism for electronic control of low-dimensional ferroelectrics is established, applicable to all polar multilayers with type II band alignment, with implications for ultrahigh-speed volatile optical memory.

Abstract: Light-induced sliding ferroelectricity in two-dimensional van der Waals materials enables polarization control via relative layer motion. However, polarization switching occurs on the time scale of shear modes (tens of ps) and requires very large fluences, potentially damaging the samples. Here, using constrained density functional theory and many-body real-time simulations, we demonstrate an ultrafast electronic reversal of the total out-of-plane polarization sign in the photoexcited state, without requiring interlayer sliding, in rhombohedrally stacked transition-metal dichalcogenide bilayers. The polarization changes sign relative to its initial ground-state value at moderate fluences and within 200 fs, about 50 times faster than the typical shear-mode period. The ultrafast switching is driven by a rearrangement of localized dipoles around the tungsten sites. We establish a novel general mechanism for electronic control of low-dimensional ferroelectrics common to all polar multilayers having type II band alignment. Our work has direct implications for ultrahigh-speed volatile optical memory operating on sub-ps time scales.

</details>


### [47] [Nuclear-Electron Hyperfine Coupling of the Shallow States Associated with Vacancies in Gallium Nitride](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26053)
*Joseph Sink,Michael E. Flatté*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper uses multiband real space Green's functions with open-boundary conditions to solve the Dyson equation for single nitrogen and gallium vacancies in GaN, computing local density of states and hyperfine field contributions relevant to electrically-detected magnetic resonance signals for defect identification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable identification of single nitrogen and gallium vacancies in GaN devices via electrically-detected magnetic resonance signals by computing their electronic structure and hyperfine properties.

Method: Multiband real space Green's functions computed using open-boundary conditions for clean GaN are used to exactly solve the potential-scattering Dyson equation for vacancy electronic structures.

Result: The local density of states and both Fermi contact and anisotropic contributions to the hyperfine field in the vicinity of the defect are computed.

Conclusion: The computed quantities directly affect electrically-detected magnetic resonance signals and can be used to identify these defects when present in GaN devices.

Abstract: We use multiband real space Green's functions computed using open-boundary conditions for clean GaN to exactly solve the potential-scattering Dyson equation to obtain the electronic structure of single nitrogen and gallium vacancies. From these vacancy solutions, we compute the local density of states as well as the Fermi contact and anisotropic contributions to the hyperfine field in the vicinity of the defect. These quantities directly affect electrically-detected magnetic resonance signals, which can be used to identify these defects when present in GaN devices.

</details>


### [48] [Magneto-optic phonon resonances in magnetic topological EuCd2As2 via helical Raman spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26073)
*Jin Ho Kang,Liangbo Liang,Ioannis Petrides,Kai-Chi Chang,Subhajit Roychowdhury,Chandra Shekhar,Claudia Felser,Prineha Narang,Chee Wei Wong*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The authors study Raman spectroscopy of FM-EuCd2As2 and AFM-EuCd2As2 crystals. In FM-EuCd2As2 (Tc=26 K), they observe spin-phonon coupling below Tc, in-plane magneto-anisotropy, and a 60% degree of circular polarization (DCP) for the 12.5 meV peak at 4.2 K. AFM-EuCd2As2 (TN=9 K) shows negligible spin-phonon coupling and near-zero DCP due to vanishing net magnetization. The work advances understanding of phonon dynamics and topology-magnetism interplay in Weyl semimetals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the phonon dynamics, spin-phonon coupling, and magneto-anisotropy in FM-EuCd2As2 (a minimal Weyl semimetal candidate) using cryogenic Raman spectroscopy, and compare with AFM-EuCd2As2 to elucidate the interplay between topology and magnetism.

Method: Cryogenic low-frequency Raman spectroscopy with circular and linear polarization configurations, supported by density functional theory (DFT) calculations, on FM-EuCd2As2 and AFM-EuCd2As2 single crystals below their respective magnetic transition temperatures.

Result: Anomalous Raman intensity enhancements below Tc attributed to spin-phonon coupling; A-mode peak distinction via magneto-helical Raman spectroscopy; DCP of 12.5 meV peak reaches 60% at 4.2 K in FM phase; AFM-EuCd2As2 shows negligible spin-phonon coupling and DCP.

Conclusion: The results deepen understanding of phonon dynamics and topology-magnetism interplay in FM-EuCd2As2, laying groundwork for applications in thermoelectrics, phononic devices, and topological quantum computing.

Abstract: EuCd2As2 materials have two magnetic ordering states: antiferromagnetic (AFM) and ferromagnetic (FM) when their chemical tunability is utilized. While AFM-EuCd2As2 has a nonzero magnetoelectric response due to its symmetry breaking with spin configuration, FM-EuCd2As2 is an ideal candidate for studies of Weyl physics because of its minimum number of Weyl points with opposite chirality. In this article, we examine cryogenic low-frequency Raman spectroscopy of phonon modes in FM-EuCd2As2 crystals using circular polarization configurations, with support from density functional theory calculations, and investigate in-plane magneto-anisotropy by linear polarization configuration below the Curie temperature (Tc = 26 K). We attribute the anomalous enhancements in Raman intensities below the Curie temperature are due to spin-phonon coupling. Furthermore, we see that A-mode peaks can be distinguished by magneto-helical Raman spectroscopy through the magneto-optic effect and that the degree of circular polarization (DCP) of 12.5 meV peak reaches 60% at 4.2 K and becomes saturated. We also examine AFM-EuCd2As2 below Néel temperature (TN = 9 K) to compare with FM-EuCd2As2, but we hardly observe spin-phonon coupling and find negligible DCP values due to almost zero net magnetization. Our results contribute to the understanding of the phonon dynamics and the interplay between topology and magnetism in FM-EuCd2As2, through helical light and external magnetic fields. This lays the foundation for utilizing state-of-the-art Weyl systems for applications in thermoelectrics, phononic devices, and topological quantum computing.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [49] [Supersymmetry Without Time-Reversal Invariance in Model A: A FRG perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24029)
*Sankarshan Sahu,Bertrand Delamotte,Adam Rançon,Matthieu Tissier*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Supersymmetry alone is insufficient to guarantee time-reversal invariance (TRI) in Model A dynamics; an additional condition is required. The authors construct a supersymmetric but TRI-violating model, show TRI emerges as an effective large-scale symmetry perturbatively, prove via FRG that the dynamical effective action contains the equilibrium effective action with identical RG flow, and recover the Ising model magnetization distribution within the Model A framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge the common belief that supersymmetry alone ensures relaxation to a stationary state satisfying time-reversal invariance (TRI) in Model A dynamics, and to identify the precise conditions required for TRI.

Method: Explicit construction of a supersymmetric but TRI-violating model; perturbative analysis of TRI as an emergent large-scale symmetry; functional renormalization group (FRG) analysis of the dynamical effective action; extension to recover the Ising model magnetization distribution.

Result: Supersymmetry is not sufficient for TRI; an additional condition is needed. The dynamical effective action contains the derivative of the equilibrium effective action, whose RG flow matches the equilibrium theory order by order in the derivative expansion. The Ising model's magnetization probability distribution is recovered within Model A.

Conclusion: TRI requires more than supersymmetry in Model A dynamics, though it emerges as an effective large-scale symmetry perturbatively. The FRG framework connects dynamical and equilibrium effective actions, and the approach can be extended to the Ising model.

Abstract: We show that, contrary to common belief, supersymmetry alone is not sufficient in Model A dynamics to ensure relaxation toward a stationary state satisfying time-reversal invariance (TRI). An additional condition on top of supersymmetry is required for TRI, which we analyze in detail. We explicitly construct a model that is supersymmetric but violates TRI, and argue that, at least perturbatively, TRI nevertheless emerges as an effective large-scale symmetry. Using the functional renormalization group (FRG), we further show that the dynamical effective action, $Γ[\varphi,\tilde\varphi]$, contains the derivative of the equilibrium effective action, $Γ^{\mathrm{eq}}[\varphi]$, whose renormalization-group flow is identical to that of the equilibrium theory order by order in the derivative expansion. Finally, extending the same line of reasoning, we show that the probability distribution of the total magnetization in the Ising model can be recovered within the Model A framework.

</details>


### [50] [Universal classification of continuous phase transitions with two order parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24061)
*Yilun Xu,Feng-xiao Sun*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper classifies systems with two order parameters based on coupling terms in free energy, revealing richer phase diagrams and critical phenomena beyond the Ginzburg-Landau paradigm for single order parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the Ginzburg-Landau paradigm to systems with multiple order parameters, which exhibit richer phase diagrams and critical phenomena not captured by single-parameter models.

Method: Classifying systems with two order parameters into categories based on coupling terms in the free energy expression, then analyzing resulting phase diagrams and critical scaling patterns.

Result: Identification of several categories of two-order-parameter systems, each featuring distinct phase diagram patterns and critical scaling behaviors.

Conclusion: The coupling terms in free energy provide a new avenue for studying and evaluating complex systems with multiple order parameters.

Abstract: The continuous phase transition, indicated by the macroscopic order parameter and the occurrence of the spontaneous symmetry breaking, is well illustrated based on the Ginzburg-Landau's paradigm. In systems described by one order parameter, the phase diagram is only composed of the normal phase and the ordered phase. However, in systems with two or more order parameters, much richer phase diagrams and critical phenomena may emerge. According to coupling terms of the free energy, we classify the systems with two order parameters into several categories, featuring different patterns of phase diagrams and critical scalings respectively. Our work propose the new avenue to studying and evaluating the complex systems only based on the coupling terms in expressions of free energy.

</details>


### [51] [Theory of supercooled water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24105)
*Jacobo Troncoso,Claudio A. Cerdeiriña*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A water model reproduces supercooled liquid behaviors and shows a liquid-liquid critical point, supported by an analogy with Joule-Thomson throttling and black hole thermodynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the nature of supercooled water states and the existence of a liquid-liquid critical point, connecting metastable thermodynamics with analogies from other physical systems.

Method: Introduces a numerical model of water that captures true supercooled-liquid states metastable with respect to crystalline solids, and draws a theoretical analogy with inversion thermodynamics of Joule-Thomson throttling and black hole phenomenology.

Result: The model reproduces both the Speedy-Angell stability-limit picture and Poole et al.'s liquid-liquid criticality scenario, and the analogy adds plausibility to the existence of a liquid-liquid critical point.

Conclusion: The liquid-liquid critical point in supercooled water gains theoretical support from an analogy with Joule-Thomson inversion thermodynamics and black hole behavior.

Abstract: We introduce a model of water contemplating true supercooled-liquid states that, as such, are metastable with respect to the crystalline-solid ones. Its numerical solutions reproduce from Speedy-Angell's stability-limit picture to Poole et al.'s metastable liquid--liquid criticality. The real existence of a liquid--liquid critical point is found to gain significant theoretical plausibility from a hitherto unnoticed analogy with the ``inversion thermodynamics'' of Joule-Thomson gas throttling process and, by extension, with the equivalent phenomenology exhibited by black holes.

</details>


### [52] [Resonant interactions in the $α$-FPUT lattice with site-dependent coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24268)
*Lorenzo Migliorelli,Giovanni Dematteis,Sergio Chibbaro,Miguel Onorato*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper extends wave turbulence theory to the α-FPUT problem with site-dependent spring stiffness and nonlinear coefficients, deriving a new kinetic equation that predicts faster thermalization and includes a Bragg-scattering term promoting isotropization of wave-action spectral density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend the wave turbulence framework to the α-FPUT problem with spatially modulated (site-dependent) parameters, where three-wave interactions become resonant due to spatial modulation, unlike the constant-coefficient case where they are non-resonant.

Method: The authors generalize wave turbulence theory to the α-FPUT chain with site-dependent spring stiffness χ and nonlinear coefficient α, analyzing how spatial modulation creates a non-trivial resonant manifold for three-wave interactions, and derive a corresponding kinetic equation.

Result: A new kinetic equation is derived that suggests substantially faster thermalization compared to the constant-coefficient case. The equation contains an additional term associated with Bragg scattering, which promotes isotropization of the wave-action spectral density function.

Conclusion: Spatial modulation of parameters in the α-FPUT chain enables resonant three-wave interactions that are absent in the uniform case, leading to faster thermalization and the emergence of Bragg-scattering-driven isotropization in wave turbulence dynamics.

Abstract: The wave turbulence framework has proven to be an effective tool for analyzing certain features of nonlinear energy transfer in one-dimensional nonlinear chains. In this work, we extend this approach to the $α$-FPUT problem when the spring stiffness $χ$ and the nonlinear coefficient $α$ are site-dependent. Although three-wave interactions are non-resonant for constant coefficients, their spatial modulation gives rise to a non-trivial resonant manifold. In this framework, we derive a new kinetic equation that suggests the possibility of substantially faster thermalization with respect to the constant coefficient case. The new kinetic equation includes also an extra term that can be associated to the Bragg-scattering mechanism, which promotes the isotropization of the wave-action spectral density function.

</details>


### [53] [Streaming Molecular Dynamics Simulation Data for On-the-fly Processing and Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24271)
*Amruthesh Thirumalaiswamy,Lawson J. Woods,Heekun Cho,Hugo MacDermott-Opeskin,Jennifer Clark,Irfan Alibay,Yuxuan Zhuang,Oliver Beckstein,Matthias Heyden*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper introduces IMDv3, a streaming framework for molecular dynamics simulations that enables real-time access to all simulation data (including fast processes like vibrations and solvent dynamics) instead of traditional disk-based trajectory storage. Implemented in GROMACS, NAMD, LAMMPS, and integrated with MDAnalysis via the imdclient Python package, it improves performance and enables live monitoring, velocity correlation analysis, and membrane pore current analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MD simulations discard most generated data (femtosecond-level) due to storage limitations, retaining only sparse trajectory frames at 10-100 ps intervals. This loses information on fast processes (vibrations, solvent dynamics, transition states, transport phenomena) that are crucial for linking to macroscopic properties and experimental observables.

Method: The authors extend the Interactive Molecular Dynamics (IMD) protocol to create IMDv3, a streaming protocol implemented in GROMACS, NAMD, and LAMMPS. They develop the imdclient Python package to receive and expose data streams, and integrate an imdclient-based reader into MDAnalysis for analysis alongside conventional trajectories.

Result: Benchmark results show streaming can improve performance over high-frequency trajectory output. Applications demonstrate live monitoring of custom variables, evaluation of velocity time-correlation functions capturing fast fluctuations, and real-time analysis of membrane pore currents.

Conclusion: IMDv3 provides a viable streaming solution that gives full access to all generated MD simulation data without storage bottlenecks, enabling analysis of fast processes and improving performance, with broad compatibility across major MD engines and analysis tools.

Abstract: Only a small fraction of the data generated in state-of-the-art all-atom multi-microsecond molecular dynamics (MD) simulations is typically analyzed. With femtosecond integration steps, microsecond simulations generate billions of time steps containing atomic positions, velocities, and forces, often corresponding to petabytes of data. Since this exceeds practical storage capacities, only a subset of frames is usually written to trajectory files at intervals of 10-100 ps. While sufficient for ensemble averages and slow dynamics, this approach discards information on fast processes such as molecular vibrations, solvent dynamics, short-lived transition states, and transport phenomena that are directly related to macroscopic properties and experimental observables. Here, we introduce a streaming framework for MD simulations that provides direct access to all data generated during a running simulation. Instead of writing high-frequency output to disk, the framework enables user-defined analysis and processing routines to access simulation data in real time. To achieve this, we extend the Interactive Molecular Dynamics (IMD) protocol and implement an enhanced version, termed IMDv3, in the MD packages GROMACS, NAMD, and LAMMPS. We further introduce the Python package imdclient, which receives IMDv3 data streams and exposes them to external applications. To maximize usability, we add an imdclient-based reader to the MDAnalysis package, enabling streamed simulation data to be analyzed alongside conventional trajectory files. Benchmark results show that streaming can improve performance compared to simulations with high-frequency trajectory output. Example applications include live monitoring of custom variables, evaluation of velocity time-correlation functions with fast fluctuations, and real-time analysis of membrane pore currents.

</details>


### [54] [Thermodynamics of classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24365)
*Yoshihiko Hasegawa*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper derives a fundamental trade-off between classification error and thermodynamic cost in binary classifiers, showing that the Bayes error is bounded below by entropy production and dynamical activity, with zero cost yielding random guessing (error=1/2), and extends the result to quantum classifiers where error is bounded by Hamiltonian variance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As computing energy demands rise, approximate computing trades accuracy for energy efficiency, raising the fundamental question of how error relates to thermodynamic cost in information processing.

Method: The authors model binary classification using Markov processes and derive lower bounds on Bayes error in terms of entropy production and dynamical activity, with a quantum generalization linking error to Hamiltonian variance.

Result: When entropy production or dynamical activity vanishes, Bayes error reaches 1/2 (random guessing); greater thermodynamic costs enable lower error. The quantum classifier's Bayes error is bounded below by the variance of the Hamiltonian.

Conclusion: There exists a fundamental error-cost trade-off in thermodynamic information processing: error cannot fall below bounds set by entropy production or dynamical activity, establishing a physical limit on classification accuracy.

Abstract: The Landauer principle bridges the energetic cost and information processing, showing that irreversible computation inevitably demands energy dissipation. As energy demands from computation continue to rise, approximate computing has attracted considerable attention. Approximate computing is based on the idea that energy consumption can be reduced by sacrificing computational accuracy. This raises a fundamental question about the relationship between error and thermodynamic cost in information processing. In this study, we derive the error-cost trade-off in the binary classifier by considering classification based on Markov processes. We obtain the lower bounds on the Bayes error in terms of thermodynamic costs such as entropy production and dynamical activity. Our results show that when entropy production or dynamical activity vanishes, the Bayes error reaches $1/2$, equivalent to random guessing, while greater thermodynamic costs enable lower error. This establishes a fundamental trade-off between error and cost in information processing by thermodynamic systems. Because the Bayes error provides the lowest achievable error among all possible classifiers, the classification error cannot fall below the obtained bounds given the entropy production or dynamical activity. We also discuss the quantum generalization and show that the Bayes error of the quantum classifier is bounded from below by the variance of the Hamiltonian.

</details>


### [55] [The peculiar response of Kelvin-Voigt chains with a free end](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24467)
*Rupayan Saha,Matthias Krüger*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A heterogeneous chain of overdamped, harmonically coupled particles with momentum-conserving dissipation is exactly solved via a forward-difference transformation, revealing a staircase response matrix and a decomposition into free (steady-state) and constrained (relaxation) subspaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To exactly solve a heterogeneous chain model with non-symmetric drift operators and momentum-conserving dissipation, which typically resists analytical treatment.

Method: Exact analytical diagonalization using a forward-difference transformation to handle the non-symmetric drift operator; decomposition of state space into free and constrained subspaces via rank-deficient interaction matrices.

Result: The response matrix exhibits a staircase form: response of particle i to a force on particle j is independent of properties and length of the chain segment between them. Free subspace governs steady-state responses; constrained subspace governs relaxation after forcing cessation.

Conclusion: Establishes a general framework for analyzing heterogeneous overdamped dynamics with momentum conservation, enabling clean separation of steady-state and relaxation behaviors.

Abstract: We exactly solve a model of a heterogeneous chain of overdamped, harmonically coupled particles with momentum-conserving dissipation. Despite being governed by a non-symmetric drift operator, the system admits an analytical diagonalization by use of a forward-difference transformation. In case of one free end, the response matrix shows a peculiar staircase form: the response of particle i to a force acting on particle j is independent of the properties and the length of the chain-part between i and j. For rank-deficient interaction matrices, the state space is decomposed into free and constrained subspaces. We demonstrate that this separation has clear physical consequences: the free subspace governs steady state responses, while the constraint subspace governs the relaxation after cessation of forcing. These results establish a framework for analyzing heterogeneous overdamped dynamics with momentum conservation.

</details>


### [56] [Implicit Binarization via Complex Phase Dynamics in Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24502)
*Khen Cohen,Mark Glass,Meir Feder,Yaron Oz*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A physics-inspired continuous relaxation framework that parameterizes binary variables as complex phases on the unit circle, introducing an implicit regularization mechanism that significantly improves solutions for NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems (QUBO, binary sparse coding, planted-solution Ising models), achieving near-perfect recovery rates even under high noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems like QUBO, binary sparse coding, and Ising models suffer from highly non-convex energy landscapes, making it difficult for standard solvers to escape local minima and find ground-state solutions.

Method: Discrete binary variables are parameterized as continuous wave-like states on the complex unit circle, smoothing the non-convex energy landscape. An implicit regularization mechanism promoting convergence to discrete states is extracted and applied as an explicit regularizer in standard real-valued optimization frameworks.

Result: Zero error on 160x160 QUBO tasks under severe noise (sigma=0.25); outperformed OMP and LASSO in underdefined sparse coding with perfect recovery at sigma=0.15; recovered exact ground states in 8 out of 11 planted-solution benchmarks.

Conclusion: The proposed complex-phase continuous relaxation with implicit/explicit regularization yields substantially higher ground-state convergence rates than standard real-valued alternatives, demonstrating strong robustness to noise and scalability to large problem instances.

Abstract: We introduce a physics-inspired continuous relaxation framework that yields substantially improved solutions for NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems, including Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO), binary sparse coding, and planted-solution Ising models. By parameterizing discrete binary variables as continuous wave-like states on the complex unit circle, we inherently smooth highly non-convex energy landscapes. We show that representing binary variables as complex phases reveals an implicit regularization mechanism that promotes convergence toward discrete states. Extracting this mechanism yields significant improvements even within standard real-valued optimization frameworks, using this regularizer explicitly. Empirically, this regularization yields vastly higher ground-state convergence rates than standard real-valued alternatives. Our models achieved zero error in large-scale 160x160 QUBO tasks under severe noise (sigma=0.25), and outperformed traditional algorithms (OMP and LASSO) in underdefined sparse coding with perfect recovery at sigma=0.15. The solver's robustness was further validated by recovering exact ground-state configurations in 8 out of 11 rigorously engineered planted-solution benchmarks.

</details>


### [57] [Analysis of Critical Points in a Permutation Model on Hierarchical Lattices by Real-Space Renormalization Group](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25683)
*Ryuki Ito,Taisei Matsuo,Masayuki Ohzeki*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies the ferromagnetic-paramagnetic phase transition of the permutation model, which relates to entanglement entropy transitions in random quantum circuits and random tensor networks. Using exact real-space renormalization group calculations on self-dual hierarchical lattices, the authors determine finite-replica critical points for q=mn=2,...,6, compare with duality predictions based on Fourier transforms of the symmetric group, and extrapolate toward the replica limit mn→0 where the effective dimension is two. Results support the duality-based estimate while clarifying systematic uncertainties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The permutation model's phase transition corresponds to entanglement entropy transitions between area-law and volume-law scaling in random quantum circuits and random tensor networks. Understanding this transition point has attracted considerable attention, motivating precise numerical determination of critical points.

Method: Exact real-space renormalization group calculations on self-dual hierarchical lattices, determining finite-replica critical points for q=mn=2,...,6, followed by comparison with duality predictions based on Fourier transform of the symmetric group, and extrapolation toward the replica limit mn→0.

Result: Finite-replica critical points determined for q=mn=2,...,6; comparison supports duality-based estimate while clarifying systematic uncertainty associated with the extrapolation formula.

Conclusion: The duality-based estimate is supported by the numerical data, with clarified understanding of systematic uncertainties in the extrapolation to the replica limit.

Abstract: The permutation model is a classical spin system where elements of the symmetric group interact with one another. The partition function of this model is directly related to the entanglement structure of random quantum circuits and random tensor networks. In these contexts, the entanglement entropy undergoes a transition between area-law and volume-law scaling, depending on the model parameters. This transition point has attracted considerable attention. In the present work, we investigate the ferromagnetic-paramagnetic phase transition of the permutation model, which corresponds to the entanglement entropy transition. Using exact real-space renormalization group calculations on self-dual hierarchical lattices, we numerically determine finite-replica critical points for (q=mn=2,...,6). We compare the results with the duality prediction based on the Fourier transform of the symmetric group and then extrapolate the b=3 data toward the replica limit $mn\to0$, where the effective dimension is two. The comparison supports the duality-based estimate while also clarifying the systematic uncertainty associated with the extrapolation formula.

</details>


### [58] [Strong Eigenstate Thermalization from Mean-Ergodic Non-chaotic Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24510)
*Avadhut V. Purohit,Harshit Sharma,Udaysinh T. Bhosale*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A double kicked top model with non-chaotic but mean-ergodic dynamics is shown to exhibit strong eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH), with diagonal matrix element fluctuations scaling as D^{-1/2} and Gaussian off-diagonal statistics, demonstrating that strong ETH does not require chaos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address whether strong ETH is a quantum analog of ergodicity (or mean-ergodicity) by investigating if non-chaotic yet mean-ergodic systems can satisfy strong ETH.

Method: Study a many-body system derived from the double kicked top (DKT) that exhibits mean-ergodic but non-chaotic dynamics, analyzing the scaling of diagonal matrix element fluctuations (D^{-1/2}) and the statistical properties of off-diagonal matrix elements.

Result: Diagonal matrix element fluctuations scale as D^{-1/2}; off-diagonal elements follow Gaussian statistics with a smooth, nearly uniform function f_O(Ē, ω) in the large-k_θ domain.

Conclusion: Even mean-ergodic and non-chaotic systems can exhibit strong ETH, implying strong ETH is a quantum analog of mean-ergodicity rather than chaos.

Abstract: We report an example of a many-body system, derived from the double kicked top (DKT), with non-chaotic yet mean-ergodic dynamics that displays \textit{strong} eigenstate thermalization hypothesis (ETH) in the quantum regime. The analysis addresses a key open question: whether \textit{strong} ETH is a quantum analog of ergodicity (or mean-ergodicity). Despite non-chaotic dynamics, the fluctuations of the diagonal matrix elements of an observable scale as $D^{-1/2}$, where $D$ denotes the Hilbert space dimension. Furthermore, the off-diagonal matrix elements show Gaussian statistics together with a smooth function $f_O(\bar{E}, ω)$ that becomes nearly uniform in the large-$k_θ$ domain. Our findings show that even mean-ergodic and non-chaotic systems can exhibit \textit{strong} ETH.

</details>


### [59] [Rare events of small-noise Doob conditioned processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24537)
*Iago N. Mamede,Francesco Coghi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper studies the statistics of Doob-conditioned path ensembles in the weak-noise large deviation regime, avoiding explicit Doob transform by using a post-selection interpretation and optimal-control representation, with applications to rare trajectories and heat dissipation in biomolecular folding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To analyze the statistics of rare trajectories of Markov processes conditioned on reaching a target at a fixed time, without requiring explicit construction of the Doob drift, which is rarely available in closed form.

Method: The conditioned ensemble is reinterpreted as the original process post-selected on the terminal constraint, yielding an optimal-control representation for the leading exponential contribution to the moment generating function, formulated as a variational principle with terminal boundary conditions from the Doob end-point constraint.

Result: The framework is illustrated with two analytical examples and applied to heat dissipation in a minimal model of biomolecular folding.

Conclusion: The post-selection viewpoint avoids explicit Doob transform construction and provides a tractable variational principle for studying large deviations of conditioned path ensembles.

Abstract: Doob fixed-time conditioning enables the sampling of rare trajectories of Markov processes by modifying the drift so that reaching a prescribed target at a given time is guaranteed. We study the statistics of this conditioned path ensemble through the moment generating function in the weak-noise large deviation regime. Since the Doob drift is rarely available in closed form, we reinterpret the conditioned ensemble as the original process post-selected on the terminal constraint, thereby avoiding explicit construction of the Doob transform. This viewpoint then yields an optimal-control representation for the leading exponential contribution to the generating function, expressed as a variational principle with terminal boundary conditions set by the Doob end-point constraint. We illustrate the framework with two analytical examples and with an application to heat dissipation of a minimal model of biomolecular folding.

</details>


### [60] [Manipulation of information flow and thermodynamic performance in nonreciprocal quantum dot information engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24554)
*Hao Feng,Junjie Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper shows that nonreciprocal coupling between quantum dots and reservoirs can control information flow in a double-quantum-dot information engine, outperforming reciprocal designs. This is formalized via an extended second law and an effective mapping to a reciprocal system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While information engines use information as a thermodynamic resource, strategies for efficiently manipulating the information flow between the working substance and the controller remain largely unexplored.

Method: The authors investigate an autonomous double-quantum-dot information engine with a downstream dot (working substance) coupled to two reservoirs and an upstream dot (controller) coupled to one reservoir. They extend the second law to incorporate nonreciprocal coupling effects and develop a thermodynamic framework to analyze inter-dot information flow modulation.

Result: Nonreciprocity significantly modulates inter-dot information flow, providing a robust control mechanism. The effect is equivalent to a mapping to an effective reciprocal system via reparameterization of chemical potentials and electron-electron coupling strength. Nonreciprocal coupling surpasses conventional reciprocal configurations in engine performance and operational flexibility.

Conclusion: Nonreciprocal coupling serves as an effective control knob for designing and optimizing quantum dot information engines, offering capabilities beyond those of reciprocal configurations.

Abstract: Quantum information engines leverage information as a thermodynamic resource to facilitate energy conversion. In the operation of such engines, the information flow between the working substance and the controller is pivotal, however, strategies for its efficient manipulation remain largely unexplored. Here, we investigate an autonomous information engine based on a double-quantum-dot setup, where a downstream dot coupled to two reservoirs acts as the working substance, and an upstream dot coupled to a single reservoir serves as the controller. By extending the second law of thermodynamics to incorporate the effects of nonreciprocal couplings between the dots and their electronic reservoirs, we develop a thermodynamic framework that allows us to demonstrate that nonreciprocity can significantly modulate the inter-dot information flow, thereby providing a robust control mechanism. We show that the influence of nonreciprocity can be equivalently understood through a mapping to an effective reciprocal system upon a reparameterization of chemical potentials and the electron-electron coupling strength. We further analyze the impact of nonreciprocity on the engine's performance and operation regime. Our findings establish nonreciprocal coupling as an effective control knob for designing and optimizing quantum dot information engines, surpassing the capabilities of conventional reciprocal configurations.

</details>


### [61] [A particle-resolved rheological study of chirality transfer and odd transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25136)
*Rémi Goerlich,Alexander P. Antonov,Kristian Stølevik Olsen,Lorenzo Caprini,Christian Scholz,Hartmut Löwen,Yael Roichman*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A particle-resolved study demonstrates that a symmetric passive tracer embedded in a chiral active bath exhibits chirality transfer (circular trajectories) and odd transport (transverse drift under constant pulling force), with nonlinear friction rectifying chiral fluctuations into macroscopic odd response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how chiral environments influence embedded tracers and identify the microscopic mechanisms through which anomalous (odd) transport responses emerge in chiral active matter systems.

Method: Combination of table-top experiments with a non-equilibrium chiral active bath, many-body simulations, and a reduced coarse-grained theory, where a symmetric passive tracer is driven through collisions with chiral active particles.

Result: Local collisions transfer chiral active dynamics to the tracer, producing circular trajectories; a systematic transverse drift emerges under constant pulling force; nonlinear friction is identified as the essential factor rectifying chiral fluctuations into macroscopic odd response.

Conclusion: The work reveals a microscopic mechanism for odd transport in chiral active matter and provides general insights into transverse transport in driven non-equilibrium systems.

Abstract: Chirality, or the breaking of mirror symmetry, appears across all scales in nature, from molecular conformations to the dynamics of bacterial collectives. Environments composed of such symmetry-breaking constituents can give rise to emergent physical phenomena, particularly in the transport and response of embedded tracers. Yet it remains unclear how chiral environments influence such tracers and through which microscopic mechanisms anomalous responses emerge. Here, we present a particle-resolved study of these systems, demonstrating chirality transfer and odd transport of an object embedded in a chiral active bath. In a rheological experiment, a symmetric passive tracer is driven through collisions with the particles of a non-equilibrium chiral bath. Combining table-top experiments, many-body simulations, and a reduced coarse-grained theory, we demonstrate that local collisions transfer chiral active dynamics to the tracer, which displays circular trajectories. We show that the same mechanism gives rise to a systematic transverse drift under a constant pulling force. Crucially, we identify nonlinear friction as an essential factor that rectifies these transferred chiral active fluctuations into a macroscopic odd response. Our results reveal a microscopic mechanism for odd transport in chiral active matter and provide general insights into transverse transport in driven non-equilibrium systems.

</details>


### [62] [First-passage time distribution of a Brownian particle harmonically confined in a viscoelastic bath](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25215)
*Brandon R. Ferrer,Juan Ruben Gomez-Solano*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper derives a general expression for the first-passage time distribution of a harmonically trapped Brownian particle in non-Markovian baths using the overdamped generalized Langevin equation, and validates it experimentally with colloidal beads in viscoelastic polymer, micellar solutions, and viscous media, showing that viscoelasticity can lead to longer mean first-passage times due to slowly decaying tails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how memory effects (non-Markovian friction) influence the first-passage time properties of a Brownian particle trapped in a harmonic potential, which is relevant for microrheology, biophysical transport, and stochastic thermodynamics.

Method: Theoretical derivation using the overdamped generalized Langevin equation to obtain the first-passage time probability density function in terms of the friction memory kernel, validated experimentally with optical tweezers tracking colloidal beads in various fluids (viscoelastic polymer solutions, micellar solutions, glycerol/water, and water).

Result: The first-passage time distribution reduces to known expressions for Markovian baths. In viscoelastic (non-Markovian) baths, the mean first-passage time can exceed that in a purely viscous medium with the same zero-shear viscosity due to slowly decaying tails in the probability density. Experimental results show quantitative agreement with theory.

Conclusion: Memory effects in viscoelastic baths significantly alter first-passage time statistics, producing longer mean first-passage times compared to Markovian baths with equivalent zero-shear viscosity, which has implications for transport phenomena in complex fluids and biological environments.

Abstract: We investigate theoretically and experimentally the first passage-time properties of a spherical Brownian particle that is harmonically trapped at thermal equilibrium in a fluid at constant temperature. By using the overdamped version of the generalized Langevin equation, we derive a general expression for the probability density function of the time that the particle takes to reach for the first time the minimum of the potential starting from an arbitrary position. We show that such a first-passage time distribution can be implicitly expressed in terms of the friction memory kernel that encodes the interaction of the particle with its surroundings, and correctly reduces to previously found expressions in the case of a Markovian viscous bath. We validate our theoretical results by measuring the first-passage time of colloidal beads optically trapped in non-Markovian baths such as viscoelastic polymer and micellar solutions, as well as in a viscous glycerol/water mixture and water, which behave as Markovian media, having quantitative agreement with the derived expressions. In particular, we find that the mean first-passage time in a viscoelastic bath can surpass that in a viscous medium of the same zero-shear viscosity due to the emergence of slowly decaying tails in the first-passage time probability density of the former.

</details>


### [63] [Beyond Local Detailed Balance: Microscopic Rates Reshape Nonequilibrium Phase Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25406)
*Takahiro Kanazawa,Kyogo Kawaguchi,Kyosuke Adachi*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: For a driven lattice gas with attractive interactions, tuning a parameter that preserves local detailed balance but changes hopping asymmetry can reverse correlation anisotropy and switch phase orientation, showing that nonequilibrium phase behavior depends on specific dynamical rules even under the same LDB.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve whether the functional form of rates under the same local detailed balance can reshape phase behavior in strongly interacting nonequilibrium systems.

Method: The authors study a two-dimensional driven lattice gas with attractive nearest-neighbor interactions, using hopping rates with a parameter that preserves LDB but tunes asymmetry along the driving force, and analyze the system via fluctuations and approximate fluctuating hydrodynamics.

Result: Tuning the asymmetry parameter reverses the sign of the structure-factor discontinuity (homogeneous phase) and switches orientation/anisotropy patterns and their long-time stability (phase-separated regime).

Conclusion: Nonequilibrium phase behavior depends on specific dynamical rules even when those rules satisfy the same local detailed balance condition, in contrast to equilibrium systems.

Abstract: Local detailed balance (LDB) is a central guiding principle for modeling nonequilibrium stochastic dynamics, yet it only constrains the ratio of forward and backward transition rates and does not fix the steady state. Although the functional form of rates under the same LDB has been shown to affect correlation properties in weakly interacting systems, whether it can reshape phase behavior in strongly interacting systems remains unclear. Here, for a two-dimensional driven lattice gas with attractive nearest-neighbor interactions, we consider hopping rates with a parameter that preserves the same LDB but tunes asymmetry along the driving force. We find that this parameter controls qualitative phase behavior: in the homogeneous phase, it reverses the sign of the structure-factor discontinuity and hence the anisotropy in long-range density correlations; in the phase-separated regime, it switches the orientation of anisotropic patterns and their long-time stability. Both effects are coherently captured by an approximate fluctuating hydrodynamic equation. The results demonstrate that, in contrast to equilibrium systems, nonequilibrium phase behavior depends on specific dynamical rules even when following the same LDB.

</details>


### [64] [Equivalent-neighbor $k$-core percolation in two dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25812)
*Qiyuan Shi,Ming Li,Youjin Deng*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Large-scale 2D simulations of k-core percolation with extended interaction range r reveal that no hybrid transition occurs; instead, the phase diagram shows a continuous (2D percolation universality) regime and a strictly first-order regime separated by a tricritical point. The first-order regime exhibits unusual 1/ln L finite-size scaling due to nucleation-driven collapse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how geometric constraints (finite dimensions and interaction range) affect the nature of k-core percolation, which is known to exhibit hybrid phase transitions in infinite dimensions but whose behavior in low dimensions with finite-range interactions remains unclear.

Method: Large-scale numerical simulations of k-core percolation in two dimensions, systematically varying both the core index k and the interaction range r to construct a comprehensive phase diagram in the (k,r) plane.

Result: No hybrid transition observed in 2D. The phase diagram contains a continuous transition regime (belonging to 2D percolation universality class) and a strictly first-order regime, separated by a tricritical/critical-end point (k_s, r_s). In the first-order regime, the pseudocritical point scales as 1/ln L, deviating from the conventional L^{-d} scaling.

Conclusion: Geometric constraints fundamentally alter the nature of k-core percolation relative to infinite dimensions. The logarithmic finite-size drift in the first-order regime is consistent with nucleation-driven collapse triggered by rare voids.

Abstract: We perform large-scale numerical simulations to investigate the critical behavior of $k$-core percolation in two dimensions with an extended interaction range $r$. By systematically varying both the core index $k$ and the interaction range $r$, we construct a comprehensive phase diagram in the $(k,r)$ plane. In contrast to $k$-core percolation in infinite dimensions, no hybrid transition is observed in two dimensions: the phase diagram contains only a continuous transition regime and a strictly first-order regime, separated by a tricritical or critical-end point $(k_s,r_s)$. For $k<k_s$ and $r<r_s$, the transition is continuous and belongs to the universality class of standard two-dimensional (2D) percolation. For $k>k_s$ and finite $r>r_s$, the transition is discontinuous, with no hybrid features or critical singularities. In this first-order regime, the pseudocritical point approaches the critical point as $1/\ln L$, where $L$ is the linear system size, distinct from the $L^{-d}$ scaling typical of conventional thermodynamic first-order transitions in $d$ dimensions. This logarithmic finite-size drift is consistent with a nucleation-driven mechanism, in which rare voids trigger the collapse of the finite-range $k$-core. These results demonstrate that geometric constraints can fundamentally alter the nature of $k$-core percolation found in finite dimensions.

</details>


### [65] [Freezing of the tetrahedral amorphous network in supercooled water triggers crystallization towards LDA ice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25926)
*Ashutosh Srivastava,Pankaj A. Apte*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Using MD simulations with TIP4P/2005, the authors elucidate the mechanism of initial ice formation across the limit of stability of supercooled water, showing that crystallization initiates after dynamical crossover and tetrahedral network freezing, with cubic ice contributing significantly to LDA ice formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide mechanistic insight into the initial stages of ice formation across the limit of stability of supercooled water, where crystal nucleation is not a relevant mechanism.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations using the TIP4P/2005 potential, cooling water at constant pressure with cooling rates of 5-10 K/ns, analyzing structural and dynamical changes as the system crosses the temperature of maximum density (T₀ = 277 K) and the limit of stability (T_s ≈ 235 K).

Result: Cooling across T₀ causes continuous increase in tetrahedrality. Across T_s, dynamical crossover occurs where large-scale fluctuations dissipate and small-scale fluctuations dominate. Subsequently, the tetrahedral network drives energy/density decrease until bond rigidity causes network freezing, after which relaxation shifts to crystallization with increased cubic and hexagonal medium-range-order motifs; cubic ice contributes significantly to LDA ice.

Conclusion: The specific mechanism by which crystallization (leading to LDA ice) is initiated across the limit of stability of supercooled water involves: (1) dynamical crossover ending the liquid state, (2) tetrahedral network freezing, and (3) subsequent crystallization with significant cubic ice contribution, consistent with experiments.

Abstract: In this work, we provide mechanistic insight into the initial stages of formation of ice across the limit of stability of supercooled water. Such an analysis is particularly important since crystal nucleation is not a relevant mechanism under these conditions. Using molecular dynamics simulation with the TIP4P/2005 potential, water is cooled at a constant pressure with cooling rates of 5 to 10 K per nanosecond. As the liquid is cooled across the temperature of maximum density (T_0 = 277 K), we find that there is a continuous increase in the tetrahedrality of the system. As the cooling continues across the limit of stability of water (T_s $\approx$ 235 K), large scale thermal fluctuations dissipate while the thermal equilibration is achieved through small scale fluctuations. This phenomenon, known as the dynamical crossover [Goutam et. al. in J. Stat. Phys., 168: 1302--1318 (2017)], ends the existence of the liquid state. Subsequently, we find that the tetrahedral network drives the decrease of energy and density. This process terminates when the network undergoes `freezing' (i.e., the bonds of the network acquire sufficient rigidity), due to which the network evolution, as a whole, stops. This triggers a qualitative change in the relaxation mechanism: subsequent relaxation occurs through crystallization, i.e., an increase in the structural order. In particular, we find that the cubic and hexagonal crystalline motifs, which possess medium range order, increase rapidly across the freezing point. In the resulting LDA ice states, cubic ice is found to have a significant contribution in the overall extent of crystallization, which is consistent with the experimental findings. Overall, our work provides the specific mechanism by which crystallization (leading to LDA ice) is initiated across the limit of stability of supercooled water.

</details>


### [66] [Generalized field mixing for endpoint criticality with marginal flow: resolving the four-state Potts endpoint in the square-lattice $J_1$--$J_2$ Ising model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25946)
*Yihua Sun,Yuchen Fan*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper develops a generalized field-mixing framework for critical endpoints governed by one relevant and one marginally irrelevant scaling field, addressing the challenge of pseudo-first-order signatures that mask asymptotic criticality. Applied to the frustrated square-lattice J1-J2 Ising model, it resolves a decades-old controversy by showing the stripe-ordering endpoint follows four-state Potts universality with logarithmic corrections, validated through Binder scaling and Ashkin-Teller model comparison.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying asymptotic criticality of critical endpoints is challenging due to pseudo-first-order signatures that persist over accessible system sizes and mask underlying critical nature, especially when endpoints are controlled by a marginally irrelevant scaling field where logarithmic flow delays onset of asymptotic scaling.

Method: A generalized field-mixing framework for endpoint criticality with one relevant and one marginally irrelevant scaling field is constructed. It builds a finite-size pseudocritical manifold that removes the normal relevant detuning and exposes residual marginal drift, enabling controlled histogram- and Binder-based finite-size analyses.

Result: The endpoint of the frustrated square-lattice J1-J2 Ising model is isolated as a distinct singular point and shown to follow four-state Potts universality with logarithmic corrections. This identification is independently supported by direct comparison with the Ashkin-Teller model Potts point and consistent Binder scaling in both magnetic and nematic sectors.

Conclusion: The work resolves a longstanding numerical ambiguity in a paradigmatic frustrated Ising system and establishes a general framework for extracting asymptotic endpoint criticality in the presence of marginal flow.

Abstract: Identifying the asymptotic criticality of a critical endpoint is challenging, as pseudo-first-order signatures persist over accessible system sizes and mask its underlying critical nature. This ambiguity is amplified at endpoints controlled by a marginally irrelevant scaling field, where logarithmic flow delays the onset of asymptotic scaling. Here we develop a generalized field-mixing framework for endpoint criticality governed by one relevant scaling field together with a marginally irrelevant one, a setting that lies outside the conventional two-relevant-field formulation. By constructing a finite-size pseudocritical manifold, the framework removes the normal relevant detuning and exposes the residual marginal drift, enabling controlled histogram- and Binder-based finite-size analyses. We apply this approach to the frustrated square-lattice $J_1$--$J_2$ Ising model, where the location and even the nature of the stripe-ordering endpoint have remained controversial for decades. The endpoint is isolated directly as a distinct singular point, rather than inferred from where the phase boundary appears most Potts-like, and its asymptotic criticality is shown to follow four-state Potts universality with logarithmic corrections. This identification is independently supported by direct comparison with the Potts point of the Ashkin--Teller model and by consistent Binder scaling in both the magnetic and nematic sectors. Our results resolve a longstanding numerical ambiguity in a paradigmatic frustrated Ising system and establish a general framework for extracting asymptotic endpoint criticality in the presence of marginal flow.

</details>


### [67] [Boltzmann Distribution from Invariance of Coarse-Graining-Scale and Energy-Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25987)
*Weicheng Fu,Yisen Wang,Yong Zhang,Hong Zhao*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper derives the Boltzmann distribution in classical many-body systems from two principles: coarse-graining-scale invariance and energy-zero shift invariance, demonstrating its validity across various physical quantities in diatomic hard-core gases and nonlinear lattice chains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a concise derivation of the Boltzmann distribution based on fundamental physical invariance principles rather than traditional statistical mechanics assumptions.

Method: The derivation uses coarse-graining-scale invariance and uniform energy-zero shift invariance of empirical distributions to uniquely yield the Boltzmann factor. The parameter is fixed by mean energy per particle.

Result: The principle successfully predicts velocity, energy, spacing, collision-time, and pressure-dependent displacement distributions in one-dimensional diatomic hard-core gas and nonlinear lattice chain, validated by simulations. The lattice model shows harmonic elasticity, anharmonic corrections, internal pressure, and thermal expansion emerging from exponential equilibrium weights.

Conclusion: The Boltzmann distribution can be derived from fundamental invariance principles and extends to any physical quantity with stationary distribution and translational invariance. The paper briefly discusses relationships among different ensembles.

Abstract: We present a concise derivation of the Boltzmann form for single-particle energy distributions in classical many-body Hamiltonian systems. The derivation relies on two physical facts: coarse-graining-scale invariance of the empirical distribution and invariance under a uniform shift of the energy zero. These conditions uniquely yield the Boltzmann factor, whose parameter is fixed by the mean energy per particle. For separable Hamiltonians, the equilibrium weight factorizes into kinetic and configurational contributions sharing the same parameter, identified from the kinetic part as the inverse kinetic temperature. The principle extends to any physical quantity with a stationary distribution and translational invariance. It is illustrated in a one-dimensional diatomic hard-core gas and a nonlinear lattice chain, where it predicts velocity, energy, spacing, collision-time, and pressure-dependent displacement distributions in agreement with simulations. The lattice model further shows how harmonic elasticity, anharmonic corrections, internal pressure, and thermal expansion emerge from the same exponential equilibrium weights. Finally, the relationships among different ensembles are briefly discussed.

</details>


### [68] [Effect of slow bonds on current fluctuations in the symmetric simple exclusion process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26069)
*Soumyabrata Saha,Sandeep Jangid,Kapil Sharma,Tridib Sadhu*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper derives exact large deviation functions for particle current in the symmetric simple exclusion process (SSEP) with localized slow bonds, considering three geometries: finite chain with boundary reservoirs, semi-infinite chain with a reservoir, and infinite chain with slow bonds near the origin. Results are validated via rare-event simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how localized slow bonds modify the exact large deviation results for particle current in the SSEP, which is a fundamental model of non-equilibrium dynamics.

Method: Integrability-based methods combined with an elementary derivation for the semi-infinite case; validation via rare-event simulations using the cloning algorithm.

Result: Exact expressions for the large deviation function of current are obtained for three geometries: finite 1D lattice with unequal boundary reservoirs, semi-infinite 1D lattice with a boundary reservoir, and infinite 1D lattice with slow bonds near the origin.

Conclusion: The presence of localized slow bonds modifies the large deviation function of current in the SSEP, and the derived exact results are validated by simulations, with an elementary derivation provided for the semi-infinite case.

Abstract: The symmetric simple exclusion process (SSEP) is a paradigmatic model of classical non-equilibrium dynamics. Exact results for large deviations of particle current in the SSEP have been obtained in various settings using integrability-based methods. In this Article, we discuss how these results are modified in the presence of localized slow bonds. We consider three conventional geometries: (a) a finite one-dimensional lattice weakly coupled to unequal reservoirs at its boundaries, (b) a semi-infinite one-dimensional lattice weakly coupled to a boundary reservoir, and (c) an infinite one-dimensional lattice with localized slow bonds near the origin. For each case, we present exact expressions for the large deviation function of current and validate them through rare-event simulations based on the cloning algorithm. In connection with our results, we present an elementary derivation of the exact large deviation function for the current in the semi-infinite SSEP, complementing recent results obtained through more elaborate techniques.

</details>

### [69] [Physics-Guided Concentration Inference from Resistance Transients in a Mixed-Phase SnO-SnO$_2$ Carbon Monoxide Sensor with p-n Switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.23971)
*Sani Biswas,Preetam Singh,Amit Kumar Gangwar*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: A physics-guided ML framework infers CO concentration from SnO-SnO₂ gas sensor resistance transients, leveraging p-type (classification, ~96.5% accuracy) and n-type (regression, MAE≈1.48 ppm, R²≈0.992) dual-regime behavior with physically interpretable features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable carbon monoxide concentration inference from experimentally measured resistance transients of a mixed-phase SnO-SnO₂ gas sensor with temperature-dependent p-n switching, while preserving physical interpretability beyond conventional single-response metrics.

Method: Cycle-level transient responses are represented using physics-guided descriptors, compact FFT, and DWT-based summaries. Leakage-aware grouped cross-validation is employed. Both multi-class classification and continuous regression are studied separately for p-type and n-type regimes, with fused feature sets evaluated via Random Forest models.

Result: Fused features yield strongest overall performance; physics-guided descriptor block alone remains highly competitive. p-type regime achieves ~96.5% classification accuracy; n-type regime achieves MAE≈1.48 ppm and R²≈0.992 for regression. Dual-regime behavior: p-type favors classification, n-type favors regression.

Conclusion: Leakage-aware, cycle-level, physics-guided ML can extend conventional gas-sensing analysis with physical interpretability, revealing distinct regime-specific advantages for classification vs. regression tasks.

Abstract: This work presents a physics-guided machine-learning framework for carbon monoxide concentration inference from experimentally measured resistance transients of a mixed-phase SnO-SnO$_2$ material gas sensor exhibiting temperature-dependent p-n switching behavior. Cycle-level transient responses are represented through physically interpretable descriptors and complemented by compact fast Fourier transform (FFT) and discrete wavelet transform (DWT)-based summaries. Using leakage-aware grouped cross-validation, we study both multi-class concentration classification and continuous concentration regression for the p-type and n-type sensing regimes separately. Across both regimes, fused features provide the strongest overall performance, while the physics-guided descriptor block remains highly competitive, indicating that the dominant concentration information is already encoded in physically meaningful transient dynamics.
