TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that perfect superconducting diode effect (100% efficiency) can be achieved in light-driven nonequilibrium systems, with monochromatic light enabling it in non-centrosymmetric systems and multifrequency light enabling it even in centrosymmetric systems through dynamical symmetry breaking, establishing a general principle for unidirectional superconducting transport via nonequilibrium control and symmetry engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The perfect superconducting diode effect (SDE) with 100% diode efficiency is difficult to achieve in equilibrium systems, motivating the exploration of nonequilibrium light-driven approaches to overcome this limitation.

Method: The authors employ monochromatic light to drive systems lacking inversion and time-reversal symmetries, and multifrequency light to drive centrosymmetric systems via dynamical symmetry breaking, achieving perfect SDE through nonequilibrium control.

Result: Perfect SDE (unidirectional supercurrent with 100% diode efficiency) is achieved in light-driven nonequilibrium systems; monochromatic light works for systems with broken inversion and time-reversal symmetries, while multifrequency light enables the effect even in centrosymmetric systems.

Conclusion: A general principle for realizing unidirectional superconducting transport is established, based on nonequilibrium control and symmetry engineering, demonstrating that perfect SDE is achievable beyond equilibrium constraints.

Abstract: We demonstrate the perfect superconducting diode effect (SDE) -- unidirectional supercurrent with 100% diode efficiency -- in light-driven nonequilibrium systems. Although the perfect SDE is difficult to achieve in equilibrium, monochromatic light induces the perfect SDE in systems lacking inversion and time-reversal symmetries. More strikingly, multi-frequency light enables the perfect SDE even in centrosymmetric systems via dynamical symmetry breaking. Our results establish a general principle for realizing unidirectional superconducting transport based on nonequilibrium control and symmetry engineering.

</details>


### [123] [Nitrogen-doped W0.75Re0.25 Superconducting Nanowire Single Photon Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25597)
*F. Colangelo,Abhishek Kumar,H. Wang,F. Avitabile,C. Attanasio,I. Esmaeil Zadeh,C. Cirillo*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This paper reports on SNSPDs made from nitrogen-doped W0.75Re0.25 thin films, achieving saturated internal detection efficiency up to 1310 nm at 2.5 K with fast timing performance (73.2 ps jitter) and sub-nanosecond response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nitrogen-doped W-Re superconducting alloys were recently identified as a promising material platform for SNSPDs, offering high normal state resistivity and tunable superconducting properties, motivating further device-level exploration.

Method: Ultrathin W0.75Re0.25 films were deposited by reactive DC magnetron sputtering in mixed Ar/N2 atmosphere. Meander detectors with 70 nm linewidth were fabricated and characterized for detection efficiency, timing response, and jitter.

Result: The detectors exhibit saturated internal detection efficiency up to 1310 nm at 2.5 K, sub-nanosecond rise times, decay times of a few nanoseconds, and a timing jitter of 73.2 ps measured with room-temperature amplifiers.

Conclusion: N-doped W0.75Re0.25 is a viable and high-performance material platform for SNSPDs, combining good detection efficiency at telecom wavelengths with competitive temporal resolution.

Abstract: Nitrogen-doped Tungsten-Rhenium superconducting alloys were recently proposed as a promising material platform for superconducting nanowire single-photon detectors (SNSPDs), offering a favorable balance between high normal state resistivity and tunable superconducting properties. In this work, we report on the fabrication and characterization of SNSPDs based on ultrathin W0.75Re0.25 films deposited by reactive DC magnetron sputtering in a mixed Ar/N2 atmosphere. Meander detectors with 70 nm linewidth exhibit saturated internal detection efficiency up to 1310 nm at 2.5 K, with sub-nanosecond rise times, decay times of the order of a few nanoseconds, and timing jitter of 73.2 ps measured with room temperature amplifiers.

</details>


### [124] [Superconductivity and electronic structure evolution in the enforced semimetal Fe-doped ZrTe$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25898)
*L. M. Ishikura,C. F. Schuch,A. Faé Rabello,F. F. Nogueira,L. E. Corrêa,L. R. de Faria,J. Larrea Jiménez,L. T. F. Eleno,A. J. S. Machado*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Fe intercalation in ZrTe₂ induces superconductivity (Tc=2.74K at x=0.03) while preserving topological semimetal features, with a van Hove singularity at x=0.125 enhancing density of states near EF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the electronic structure evolution of ZrTe₂ upon Fe intercalation and explore the interplay between superconductivity and topological properties.

Method: Combined experimental measurements and ab initio calculations on FexZrTe₂ (x=0–0.25), analyzing superconducting properties, band structure topology, and density of states.

Result: Superconductivity emerges with Tc up to 2.74K at x=0.03; topological features are sensitive to Te-Zr interlayer distance; band crossings shift below EF; a van Hove singularity appears at x=0.125; semimetal classification is preserved.

Conclusion: Fe intercalation in ZrTe₂ induces superconductivity linked to enhanced DOS from a van Hove singularity, while maintaining the nontrivial topological semimetal character.

Abstract: ZrTe$_2$ is an outstanding layered semimetal due to the topologically nontrivial electronic structure. In this work, we present an investigation of the electronic evolution of ZrTe$_2$ in the presence of Fe intercalation, namely Fe$_{x}$ZrTe$_2$ ($x= 0 - 0.25$), scrutinized by both experimental measurements and \textit{ab} initio calculations. While the first reveals a superconducting state with a maximum critical temperature $T_c = 2.74$ K ($x=$ 0.03), the latter indicates that the topological features of the pristine ZrTe$_2$ is sensitive to the distance between Te atoms and Zr layers. Also, the intercalation of Fe does not modify the non-trivial electronic band structure unlike the band crossings are now shifted slightly below $E_{F}$. In particular, a van Hove singularity near the Fermi level for a Fe content of $x=0.125$ is observed in the density of states, indicating that the superconducting order may be associated with features of the unfolded band structure and the concomitant enhancement of the density of states at $E_F$. Finally, our results reveal that the new compound with inclusion of Fe intercalation preserves the enforced semimetal classification.

</details>


### [125] [Substitution modulated transition from semimetal to superconductor in ZrTe$_{2-x}$Se$_x$ with coexistence of nontrivial electronic topology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25906)
*V. M. Fim,C. F. Schuch,L. R. de Faria,F. A. Santos,M. S. da Luz,S. S. Tsirkin,L. T. F. Eleno,A. J. S. Machado*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Substituting Se into ZrTe₂ induces superconductivity up to 4.8 K at x=0.15, with a CDW-to-Dirac-semimetal+SC transition and evidence for topological surface states, suggesting a candidate platform for topological superconductivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore whether Se substitution in ZrTe₂ can induce superconductivity and potentially realize a topological superconducting phase.

Method: ICVT crystal growth; resistive, structural, and calorimetric measurements; ab initio calculations and Wannier Hamiltonian interpolation.

Result: Se substitution induces superconductivity with a peak Tc of 4.8 K at x=0.15; calorimetry shows modification of electronic and lattice contributions; a phase diagram from CDW to Dirac semimetal+SC coexistence to semiconductor is constructed; a nontrivial ℤ₂ invariant with surface states on (001) planes is found.

Conclusion: ZrTe₁.₈₅Se₀.₁₅ is a candidate topological superconductor with coexisting Dirac semimetal and superconducting states.

Abstract: This study explores the emergence of superconductivity in high-quality ZrTe$_{2-x}$Se$_x$ crystals, grown via the isothermal chemical vapor transport (ICVT) technique. Resistive, structural, and thermal measurements reveal that substituting Te with Se in the ZrTe$_2$ matrix induces a superconducting state at low temperatures. The critical temperature ($T_c$) exhibits a clear dependence on the selenium concentration, peaking at $x=0.15$ with a $T_c$ of $4.8$ K. Calorimetric data indicates that even a low Se substitution range is capable of modifying both the electronic contribution and the vibrational modes of the crystal lattice. Combined with ab initio calculations and Wannier Hamiltonian interpolation between ZrTe$_2$/ZrSe$_2$, we established an extensive phase diagram mapping the transition from charge density wave (CDW) to the state with coexistence between the Dirac semimetal and superconductivity (SC), up to the semiconductor phase. This coexistence suggests that ZrTe$_{1.85}$Se$_{0.15}$ could be a candidate platform for topological superconductivity, as it hosts a nontrivial $\mathbb Z_2$ invariant, with nonvanishing surface states in its $(001)$ planes.

</details>


### [126] [Exact Single-Scale Outer Solution of the Abrikosov Vortex in the Extreme Type-II Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25938)
*Eugene B. Kolomeisky*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper analytically determines the exact outer structure of Abrikosov vortices in the extreme type-II limit (κ → ∞), showing that both magnetic field and superconducting density vary on the single scale of the London penetration depth, contradicting the conventional two-length-scale picture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the precise asymptotic structure of Abrikosov vortices in the extreme type-II (κ → ∞) limit, where conventional understanding predicts two distinct length scales (coherence length ξ and penetration depth λ) but the actual behavior remains controversial.

Method: Asymptotic analysis of Ginzburg-Landau theory in the κ → ∞ limit, reducing the equations outside the shrinking vortex core to a closed nonlinear theory for superfluid velocity under an algebraic density constraint, yielding an exact outer solution.

Result: The solution is asymptotically exact everywhere outside the vanishing core. Both magnetic field and superconducting density vary on the single length scale of the London penetration depth, not two separate scales.

Conclusion: The conventional two-length-scale picture (coherence length for density, penetration depth for magnetic field) does not hold in the κ ≫ 1 limit; instead, a single scale governs both fields outside the core.

Abstract: We determine the exact outer structure of the Abrikosov vortex in the extreme type-II limit, which occurs when the Ginzburg-Landau parameter $κ$ diverges. In this limit, Ginzburg-Landau theory simplifies, outside a shrinking core, to a closed nonlinear theory for the superfluid velocity subject to an algebraic density constraint. The resulting solution is asymptotically exact everywhere outside the vanishing vortex core, demonstrating that both magnetic field and superconducting density vary on the length scale of the London penetration depth. This establishes that the conventional two-length-scale picture of the vortex does not hold in the $κ\gg 1$ limit.

</details>


<div id='physics.atm-clus'></div>

# physics.atm-clus [[Back]](#toc)

### [127] [Emerging Amines reshape the paradigm of urban atmospheric particle formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25795)
*Yongjian Lian,Xurong Bai,Ruoying Yuan,Wenli Xu,Hongjun Mao,Jianfei Peng,Shuai Jiang*

Main category: physics.atm-clus

TL;DR: New field measurements in urban Beijing show that emerging amines from carbon capture (DEA, PZ) can dominate sulfuric acid-amine nucleation pathways for new particle formation, surpassing the traditionally dominant dimethylamine (DMA), suggesting the current paradigm of urban nucleation needs revision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge and revise the prevailing paradigm that urban new particle formation (NPF) is primarily driven by sulfuric acid-dimethylamine (DMA) nucleation, by evaluating the contributions of emerging amines (MEA, PZ, DEA, MDEA) recently detected in urban Beijing from carbon capture processes.

Method: Systematic evaluation of sulfuric acid-amine nucleation pathways using field measurement data of emerging amines (MEA, PZ, DEA, MDEA) in summer urban Beijing, comparing their contributions to NPF against the traditional DMA pathway under polluted urban conditions.

Result: Emerging amines, particularly DEA and PZ, can dominate nucleation pathways under polluted urban conditions, surpassing the contribution of DMA, which has been previously considered the dominant amine involved in urban NPF.

Conclusion: The current universal paradigm of urban nucleation should be revisited to explicitly account for emerging amines; their role in NPF will become increasingly important under future co-control policies for air pollution and carbon reduction.

Abstract: New particle formation (NPF) contributes to more than half of global aerosol number concentrations, with profound implications for human health and climate change. Observational studies have shown that the frequency of NPF events in urban Beijing during summer exceeds the global average. The prevailing paradigm attributes urban NPF primarily to sulfuric acid-base nucleation involving dimethylamine (DMA). However, recent field measurements in summer urban Beijing have identified several emerging amines emitted from carbon capture processes, including monoethanolamine (MEA), piperazine (PZ), diethanolamine (DEA) and N-methyldiethanolamine (MDEA), in addition to DMA. Here, we systematically evaluate the contributions of sulfuric acid-amine nucleation pathways to urban NPF. We found that emerging amines particularly DEA and PZ, can dominate nucleation pathways under polluted urban conditions, surpassing the contribution of DMA. These findings suggest that the current universal paradigm of urban nucleation should be revisited to explicitly account for the role of emerging amines. Moreover, emerging amine-mediated NPF will become increasingly important in the context of future co-control policies for air pollution and carbon reduction.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.mes-hall'></div>

# cond-mat.mes-hall [[Back]](#toc)

### [128] [AI-Driven SERS for Non-invasive and Label-Free Extracellular Vesicle Detection Across Cellular Origins in Tears and Sweat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25465)
*Yang Li,Xiaoming Lyu,Ling Xia,Kuo Zhan,Haoyu Ji,Lei Qin,Seppo J. Vainio,Jian-An Huang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops an AI-assisted, label-free SERS method using salt-induced nanoparticle aggregation for rapid and highly accurate (>92%) identification of exosomes from 6 cell lines in tears and sweat, enabling non-invasive point-of-care diagnostics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing EV detection methods require labeling and are time-consuming, and label-free SERS with deep learning had not been applied to non-invasive biofluids like tears and sweat.

Method: Salt-induced nanoparticle aggregation combined with AI-assisted SERS for label-free EV detection; molecular dynamics simulations to study Ag-amino acid interactions.

Result: Achieved >92% accuracy in identifying EVs from 6 cell lines across tear fluids from 7 disease sources; highly reproducible and selective signals without labeling or separation.

Conclusion: This platform enables ultra-sensitive, anti-interference, non-invasive EV detection, offering a new approach for rapid clinical disease diagnosis.

Abstract: Wearable sensing technology capable of point-of-care, continuous and non-invasive analysis of exosomes in biofluid such as tears and sweat is an essential part for future personalized medicine. Major detection and identification methods of cell secreted Extracellular Vesicles (EVs) often require labeling and are time-consuming, resulting in low efficiency in EV mechanism research and disease diagnosis. While the label-free Surface-enhanced Raman spectroscopy (SERS) has been combined with deep learning model for EV identification in blood, their application to non-invasive detection of EVs in tears and sweat are missing. Here, we filled this gap by developing an artificial intelligence (AI)-assisted Surface-enhanced Raman spectroscopy (SERS) method based on salt-induced nanoparticle aggregation for fast EV identification in tears and sweat with high accuracy. Significantly, our label-free detection and AI differentiation of EVs from 6 cell lines (HepG2, Hela, 143B, LO-2, BMSC, H8) achieved the identification of EVs in tear fluids from 7 different disease sources with accuracies >92%. Our results showed that this platform can not only distinguish EVs from multiple cell sources but also generate highly reproducible and selective EV signals in tear fluids without a need for chemical labeling or separation steps. Molecular dynamics simulations revealed that silver atoms (Ag) form electrostatic interactions with oxygen atoms of multiple amino acid residues in proteins, suggesting a high affinity. This strategy realizes ultra-sensitive and anti-interference detection of EVs, providing a new idea for the rapid diagnosis of clinical diseases.

</details>


### [129] [Towards terahertz excitons in hydrogenated graphene superlattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24027)
*Vasil A. Saroka,Olivia Pulci,Marco D'Alessandro*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper investigates using first principles methods to create two-dimensional graphene superlattices through selective hydrogenation, achieving strong excitonic absorption peaks in far-infrared and terahertz frequencies for monolithic on-chip integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Carbon nanotubes and graphene nanoribbons have promising terahertz properties but suffer from property degradation when integrated on-chip; alternative integration routes are needed.

Method: First principles theoretical calculations of electronic and optical properties of graphene superlattices with alternating quasi-metallic and dielectric regions created via selective hydrogenation.

Result: Chemically engineering graphene surfaces produces strong, well-isolated excitonic absorption peaks in far-infrared and potentially terahertz frequencies.

Conclusion: Selective hydrogenation of graphene is a viable route for monolithic on-chip integration of carbon nanostructures for terahertz applications without intrinsic property degradation.

Abstract: Carbon nanostructures, such as nanotubes and graphene nanoribbons, exhibit unique electronic and optical properties that make them very promising candidates for terahertz components. However, carbon nanotube and nanoribbon monolithic on-chip integration is challenging because it may results in significant change of their intrinsic properties after an embedment into a substrate. We investigate with first principles theoretical methods the successful routes of such integration and calculate electronic and optical properties of the integrated structures -- two-dimensional graphene superlattices, where quasi-metallic and dielectric regions alternate by selective hydrogenation of graphene. It is shown that chemical engineering of the graphene surface can lead to strong and well-isolated excitonic absorption peaks in the far-infrared and possibly even terahertz frequencies.

</details>


### [130] [Radiation conduction in polaritonic nanowires](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24030)
*Livia C. McCormack,Mathieu Francoeur*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work predicts radiative thermal conductivity in SiO2 nanowires using fluctuational electrodynamics, showing modest conductivities (e.g., 0.0264 W/m-K at 400 K for 66 nm diameter) dominated by surface phonon polaritons. Kinetic theory overestimates conductivity by nearly two orders of magnitude due to bulk phonon polariton contributions, and an effective mean free path correction improves agreement but fails spectrally.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phonon polaritons are promising for offsetting reduced thermal conductivity in nanostructures due to boundary scattering, but limited experimental data and the lack of comprehensive full-wave models hinder the interpretation of phonon polariton-mediated conduction.

Method: The radiative thermal conductivity in the diffusive regime along SiO2 nanowires near room temperature is predicted using the fluctuational electrodynamics-based discrete system Green's function method. Kinetic theory combined with nanowire dispersion relations derived from Maxwell's equations is also employed for comparison.

Result: At 400 K, for nanowire diameters of 66 nm and 132 nm, radiative conductivities of 0.0264 W/m-K and 0.0165 W/m-K are obtained, dominated by surface phonon polaritons. Kinetic theory predicts conductivities nearly two orders of magnitude larger, dominated by bulk phonon polaritons. An effective mean free path correction improves total conductivity agreement but fails for spectral distribution.

Conclusion: This work establishes a solid foundation for developing phonon polariton-based systems for thermal management in micro/nanoelectronic devices.

Abstract: Phonon polaritons have attracted increasing interest as a means to offset the reduction in phonon thermal conductivity in nanostructures caused by enhanced boundary scattering. However, the interpretation of the limited experimental data on phonon polariton-mediated conduction is hindered by the lack of comprehensive full-wave models for predicting thermal electromagnetic transport in solid-state systems. Here, the radiative thermal conductivity in the diffusive regime along SiO2 nanowires near room temperature is predicted using the fluctuational electrodynamics-based discrete system Green's function method. At 400 K, for nanowire diameters of 66 nm and 132 nm, modest radiative conductivities of 0.0264 W/m-K and 0.0165 W/m-K, respectively, are obtained, with contributions dominated by surface phonon polaritons. In contrast, the kinetic theory combined with nanowire dispersion relations derived from Maxwell's equations predicts conductivities that can be nearly two orders of magnitude larger than those obtained from fluctuational electrodynamics, and are largely dominated by bulk phonon polaritons propagating within the nanowire volume. Improved agreement between the kinetic theory and fluctuational electrodynamics for the total radiative conductivity can be achieved by introducing an effective mean free path that accounts for the reduced mean free path of bulk phonon polaritons outside the Reststrahlen spectral bands of SiO2. However, even with this correction, the kinetic theory fails to accurately capture the spectral distribution of radiative conductivity. This work establishes a solid foundation for the development of phonon polariton-based systems for thermal management in micro/nanoelectronic devices.

</details>


### [131] [Controlling spin-$\frac 12$ antiferromagnetic interaction strength in nanographene dimers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24078)
*Robiatul Adawia,Pawel Tecmer,Pawel Potasz*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Tip-induced dehydrogenation of selected carbon atoms in open-shell nanographene dimers enables precise tuning of the effective spin-exchange coupling J, computed via DIP-EOM-CCSD, over a wide range (few meV to tens of meV), offering a route to tailored spin models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a simple and controllable method for tuning spin-exchange couplings in nanographene dimers, enabling the design of tailored spin models with spatially patterned interactions.

Method: Uses tip-induced dehydrogenation to modify the hydrogenation pattern of specific carbon atoms in triangulene dimers; singlet-triplet gaps (i.e., exchange coupling J) are computed with the DIP-EOM-CCSD method.

Result: The position of dehydrogenated/hydrogen-passivated sites strongly modulates the singlet-triplet splitting, tuning J from a few meV to several tens of meV.

Conclusion: Tip-induced dehydrogenation provides a simple yet powerful route for designing spin models with alternating or spatially patterned spin-exchange couplings in nanographene systems.

Abstract: We demonstrate that the effective spin-exchange coupling $J$ in open-shell nanographene dimers can be precisely tuned via tip-induced dehydrogenation of selected carbon atoms. Using the double ionization potential equation-of-motion coupled-cluster singles and doubles (DIP-EOM-CCSD) method, we accurately compute the singlet-triplet gaps, which correspond directly to the exchange coupling $J$. We show that the position of the dehydrogenated (or hydrogen-passivated) site in triangulene dimers strongly modulates the singlet-triplet splitting, allowing $J$ to be tuned over a wide range - from a few meV to several tens of meV. This strategy provides a simple yet powerful route for designing tailored spin models with alternating or spatially patterned spin-exchange couplings.

</details>


### [132] [Electrostatically stabilized surface flat bands in rhombohedral graphite at zero displacement field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24080)
*Kryštof Kolář,Andrea F. Young,Cyprian Lewandowski*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Rhombohedral multilayer graphene can exhibit flat surface bands via self-consistent nonlinear electrostatics even without displacement fields, enabling interaction-driven phases in thick flakes without strong gating.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rhombohedral multilayer graphene hosts interaction-driven phases via surface flat bands at large displacement fields, but in thick flakes strong screening suppresses internal electric fields, making it unclear whether flat-band regimes are accessible within the same experimental paradigm.

Method: The authors combine analytic arguments with fully self-consistent calculations in a realistic model to map the low-field regime and connect to finite N~6-15 layered samples.

Result: Self-consistent nonlinear electrostatics provides a robust alternative mechanism: even without displacement fields, a nonuniform near-surface potential flattens surface-band dispersion and enhances density of states. Surface-band flatness is tuned by the proximal gate, with maximal flatness at hole doping when the band is empty.

Conclusion: The results motivate future experiments in large-N devices and establish a low-field regime for exploring electrostatically induced flat-band physics.

Abstract: Rhombohedral (ABC-stacked) multilayer graphene hosts interaction-driven phases enabled by surface flat bands at large displacement fields. In thick flakes, however, strong screening suppresses internal electric fields, raising the question of whether a flat-band regime is accessible within the same experimental paradigm. Here, we show that self-consistent, nonlinear electrostatics provides a robust alternative mechanism: even in the absence of a displacement field, a nonuniform near-surface potential flattens the surface-band dispersion and enhances the density of states. In the strong-coupling limit, electrostatics drives the system toward uniform half-filling at each momentum, yielding an asymptotically flat surface band without any gating. At realistic interaction strengths, surface-band flatness is tuned by the proximal gate, with maximal flatness achieved at hole doping when the band is empty. Combining analytic arguments with fully self-consistent calculations in a realistic model, we map the resulting low-field regime and connect to finite $N\!\sim\! 6-15$ layered samples, providing a framework for analyzing the symmetry-broken phases observed in these systems. Our results motivate future experiments in large-$N$ devices and establish a low-field regime for exploring electrostatically induced flat-band physics.

</details>


### [133] [Quantized thermal current vortices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24158)
*Ferenc Márkus,Katalin Gambár*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper proposes a theoretical framework for the quantized thermal Hall effect based on Sommerfeld's flux quantization, suggests the existence of dissipationless thermal current vortices, and explores their role in stabilizing topological structures like skyrmion lattices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a theoretical foundation for the quantum analogue of the thermal Hall effect (quantized thermal Hall effect) and explore its connection to topological spin structures such as skyrmions.

Method: The authors develop a theoretical framework using Sommerfeld's flux quantization to describe the quantized thermal Hall effect, and analytically propose the existence of dissipationless thermal current vortices.

Result: The framework successfully describes a quantized version of the thermal Hall effect, and predicts dissipationless thermal current vortices that may interact with skyrmion lattices.

Conclusion: Dissipationless thermal current vortices could play a key role in the stability and dynamics of topological magnetic textures such as skyrmion lattices, offering new insight into heat transport and magnetic structure interplay.

Abstract: The thermal Hall effect has emerged as a fundamental tool for probing exotic quasiparticles and topological order, particularly in magnetic insulators where electronic conduction is suppressed. Much like skyrmions, which are characterized by their topologically protected spin configurations, the thermal Hall effect is deeply rooted in the geometric properties of the underlying physical space. Although the effect is a well-established experimental phenomenon, current research points toward the existence of its quantum analogue: the quantized thermal Hall effect. In this paper, we provide a theoretical framework for this quantum version based on Sommerfeld's flux quantization. Furthermore, we demonstrate the potential existence of dissipationless thermal current vortices. We suggest that these vortices may play a crucial role in the stability and dynamics of other topological structures, such as skyrmion lattices, offering a new perspective on the interplay between heat transport and magnetic textures.

</details>


### [134] [Quantum Printing: Laguerre-Gaussian Beam Induced Topological Magnetic Textures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24720)
*Yuefei Liu,Tien-Tien Yeh,Alexander V. Balatsky*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Laguerre-Gaussian pulses imprint non-collinear magnetic textures via optical magnetic field spatial structure, enabling direct spatial selectivity without material anisotropy, and micromagnetic simulations show topological charge density control through optical topological properties and polarization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a method for creating magnetic textures using structured light that does not rely on material anisotropic interactions, interfacial anisotropy, bulk chirality, current-driven torques, or thermal quenching.

Method: Using Laguerre-Gaussian pulses to imprint magnetic textures via the spatial structure of the optical magnetic field, demonstrated through micromagnetic simulations.

Result: Demonstrated the potential to create topological charge density emerging during the pulse and revealed control through optical topological properties and polarization.

Conclusion: Structured-light quantum printing is a viable approach for magnonics, motivating studies toward reconfigurable topological textures enabled by ultrafast THz optics and non-thermal control.

Abstract: Structured light has become a practical tool for controlling matter by applying tailored, space- and time-dependent electromagnetic fields. We show that Laguerre-Gaussian pulses imprint non-collinear magnetic textures via the spatial structure of optical magnetic field. Our route offers a direct spatial selectivity determined by the optical features without relying on material anisotropic interactions. The proposed printing approach does not require interfacial anisotropy or bulk chirality, current-driven torques, or thermal quenching. We use micromagnetic simulations to demonstrate the potential to create topological charge density emerging during the pulse and reveal control through the optical topological properties and polarization. These results suggest structured-light quantum printing as a viable approach for magnonics and motivate studies toward reconfigurable topological textures enabled by ultrafast THz optics and non-thermal control.

</details>


### [135] [High-fidelity EDSR in Si/SiGe Wiggle Wells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24790)
*Hudaiba Soomro,Minyoung Kim,Avani Vivrekar,M. A. Eriksson,Benjamin D. Woods,Mark Friesen*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how random-alloy disorder affects electric dipole spin resonance (EDSR) in Si/SiGe Wiggle Wells, showing that while it introduces spatial randomization of the Rabi frequency and a new valley-dipole-driven mechanism, high-fidelity micromagnet-free spin qubit operations are still achievable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To fully account for random-alloy disorder's impact on EDSR in Si/SiGe Wiggle Wells, as previous analyses neglected its role in valley phase randomization, despite it being crucial for valley physics in this materials system.

Method: The authors analyze two main effects of alloy disorder on EDSR: (1) the Rabi frequency's dependence on the spatially random valley phase, and (2) a new Rabi driving mechanism from valley dipoles arising from disorder-induced hybridization of ground and excited valley states. They identify sweet spots insensitive to electric-field fluctuations and test fidelity under realistic charge noise.

Result: Alloy disorder introduces spatial randomness in the Rabi frequency via valley phase dependence, but fast EDSR remains achievable at most locations. A new disorder-enabled valley dipole mechanism dominates in low valley splitting regions. Despite gradients in Rabi frequency causing dephasing, high-fidelity Rabi oscillations are possible with realistic charge noise by operating at identified sweet spots.

Conclusion: The Wiggle Well is a promising platform for high-quality, micromagnet-free spin qubit gate operations, as alloy disorder can actually strengthen EDSR rather than prevent it, provided sweet spots are used to mitigate dephasing.

Abstract: Si/SiGe quantum wells that incorporate Ge concentration oscillations, known as long-period Wiggle Wells, have previously been shown to enhance the Dresselhaus spin-orbit coupling (SOC) of conduction-band electrons. Such intrinsic SOC is desirable when performing spin-qubit gate operations based on electric dipole spin resonance (EDSR) because it eliminates the need for external micromagnets. However, random-alloy disorder plays a key role in the valley physics of this materials system by spatially randomizing the valley phase $φ_{s,s}$, and has not been fully accounted for in recent EDSR analyses. Here, we show that alloy disorder affects EDSR in two main ways. First, the Rabi frequency $Ω$ acquires a dependence on the valley phase, given by $\cosφ_{s,s}$, which causes spatial randomization of $Ω$. Despite this variability, we show that fast EDSR can be achieved at most locations across a given sample. Second, a new Rabi driving mechanism emerges, enabled by valley dipoles that result from disorder and the hybridization of ground and excited valley states in response to an EDSR driving field. This mechanism is dominant in regions of low valley splitting. Alloy disorder can therefore strengthen EDSR, but it can also cause gradients in $Ω$ that lead to dephasing in the rotating frame. We explore this problem by first identifying "sweet spots," where EDSR is relatively insensitive to electric-field fluctuations. We then show that high-fidelity Rabi oscillations can be achieved in the presence of realistic charge noise. Our results demonstrate that the Wiggle Well is a promising platform for high-quality, micromagnet-free gate operations.

</details>


### [136] [Tuning quantum tunneling in WSe$_2$ via strain engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24851)
*Rachid El Aitouni,Hasna Chnafa,Clarence Cortes,David Laroze,Ahmed Jellal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A theoretical study shows that combining strain engineering with electrostatic potentials in monolayer WSe₂ enables highly controllable modulation of spin- and valley-resolved quantum transport, including oscillatory transmission, conductance, and strong polarization effects, offering a platform for spintronic, valleytronic, and optoelectronic devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To theoretically demonstrate that strain and electrostatic engineering can serve as an efficient and versatile platform for manipulating spin and valley degrees of freedom in monolayer WSe₂, enabling the design of next-generation spintronic, valleytronic, and optoelectronic devices.

Method: A low-energy Dirac framework incorporating strain effects is used to model quantum transport in monolayer WSe₂ with an electrostatic scalar potential barrier. The system is partitioned into three regions, wave functions are derived analytically, and continuity conditions at interfaces yield exact transmission/reflection amplitudes. Transmission probability is computed from current densities, and conductance via the Landauer-Büttiker formalism.

Result: Strain acts as a powerful tuning parameter, reshaping electronic dispersion and strongly modifying transport. Transmission and conductance show pronounced oscillatory behavior due to quantum interference and resonant tunneling. Spin and valley polarizations exhibit substantial and highly controllable variations as functions of strain, barrier height, and incident energy.

Conclusion: Strain and electrostatic engineering provide an efficient and versatile platform for manipulating spin-valley degrees of freedom in WSe₂, with promising opportunities for next-generation spintronic, valleytronic, and optoelectronic devices based on 2D transition-metal dichalcogenides.

Abstract: We present a comprehensive theoretical study of strain-engineered quantum transport in monolayer tungsten diselenide (WSe$_2$) in the presence of an electrostatic scalar potential. By incorporating strain effects within a low-energy Dirac framework, we analyze their impact on spin- and valley-resolved transmission, conductance, and polarization. The applied potential barrier partitions the system into three distinct regions, allowing for an analytical derivation of the wave functions in each domain. Enforcing continuity conditions at the interfaces yields exact expressions for the transmission and reflection amplitudes. The transmission probability is evaluated from the corresponding current densities, while the conductance is obtained using the Landauer-Büttiker formalism, enabling a quantitative determination of spin and valley polarizations. Our numerical analysis reveals that strain acts as a powerful tuning parameter that reshapes the electronic dispersion and strongly modifies transport characteristics. In particular, the transmission and conductance exhibit pronounced oscillatory behavior driven by quantum interference and resonant tunneling mechanisms. More importantly, both spin and valley polarizations display substantial and highly controllable variations as functions of strain, barrier height, and incident energy. These results demonstrate that strain and electrostatic engineering provide an efficient and versatile platform for manipulating spin-valley degrees of freedom in WSe$_2$. The ability to tailor polarization and interference effects suggests promising opportunities for the design of next-generation spintronic, valleytronic, and optoelectronic devices based on two-dimensional transition-metal dichalcogenides.

</details>


### [137] [Cascaded Four-Wave Mixing on Quantum Paraelectrics for On-chip Cryogenic Microcombs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25112)
*Harikrishnan Sundaresan,Prasad Muragesh,Madhu Thalakulam*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper demonstrates an on-chip cryogenic microwave frequency-comb using a superconducting resonator fabricated on Strontium titanate (STO), leveraging its quantum paraelectric phase Kerr nonlinearity to enable comb generation via four-wave mixing for low-power, cryogenic nonlinear microwave photonics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a low-power, cryogenically compatible platform for nonlinear microwave photonics that enables on-chip frequency-comb generation, with potential applications in scalable quantum control and on-chip frequency synthesis.

Method: Planar superconducting resonator fabricated on Strontium titanate (STO); utilizes the material's Kerr-type nonlinearity from the quantum paraelectric phase to achieve resonance shifts, Duffing-like bifurcations, and cascaded four-wave mixing for comb generation.

Result: Successful demonstration of cryogenic microwave frequency-comb generation on a planar STO-based superconducting resonator via cascaded four-wave mixing, enabled by the quantum paraelectric phase Kerr nonlinearity.

Conclusion: STO resonators are established as low-power, cryogenically compatible platforms for nonlinear microwave photonics, with promise for scalable quantum control and on-chip frequency synthesis.

Abstract: Here, we demonstrate an on-chip cryogenic microwave frequency-comb on a planar superconducting resonator fabricated on Strontium titanate(STO), a quantum paraelectric material. The material's Kerr-type nonlinearity arising from the quantum paraelectric phase, an underexplored state, enables pronounced resonance shifts, Duffing-like bifurcations and comb generation via cascaded foure-wave mixing. Our results establish STO resonators as a low power, cryogenically compatible platforms for nonlinear microwave photonics, with applications in scalable quantum control and on-chip frequency synthesis.

</details>


### [138] [Intrinsic Topological Control of the Orbital Hall Effect in Buckled Dirac Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25121)
*Madiha Zia,Muzamil Shah,Kashif Sabeeh,Gao Xianlong,Reza Asgari*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper investigates orbital Hall conductivity in buckled 2D Dirac materials under antiferromagnetic fields, electric fields, and spin-orbit coupling, showing enhanced response near band-inversion points and robust phase-dependent features across quantum spin Hall, valley Hall, and anomalous Hall regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and characterize the orbital Hall response in buckled 2D Dirac materials as a sensitive probe of band topology and to explore tunable orbital currents for orbitronic applications.

Method: Low-energy massive Dirac model combined with Berry-curvature-based linear response theory, systematically varying antiferromagnetic exchange field, perpendicular electric field, and intrinsic spin-orbit coupling.

Result: Orbital Hall conductivity is significantly boosted near band-inversion points; distinct signatures emerge across quantum spin Hall, valley Hall, and anomalous Hall phases; thermal broadening suppresses magnitude but preserves phase-dependent features; response evolution is governed by Berry curvature redistribution between spin and valley sectors.

Conclusion: Orbital Hall conductivity serves as a sensitive probe of band topology in Dirac systems, and buckled 2D materials provide a versatile platform for engineering tunable orbital currents for orbitronic applications.

Abstract: We study the orbital Hall response in buckled two-dimensional Dirac materials using a unified framework that includes an antiferromagnetic exchange field, a perpendicular electric field, and intrinsic spin-orbit coupling. We show that the orbital Hall conductivity is considerably boosted around band-inversion points and shows different signatures across multiple electronic phases using a low-energy massive Dirac model in conjunction with Berry-curvature-based linear response theory. We find a series of quantum spin Hall, valley Hall, and anomalous Hall regimes by methodically adjusting external fields, and demonstrate how the evolution of the orbital response is controlled by the redistribution of Berry curvature between spin and valley sectors. We examine the impacts of finite temperature in more detail and find that although the response s size is suppressed by thermal broadening, the distinctive phase-dependent features remain robust. Our findings demonstrate that orbital Hall conductivity offers a sensitive band topology probe in Dirac systems and emphasize buckled two-dimensional materials as a flexible platform for engineering tunable orbital currents for orbitronic applications.

</details>


### [139] [Non-Hermitian Twisting Theory under the open boundary condition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25386)
*Chen-Hao Zhao,Jia-Rui Li,Yuping Tian,Wei-Jiang Gong*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper develops a site-resolved local scaling transformation (LST) theory for the non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE), introducing the Zahlen-Brillouin Zone (ZBZ) to extend band theory to nonperiodic systems, and establishing the metric and state correspondence (MSC) as a universal principle for real-space localization in non-Hermitian physics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The generalized Brillouin zone (GBZ) description of the non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE) is restricted to periodic systems, limiting its applicability to nonperiodic and disordered lattices.

Method: The authors develop a site-resolved theory via a local scaling transformation (LST), introducing a local twisting parameter T_n to quantify the nontriviality of the metric operator ξ. They define the Zahlen-Brillouin Zone (ZBZ) by exploiting T_n's translational independence, and establish the metric and state correspondence (MSC) to unify ξ with GBZ Riemannian geometry. A global skin index Γ is introduced for phase transitions.

Result: The theory elucidates the origin of NHSE and uncovers a generalized multiple-channel skin effect (MCSE). It extends non-Hermitian band theory to nonperiodic and disordered lattices, and provides a universal paradigm applicable to both crystalline and amorphous media.

Conclusion: The work establishes a universal framework for non-Hermitian physics beyond periodic systems, unifying real-space localization principles across ordered and disordered media.

Abstract: The non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE) is a hallmark of non-Hermitian system, yet its generalized Brillouin zone (GBZ) description is restricted to periodic systems. We develop a site-resolved theory via a local scaling transformation (LST), introducing local twisting $T_n$ to quantify metric operator $ξ$ nontriviality. This elucidates the NHSE's origin and uncovers the generalized multiple-channel skin effect (MCSE). Exploiting $T_n$'s translational independence, we define the Zahlen-Brillouin Zone (ZBZ), extending non-Hermitian band theory to nonperiodic and disordered lattices. Furthermore, we unify the $ξ$ with GBZ Riemannian geometry, establishing the metric and state correspondence (MSC) as the principle for real-space localization. With a global skin index $\mathbfΓ$ for phase transitions, our results provide a universal paradigm for non-Hermitian physics in both crystalline and amorphous media.

</details>


### [140] [Microfluidic Actuation by Einstein-de Haas Spin Torque](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25387)
*Xin Hu,Mamoru Matsuo*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method for microfluidic actuation of sealed liquid metal using spin-current-induced Einstein-de Haas torque, enabling viscous flow without traditional driving mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve microfluidic actuation of liquid metal without requiring pressure gradients, moving mechanical parts, magnetic fields, Lorentz forces, or electrical current through the liquid, potentially enabling new microfluidic devices.

Method: Spin angular momentum is injected from Pt contacts into the liquid metal, generating an Einstein-de Haas torque. This torque is converted into viscous flow through micropolar angular-momentum balance theory, without pressure drive, moving walls, magnetic fields, Lorentz forces, or charge flow through the liquid.

Result: The dc velocity follows universal spin-diffusion scaling behavior. The finite-frequency spin-mechanical admittance reveals viscous momentum diffusion, spin transport, microrotation relaxation, and interface transparency of the liquid-metal channel.

Conclusion: Spin-current-driven microfluidic actuation is a viable approach for moving sealed liquid metals, with measurable dynamics that can be characterized through spin-mechanical admittance spectroscopy.

Abstract: We propose spin-current microfluidic actuation of a sealed liquid metal. Spin angular momentum injected from Pt contacts enters the liquid as an Einstein-de Haas torque and is converted through micropolar angular-momentum balance into viscous flow without pressure drive, moving walls, magnetic fields, Lorentz forces, or charge flow through the liquid. The dc velocity obeys universal spin-diffusion scaling, and the finite-frequency spin-mechanical admittance resolves viscous momentum diffusion, spin transport, microrotation relaxation, and interface transparency of the liquid-metal channel.

</details>


### [141] [Topological fragility and bilinear magnetoelectric resistance in gapless edge states](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25774)
*Cosimo Gorini,Matthieu Bard,Sophie Gueron,Hélène Bouchiat,Giovanni Vignale*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper reveals that 1D spin-momentum locked edge/hinge states in topological insulators exhibit a "topological fragility" - they show large bilinear magnetoelectric resistance due to spin-momentum locking combined with random spin-orbit interaction (Elliott-Yafet mechanism) under a magnetic field, explaining recent observations in Bismuth without requiring gap opening or many-body effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why 1D topologically protected edge and hinge states, theoretically immune to backscattering, exhibit unexpectedly large magnetoelectric resistance in experiments.

Method: Theoretical analysis identifying two key ingredients: (i) spin-momentum locking maximizing time-reversal symmetry breaking in the nonlinear regime, and (ii) random spin-orbit interaction (Elliott-Yafet mechanism) that together create a robust backscattering channel under an external magnetic field.

Result: The bilinear magnetoelectric resistance in 1D topological states is significantly larger than in 2D systems, offering a quantitative explanation for recent observations in Bismuth hinge states without requiring gap opening or many-body effects.

Conclusion: Spin-momentum locked 1D states are not perfectly conducting under realistic conditions; random spin-orbit interaction combined with a magnetic field generates backscattering, revealing an inherent topological fragility.

Abstract: In time-reversal symmetric systems such as topological and higher-order topological insulators, 1D spin-momentum locked edge and hinge states are theoretically ``perfectly conducting'', being immune to backscattering by non-magnetic disorder. Here, we reveal a fundamental ``topological fragility'': these states exhibit a bilinear magnetoelectric resistance significantly larger than in 2D systems. This effect requires two ingredients: (i) spin-momentum locking, which maximizes time-reversal symmetry breaking in the non-linear regime, and (ii) random spin-orbit interaction -- the same mechanism behind Elliott - Yafet spin relaxation in heavy elements. Together, these generate a robust backscattering channel when a modest external magnetic field is applied. Our theory requires no gap opening or complex many-body effects, offering a simple and general mechanism that quantitatively explains recent observations in Bismuth hinge states.

</details>


### [142] [Hybridization of Ferromagnetic and Cyclotron Resonances in a Two-Dimensional Electron System on a Ferromagnetic Film](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25839)
*A. A. Zabolotnykh,I. V. Zagorodnev,A. A. Matveev,D. A. Rodionov,O. Yu. Arkhipova,D. V. Kalyabin,A. R. Safin,S. A. Nikitov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper studies the microwave response of a 2D electron system on a dielectric ferromagnetic film on a metal gate in a perpendicular magnetic field, finding that ferromagnetic resonance and cyclotron resonance interact via anticrossing, modifying both resonance frequencies and linewidths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To theoretically investigate the interaction between ferromagnetic resonance in a dielectric film and cyclotron resonance in a nearby 2D electron system, and their combined microwave response under a static magnetic field.

Method: Theoretical analysis of a layered structure: a 2D electron system on a dielectric ferromagnetic film atop a conducting metal gate, all placed in a perpendicular static magnetic field. Microwave response is calculated to study resonance behavior.

Result: Ferromagnetic resonance and cyclotron resonance interact in the magnetic field, exhibiting anticrossing (repulsion). The anticrossing region shows modified resonance frequencies compared to isolated systems and significant changes in resonance linewidths.

Conclusion: The coupled system displays strong hybridization between ferromagnetic and cyclotron resonances, leading to frequency repulsion and substantial linewidth modification, which cannot be understood from either subsystem alone.

Abstract: The microwave response of a two-dimensional (2D) electron system located on a dielectric ferromagnetic film, which in turn lies on a conducting metal (gate), has been theoretically studied. The entire system has been placed in the perpendicular static magnetic field. It has been found that the ferromagnetic resonance of the film and the cyclotron resonance of the electrons of the 2D system interact in the magnetic field, leading to their repulsion ('anticrossing'). It has been revealed that the anticrossing region is characterized not only by the modification of resonance frequencies compared to the cyclotron resonance in the 2D system without the ferromagnetic substrate and the ferromagnetic resonance in the film without the 2D system, but also by a strong change in the resonance linewidths.

</details>


### [143] [Observation of the Optical Phonons in α-MnTe films](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26075)
*Himanshu Sheokand,Arun K Kumay,Mazharul Islam Mondal,Milo Sprague,Ravinder Sharma,Jayan Thomas,Dariusz Kaczorowski,Andrzej Ptok,Madhab Neupane*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper reports the MBE growth of high-quality alpha-MnTe thin films on GaAs(111)B substrates and characterizes their structural and vibrational properties, demonstrating that all symmetry-allowed Raman-active phonon modes of the NiAs-type lattice can be resolved, establishing epitaxial alpha-MnTe as a platform for altermagnetism research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnetic materials are promising for studying spin-split electronic structures, but controlled epitaxial growth on technologically relevant substrates remains challenging. MnTe is a key candidate due to its layered structure and high Néel temperature, motivating the need for high-quality thin film growth.

Method: Molecular beam epitaxy (MBE) was used to grow alpha-MnTe thin films on GaAs(111)B substrates. The films were characterized by Raman spectroscopy combined with first-principles phonon calculations to identify vibrational modes.

Result: Raman spectroscopy revealed multiple vibrational features, including modes near 121 and 140 cm⁻¹, which were identified as Raman-active phonons of the hexagonal NiAs-type lattice. All symmetry-allowed Raman-active phonon modes were experimentally resolved.

Conclusion: High crystalline quality MBE-grown alpha-MnTe enables complete experimental resolution of all symmetry-allowed Raman-active phonon modes, establishing epitaxial alpha-MnTe as a robust thin film platform for investigating altermagnetism and its lattice-coupled excitations.

Abstract: The altermagnetic materials have emerged as model systems for studying spin split electronic structures, yet controlled epitaxial growth on technologically relevant substrates remains challenging. Among the known candidates, MnTe stands out as a prominent altermagnetic material owing to its layered structure and high Neel temperature. Here, we report the molecular beam epitaxy (MBE) growth of high quality alpha MnTe thin films on GaAs(111)B substrates and provide a comprehensive analysis of the growth evolution and structural properties. Raman spectroscopy reveals multiple vibrational features of alpha MnTe including modes near 121, and 140 1/cm. Combined with first principles phonon calculations, these features are identified as the Raman-active phonons of the hexagonal NiAs type lattice. Our results show that the high crystalline quality of MBE grown alpha MnTe enables the complete experimental resolution of all symmetry allowed Raman active phonon modes, highlighting epitaxial alpha MnTe as a robust thin film platform for investigating altermagnetism and its lattice coupled excitations.

</details>


<div id='physics.atom-ph'></div>

# physics.atom-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [144] [Minimally Destructive Fast Imaging of Single Atoms in an Optical Tweezer Array with Coherent Excitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24175)
*Rei Yokoyama,Takumi Kashimoto,Kosuke Shibata,Yuki Kawamura,Toshi Kusano,Chih-Han Yeh,Reiji Asano,Yuma Nakamura,Tetsushi Takano,Yosuke Takasu,Yoshiro Takahashi*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes a minimally destructive single-atom imaging technique using deterministic coherent excitation with alternating pi-pulses, achieving 99.89% fidelity and 98.80% survival probability in 17.6 μs with ytterbium-174 atoms, reducing heating by about half compared to incoherent methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the fundamental heating effect associated with stochastic absorption in conventional single-atom imaging, which limits scalability and trap depth requirements in quantum gas microscopes, tweezer clocks, and quantum computers.

Method: Deterministic coherent excitation of atoms using alternately applied pi-pulses from counter-propagating directions, demonstrated with ytterbium-174 atoms trapped in an optical tweezer array.

Result: Discrimination fidelity of 99.89(5)%, survival probability of 98.80(44)% in 17.6 μs, and approximately half the heating rate of incoherent excitation schemes.

Conclusion: This fast, minimally destructive imaging scheme relaxes trap depth requirements, enabling scalable atom imaging across various quantum science platforms.

Abstract: Ultracold neutral atoms in an optical lattice and an optical tweezer array offer highly-controllable quantum many-body systems, utilized for various quantum science and technology such as quantum computing, quantum metrology, and quantum simulation. By combining high-fidelity imaging of individual atoms, one can further enhance the capability of such experimental platforms as quantum gas microscopes, tweezer clocks, and tweezer-array-based quantum computers. In this work, we propose a minimally destructive single-atom imaging by deterministic coherent excitation of atoms with alternately applied pi-pulses from counter-propagating directions, mitigating the fundamental heating effect associated with the stochastic absorption process. Using ytterbium-174 atoms trapped in an optical tweezer array, we experimentally demonstrate fast and low-loss single-atom imaging with a discrimination fidelity of 99.89(5) % and a survival probability of 98.80(44) % in 17.6 microseconds. Importantly, our scheme exhibits the lower heating rate, about half of that of the former scheme utilizing the incoherent excitation. This fast and minimally destructive imaging scheme is beneficial for relaxing the requirement on the trap depth, thereby enabling scalable atom imaging across a wide range of quantum science platforms.

</details>


### [145] [The Pseudospectral Method for the Dirac Equation with Confining Potential](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25390)
*Dengshan Liu,Huihui Xie,Pengxiang Du,Jian Li,Tomoya Naito*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper identifies convergence issues in the GPS method for solving the Dirac equation under confined potentials, and proposes the MKB-GPS method incorporating kinetically-balanced conditions, which achieves stable convergence and smooth wave functions even at small confinement radii.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard generalized pseudospectral (GPS) method exhibits deteriorating convergence of energy eigenvalues and highly oscillatory wave functions when solving the Dirac equation for confined potentials with decreasing confinement radius.

Method: The authors adopt the kinetically balanced generalized pseudospectral (MKB-GPS) method, which incorporates the kinetically-balanced condition into the GPS framework to address issues stemming from the first-order differentiation formulation.

Result: The MKB-GPS method yields converged energy eigenvalues and generates smooth, continuous wave functions even for small confinement radii, whereas the standard GPS method fails under these conditions.

Conclusion: This is the first application of the MKB-GPS method to confined potentials; it is validated as effective for small confinement radii, resolving the instability of the standard GPS approach.

Abstract: We observe that solving the Dirac equation for confined potentials using the generalized pseudospectral (GPS) method leads to deteriorating convergence of energy eigenvalues and highly oscillatory in wave functions as the confinement radius decreases. It is found that this issue stems from the first-order differentiation formulation employed in GPS method. Motivated by this insight, we adopt the kinetically balanced generalized pseudospectral method, which incorporates the kinetically-balanced condition into the GPS method. Numerical results demonstrate that the mono-kinetically-balanced generalized pseu dospectral (MKB-GPS) method yields converged energy eigenvalues and generates smooth, continuous wave functions. This is the first application of the MKB-GPS method to confined potentials, and its effectiveness is validated for small confinement radii.

</details>


### [146] [FPGA-based disturbance-observer servo for broadband noise suppression in laser frequency stabilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25471)
*Meung Ho Seo,Jae Hoon Lee,Young-Ho Park,Hyun-Gue Hong,Seji Kang,Myoung-Sun Heo,Sang-Bum Lee,Taeg Yong Kwon,Sangwon Seo,Sang Eon Park*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: An FPGA-based digital disturbance observer (DOB) augmented with a PID controller achieves broadband frequency-noise suppression in laser stabilization, reducing integrated rms noise below 40 kHz by 16.9 dB and improving short-term fractional frequency instability from 7.9×10⁻¹² to 4.6×10⁻¹² at 1 ms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve laser frequency stabilization beyond conventional PID control by adding a simple, parameter-efficient disturbance observer that avoids complex frequency-domain plant identification.

Method: A digital DOB with a first-order exponential moving average Q-filter is implemented on an FPGA and integrated with a PID controller. The system is tested using modulation transfer spectroscopy on the ⁸⁷Rb D2 line at 780.24 nm, measuring beat-note frequency noise between two independently stabilized lasers.

Result: Integrated rms frequency noise below 40 kHz decreased by 16.9 dB (from ~140 kHz to ~20 kHz). Short-term instability improved from σ_y(1 ms) = 7.9×10⁻¹² to 4.6×10⁻¹². Long-term stability (τ > 1 s) remained within statistical uncertainty.

Conclusion: The DOB-augmented architecture provides a simple and effective route to enhanced noise rejection in FPGA-based servo systems for atomic physics experiments.

Abstract: We have demonstrated broadband frequency-noise suppression in a laser stabilization system by augmenting a conventional proportional-integral-derivative (PID) controller with a digital disturbance observer (DOB) implemented on a field-programmable gate array (FPGA). The DOB employs a first-order exponential moving average filter as its Q-filter, replacing multi-parameter frequency-domain plant identification with a single one-dimensional gain sweep. Using modulation transfer spectroscopy on the 87Rb D2 line at 780.24 nm, we have measured the frequency-noise power spectral density and the Allan deviation of the beat note between two independently stabilized lasers. The integrated rms frequency noise below 40 kHz decreased by 16.9 dB compared with PID alone, corresponding to a reduction from approximately 140 kHz to 20 kHz. The short-term fractional frequency instability improved from sigma_y(1 ms) = $7.9 \times 10^{-12}$ to $4.6 \times 10^{-12}$, while the long-term stability at tau > 1 s remained within statistical uncertainty. This DOB-augmented architecture offers a simple and effective route to enhanced noise rejection in FPGA-based servo systems for atomic physics experiments.

</details>


### [147] [From Vintage Mythology to Topological Physics: Unveiling a Universal Structural Attractor in Alcoholic Beverage Aging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25472)
*Xinyue Jiang,Heng Yang,Zhiyin Jiu,Youxi Luo,Lin Chen,Yuqun Xie*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Persistent homology analysis of Baijiu aged 1-10 years reveals a three-stage topological maturation pathway (scaffold consolidation, channel stabilization, cavity reorganization) converging toward a mature structural attractor, reframing beverage aging as navigation through structural state space rather than chronological time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether chronological vintage corresponds to an intrinsic structural state in alcoholic beverages, moving beyond empirical labels by using topological data analysis to understand aging.

Method: Applied persistent homology to map topological evolution of self-assembled molecular aggregates in strong-aroma Baijiu across 1-10 years of aging, analyzing three persistence dimensions (B0, B1, B2).

Result: Discovered a three-stage maturation pathway: rapid scaffold consolidation (B0), population-level channel stabilization (B1), and non-monotonic cavity reorganization (B2), converging toward a mature topological state rather than tracking chronological age linearly.

Conclusion: Proposes a universal topological attractor model where optimal aging is defined by a system's position in persistence space relative to a mature structural domain, providing a physical basis for quality evaluation and accelerated maturation.

Abstract: Alcoholic beverage properties are increasingly understood through ethanol-water structural states rather than empirical labels such as alcohol content and vintage. Yet whether chronological vintage similarly reflects an intrinsic structural state remains unclear. Here, we apply persistent homology to map the topological evolution of self-assembled molecular aggregates in strong-aroma Baijiu aged 1-10 years. The resulting fingerprints reveal a three-stage maturation pathway: rapid scaffold consolidation (B0), population-level channel stabilization (B1), and non-monotonic cavity reorganization (B2). These coupled trajectories converge toward a mature topological state rather than passively tracking chronological age. We therefore propose a universal topological attractor, in which optimal aging is defined by a system's position in persistence space relative to a mature structural domain. This framework reframes beverage aging as navigation through structural state space, providing a physical basis for quality evaluation and accelerated maturation.

</details>


### [148] [Self-calibrated multiparameter measurement of three-dimensional microwave fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26098)
*Yupeng Wang,Xinghan Wang,Aishik Panja,Md. Ehsanuzzaman,Chuan-Hsun Li,Qi-Yu Liang*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Demonstrates self-calibrated, full 3D vector microwave field reconstruction using multi-level, Zeeman-resolved Rydberg EIT spectroscopy in a laser-cooled atomic ensemble, extracting polarization amplitudes and relative phases from a single spectrum without external reference fields.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full local 3D microwave field characterization remains difficult; existing methods lack self-calibrated vector field reconstruction, which is valuable for atom-based sensing and in-situ field characterization in complex EM environments.

Method: Implement multi-level, Zeeman-resolved Rydberg electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT) spectroscopy in a laser-cooled atomic ensemble; extract three polarization amplitudes from a single spectrum; exploit closed interferometric loops within the atoms' internal Hilbert space to extract relative phases.

Result: The method is self-calibrated, requires no external reference MW fields, and MW parameters are largely separable from one another and from other experimental parameters.

Conclusion: The approach is broadly applicable to dedicated sensing platforms, quantum optics, and quantum information experiments.

Abstract: Rydberg atoms are promising for microwave (MW) sensing and control, but full local MW characterization remains difficult. Existing methods generally do not provide self-calibrated reconstruction of the three-dimensional vector field, which is valuable for both atom-based sensing and in-situ field characterization in complex electromagnetic environments. We propose and implement multi-level, Zeeman-resolved Rydberg electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT) spectroscopy in a laser-cooled atomic ensemble. We extract the three polarization amplitudes from a single spectrum and show that the MW polarization components give rise to closed interferometric loops within the atoms' internal Hilbert space, enabling extraction of their relative phases. Moreover, it is self-calibrated and requires no external reference MW fields, with MW parameters largely separable from one another and from other experimental parameters. These features make it broadly applicable to dedicated sensing platforms as well as quantum optics and quantum information experiments.

</details>
